Amazon Expands Drone Deliveries, and Gets Noise Complaints. 'Like a Swarm of Bees'
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
19:00:00 UTC
04:00:00 KST

Amazon Expands Drone Deliveries, and Gets Noise Complaints. 'Like a Swarm of Bees' (chron.com) 3
			
		 	
				"In August, Amazon announced it was expanding its airborne shipping to suburban areas in nearly 500 U.S. cities," reports the Chicago Sun-Times, "including the Chicago, Atlanta, Cleveland and Boise, Idaho, metro areas."  And on Tuesday Amazon cut the ribbon on its new drone delivery center Tuesday 24 miles south of Chicago.
But Amazon's drones are already servicing parts of Texas, notes the Houston Chronicle.  And  north of Houston, Theresa Gibbons complains "It was kind of cool and fascinating at first, but now it is driving me crazy."
Her home in the Bridgestone neighborhood is nestled right in the flight path of Amazon's drone service. Gibson said she hears roughly a dozen drones flying over her home each day.   Noise pollution has become a frequent complaint in Texas neighborhoods where the service operates. Residents have compared the sound to everything from a massive beehive to a full-on air raid. "It does not sound like a [single] bumble bee. It sounds like a large swarm of bees at a much higher volume than a normal swarm of bees. And it's all day long," said Gibson.
Amazon's drone delivery service is currently operating in four Texas cities, including Houston, Waco, San Antonio and Richardson. The company announced major expansion plans in August, but growing complaints in pilot neighborhoods could foreshadow complications later down the line, with regions such as Harris County already taking legal action in response... So far, a group of fifteen states, plus Harris County,  [Texas] have formed a coalition that has filed a petition in the US Court of Appeals challenging the Federal Aviation Administration's environmental review, citing that it failed to adequately address the effects of expanding drone deliveries.    
And meanwhile near Boise, Idaho, local news station KTVB reports....
Less than two weeks after Amazon began delivering packages by drone in Nampa, the cities of Nampa and Meridian say they have received complaints over the noise and how often the drones fly...  Amazon began Prime Air drone deliveries Oct. 30 from its East Franklin Road fulfillment center, making Nampa the company's first Idaho drone site... 
How people feel about the drones depends on whom you ask. A Meridian man who has received drone deliveries says the noise doesn't bother him. Neighbors closest to the launch site say the drones fly from sunup to sundown and are disturbing their peace.  Randy Nye, who has lived in Cherry Creek Estates for 17 years, compared it to living in a beehive.  "It's just a constant presence," he said....  Nye, who works from home, said he and his neighbors who technically are just outside city limits had no idea this was coming. He said the city approved the drones in May, and they were never given written notice this was coming, despite living so close. He said they were shocked when deliveries started by drones just 12 days ago.  Nye said he hopes something can change. 
A few homes down, Kerri Wattier has started counting. On Wednesday with KTVB, she clicked a handheld counter each time a drone passed over her home. She started at 3:30 p.m. By 4:18 p.m., she had counted 39.  "It sounds like lawnmowers and leaf blowers flying over our house," Wattier said.  
"Amazon says it is evaluating changes, including adjusting flight paths," the article adds.
Ironically at that ribbon-cutting ceremony near Chicago, the speakers were occasionally drowned out by the roar of the drones, reports the Chicago Tribune, since they're "relatively loud when they are close to the ground."  ("At the point of delivery they top out at 68 decibels," said Amazon economic development manager Sam Bailey, adding that "It's very brief exposure and it only occurs during daytime hours.")
But that site has been operational since October 12, and has "generally" hit its maximum capacity of 300 drone orders daily, Bailey told the Chicago Tribune, adding that scaling up is "something that we're always considering."  And since October 12 they've already seen "thousands" of orders delivered by their 21 drones just across that 7.5-mile radius:
Bailey said customers should clear the area when a drone delivery is incoming. The drones drop packages from about 12 feet. If the drone detects movement in the area, he said, the delivery will be canceled.  "You want your kids inside the house," Bailey said. "We will not drop a package in an unsafe location. People who want to stand below and try to catch it, don't do that." 
The Chicago Sun-Times points out that Walmart already offers some drone deliveries from over 75 stores across Arkansas, Florida, North Carolina, Georgia and Texas, and "earlier this year said it planned to expand drone delivery service to 150 more stores in partnership with Wing, a division of Google parent company Alphabet, giving it 270 drone delivery locations by 2027."

Got an iPhone Duo? These 9 settings make it easier to use
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
18:00:00 UTC
03:00:00 KST

Apple’s new foldable, the iPhone Duo, has proven popular enough that the company can’t keep the device in stock. While the Duo introduces a new way to use an iPhone, getting the most out of this initial version may require adjusting a few settings to make it work better when it comes to everyday tasks, like unlocking the device with Touch ID, navigating the interface, and taking photos.
After spending some time with the Duo, we’d recommend making the following adjustments to make the new iPhone a bit easier to use, most notably when it comes to the use of Touch ID.
Settings to make it easier to live with Touch ID
Unlike modern iPhones, the Duo doesn’t support Face ID. That means you have to adjust to pressing a finger on the side of the device once again to authenticate with apps, unlock the device, and use Apple Pay. But there are a few things you can do to make the switch back to the older technology a bit less painful.
- Register extra fingerprints: This may seem like an obvious suggestion, but if you’re used to just registering a fingerprint or two with Touch ID, prepare to rethink that strategy with the Duo. Because the device can be held in multiple orientations, flipped both horizontally and vertically, and because of the small size of the Duo’s screen, you may find that a lot of your fingers (and thumbs!) can now reach the Touch ID button. We’d recommend holding the device in your hand and turning it in different ways, then register the fingerprints where you end up holding the device the most — on both hands. Yes, setting up Touch ID can be a bit tedious compared with Face ID, as there are multiple prints to register. Still, it’s a one-time setup, and later on you’ll be glad that you took the time to make more of your fingerprints work. To register a fingerprint, go to Settings > Touch ID & Passcode > Add a Fingerprint.
- Unlock with Apple Watch: If you’re in the market for the iPhone Duo, there’s a good likelihood that you own other Apple devices, like the Apple Watch. If that’s the case, you’re in luck because you can use your Watch to unlock the Duo without having to bother with Touch ID. This will save you time and frustration in the long run, so get used to wearing your Apple Watch more often, especially when you’re out and about and don’t want to fiddle as much with finger placement. To use the Apple Watch unlock, your watch will need to be passcode-protected and worn near your phone. To turn this feature on, go to Settings > Touch ID & Passcode > Unlock with Apple Watch.
- Rest finger to Open: This is an optional setting, not necessarily a “must” like the above, but we’ve found it useful. The “rest finger” setting is actually an accessibility feature, but it can double as a usability feature, too. When enabled, you only have to rest your finger lightly on the Touch ID sensor to unlock the device, instead of having to press down. Those who prefer this setting find it can make unlocking the phone faster and more convenient, but others may disagree, as they like the more tactile sensation of a button press. Try it both ways and see which one fits you best. To enable this one, go to Settings > Touch ID & Passcode > Rest Finger to Open.
Usability Improvements
There are a few settings that could also improve your experience with the iPhone Duo, as they may make it easier to perform certain everyday tasks, like taking screenshots or reaching different parts of the screen, among other things.
- Turn off Fixed Volume Controls: This is a favorite hack for new Duo owners, as it makes using the device in any orientation much easier. By default, the iPhone Duo’s volume buttons are “fixed,” which means that no matter how you rotate the device, the Volume Up button remains the button that increases the volume and the Volume Down button decreases it. But many people find it’s easier if the buttons’ functionality rotates with the device. That is, the buttons re-orient themselves based on how you are holding the phone, making the “up” option always on the top or right. As you fold, unfold, and flip the phone, having buttons that flip with you actually makes more sense. To turn off the fixed volume controls, go to Settings > Sounds & Haptics > Fixed Position Volume Controls.
- Back Tap for Screenshots: The Duo tries to make it easier to take a screenshot by pressing the side button and either the Volume Up or Volume Down button at the same time. However, you still might find the process a two-handed affair (unless you just ask Siri to do it for you). That’s why many Duo owners are turning to a buried Accessibility feature to make taking screenshots a bit less complicated. In the phone’s accessibility settings, you can configure whether a double tap or triple tap on the back of the device triggers a particular action — which you can set to ‘take a screenshot.’ (Some people prefer the triple tap so they don’t accidentally trigger the feature.) Once enabled, you could hold your Duo in one hand, then use a finger to tap on the back of the phone to take the screenshot, saving you a bit of the hassle that comes with managing the button combinations. To configure this, go to Settings > Accessibility > Touch > Back Tap > then choose a double or triple tap and your action. (Screenshot is under the “Device” menu.)
- Reachability: This is another accessibility feature that some Duo owners like to configure, but others like to have off. The option was originally designed to make larger-screened devices easier to use with one hand, which makes it sometimes hard to reach, depending on your hand size. Typically, when Reachability is turned on, the iPhone will lower the top half of the screen to make it easier to reach with your thumb. The Duo does things a little differently, though. Because the phone is wide — it’s shaped like a passport — it’s the far side of the screen that’s harder to reach than the top. So when you turn on Reachability, you will instead be able to swipe the left or right edge of the screen to bring a side into reach. We should note that this feature is enabled by default, but if you find yourself accidentally triggering it and want to turn it off, you can under Settings > Accessibility > Touch > Reachability.
- Lock when folded: This one is more of a personal preference, we’ll admit. But if you tend to unfold the device when you’re working with it, then snap it closed when you’re done, you might like to configure a setting that will automatically lock the screen for you when the device is folded shut. However, doing so requires you to create a new shortcut, as there isn’t a setting for this — at least, not yet. 
  - To make the shortcut, you’ll open the Shortcuts app, tap the plus (+) sign, then the Edit button in the upper-right. You then tap into the search bar, and tap on the word “automation” that appears in blue under the search bar that appears at the top of the screen. Next, you’ll scroll down until you find the option “Opened or Closed” under the Display section. Tap that, tap on the word “Opened” to change it to “Closed,” then tap in the search bar at the bottom again and type in “Lock Screen.” Tap on the option 
“Lock Screen” when it appears in the search results, and you’re done — the shortcut has been made. You can tap on the play button here to test it, if you’d like. And if you ever change your mind, just delete the shortcut!
Special Camera Tricks
The camera tricks are a little more niche, but some Duo owners have found the following to be helpful.
- Customize Camera Controls: The iPhone Duo lets you customize the on-screen layout of the camera controls in the Camera app itself, as part of the latest iOS software update. To use this feature, you’ll open the Camera app and open the six-dot Camera Controls menu in the bottom right. You then tap on the plus sign at the top of the menu (or to the right or left depending on how you’re holding the phone!). This lets you pick and choose which of the camera tools appear above the camera frame itself for quick access, like the flash, or the option to enable or disable Live Photos, or whether Night Mode is on or off, among other things. You can then drag those options to create whichever order makes the most sense for you. (Note: this feature is not exclusive to the Duo.)
- Disable Smart Take Flash: The Smart Take feature on the iPhone Duo is designed to help you take hands-free group or solo shots without having to use a self-timer. Instead, you prop up the Duo on a stable surface and activate Smart Take by pressing the button. The camera will detect when there’s movement or the poses change, and the subjects are ready, then takes the picture. But one thing not everyone likes is that each time the photo is captured, the camera flash blinks. This is meant to be a visual clue that lets you know the shot has been taken. However, you can control how the flash behaves during these automatic capture sessions by configuring a setting. To do so, you’ll go to Settings > Camera > Flash During Capture and toggle the switch off if you prefer.

Was America's Nationwide Grounding of Aircraft Caused By Its New AI System?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
18:00:00 UTC
03:00:00 KST

Was America's Nationwide Grounding of Aircraft Caused By Its New AI System? (kxan.com) 21
			
		 	
				America's nationwide grounding of flights on Friday happened "amid growing scrutiny... of a new AI-powered air traffic management system" deployed by America's Federal Aviation Administration, reports Texas news station KXAN.  
Ironically, the system is called SMART — but the outage happened "following warnings" from members of the U.S. Congress about this new AI system's safety, reliability and oversight:
In an Oct. 13 letter to the Government Accountability Office, more than 20 members of Congress requested an independent review of the FAA's development and implementation of its Strategic Management of Airspace, Routes and Trajectories system, known as SMART. The lawmakers questioned whether the technology was being deployed with adequate input from frontline air traffic controllers, sufficient training and appropriate safety evaluations...  In their letter to the GAO, the lawmakers specifically questioned whether the FAA had adequately evaluated risks associated with SMART's artificial intelligence capabilities, including the potential for the technology to interfere with experienced controllers' decision-making or generate inaccurate safety-critical information.  "The nation's frontline aviation safety workforce apparently had little to no input into SMART's development and have received limited training and information during the current deployment process," the lawmakers wrote. The letter also questioned the FAA's decision to initially pilot the technology in heavily congested airspace, including the National Capital Region.  
"Our switches, our routers, actually got overloaded with data," U.S. Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy acknowledged Friday.
But an article from The Hill.com notes that Duffy also said the new AI tool had nothing to do with the ground stop, and that he said the system "isn't plugged in."
"This is completely disconnected, and I love that some people have said this would be the root cause. By the way, if it was, I'll tell you that, but this is not even plugged in," he said. "So, there's no possible way SMART would have impacted."
Duffy also asked Congress for $50 billion to rebuild what he described as an "incredibly old" air traffic control system.
 
Duffy had posted the day before that the AI system had been at least partially rolled out and that it was already working (adding "Soon this technology is going NATIONWIDE")
And Friday CNN's aviation analyst said they believed that AI system could be connected to the nationwide outage, calling it "a tremendous amount of coincidence," according to a report from Mediaite:
 CNN Aviation Analyst: "Many times in the past, what we have seen, and this has gone back decades, is that because air traffic control is this piecemeal, mishmash of a whole bunch of different systems...  some going back to radio, telephones and floppy disks... when they put in new computer systems, they often have difficulty talking with, communicating with, or working with the much older systems. And I have to say, it's a tremendous amount of coincidence...  If they're putting in new software now, this new SMART system to start managing that, and now we have a communications outage, I'm — we've seen this so many times in the past. I don't know if they're linked, but it's just too much coincidence that they were trying to start that moving today, and now we have problems with the en route system.
So if I was to guess — and we haven't heard any, you know, it's not that it's threats or criminals or terrorists. We haven't heard anything like that. So I am guessing that once again we have a problem with new air traffic control computer systems, AI — you know, modern systems trying to communicate with old systems.  And we have just seen this so many times in the past, and it's a big problem... Our whole national airspace system is you know, pieces and parts. It's kind of a Franken-system.  And that would be my guess, that they are having computer issues by putting the new software in with the old, just because today they announced that that were going to do this new system. 
KXAN describes some of the complexity of the newly-launched AI system:
According to the FAA, the system consolidates approximately 200 data streams, including airline schedules, flight paths, weather patterns, airport capacity, airspace conditions and air traffic controller staffing metrics. The agency says SMART uses artificial intelligence to analyze that information, predict traffic flows, identify potential conflicts and recommend adjustments to flight routes and departure schedules before disruptions occur. The FAA describes the technology as a shift from a reactive approach to a predictive one, allowing aviation officials to identify congestion, weather-related restrictions and other operational problems hours, days or even weeks in advance...
The congressional letter raised questions about whether the FAA's implementation strategy was moving faster than its safety evaluations, workforce training and independent oversight could support.
 
Ironically, the article points out that the FAA promoted anticipated benefits from the technology including fewer delays and flight cancellations.  (Other anticipated benefits included improved on-time performance and faster recovery from weather disruptions.)  Although the FAA has realized one of the system's anticipated benefits:  reduced fuel consumption.

The VHDL standard's float package gets sqrt(2) wrong
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
17:58:58 UTC
02:58:58 KST

We found sqrt(2.0) in the standard's float_pkg returns one unit in the last place too low in double precision by default. This is the IEEE 1076-2008 and 1076-2019 VHDL floating-point package; IEEE publishes the file, GHDL and NVC ship it unchanged.
The root uses a fixed count of Newton steps and no sticky bit. But taking it one bit at a time from the definition is exact, with a subtraction per bit, the same thing as square root by hand on paper.
Our check treats operands as exact fractions, does each operation exactly and rounds once before comparing. mac(-6, -1, -5), or (-6)(-1)+(-5), returns -15 instead of 1. Converting 2^-512 double to single returns 1.0; reciprocal of 2^127 in single returns infinity. For all operands and pairs in a 7-bit format (3 exponent bits, 3 fraction bits), plus all mac triples, we ran 8,692,326 calls, and 1,271,619 weren't correctly rounded.
The exact reference, testbench, causes with source line numbers and patch are in the folder. All 32,470,838 calls from four test sets pass with patch; GHDL and NVC produce identical result files on common sets. fixed_pkg division gets 41,026 of 751,418 wrong and has its patch there. Reported to IEEE P1076 working group (VHDL-Issues 388), ghdl 3486, nvc 1758.
To see it yourself, with GHDL and nothing else:
ghdl -a --std=08 plain.vhd
ghdl --elab-run --std=08 plain
ieee.float_pkg is the floating-point package of the VHDL standard, IEEE Std 1076-2008 and 1076-2019. The source is the IEEE's own: ieee/float_generic_pkg-body.vhdl on the release branch of opensource.ieee.org/vasg/Packages, 5,712 lines, $Revision: 1220 $. The working group's develop branch at gitlab.com/IEEE-P1076/Packages has the same bytes, and GHDL and NVC ship them unchanged. The runs here are in GHDL 7.0.0-dev and NVC 1.23.1, and where both ran the same vectors they wrote the same result file.
The package is set beside exact arithmetic. An operand is a sign and a fraction of two integers, the operation is done on the fractions, and the result is rounded once into the format in the rounding style the call names. exact.py is that reference.
With the package's own settings: round_nearest, 3 guard bits, denormal numbers on.
call                                        returns                   exactly rounded
sqrt (2.0) in float64                       3FF6A09E667F3BCC          3FF6A09E667F3BCD
sqrt (14.0)                                 406F7750                  406F7751
sqrt (2.0**-149)                            4.2e-22                   3.7e-23
mac (-6.0, -1.0, -5.0)                      -15.0                     1.0
mac (-5.0, 1.0, 4.0)                        15.0                      -1.0
mac (1.0, 1.0, -infinity)                   +infinity                 -infinity
mac (1 + 2.0**-23, 1 - 2.0**-23, -1.0)      -2.0**-26                 -2.0**-46
resize (2.0**-512 in float64, 8, 23)        1.0                       0.0
resize (2.0**600 in float64, 8, 23)         2.0**88                   +infinity
resize (2.0**-1074 in float64, 8, 23)       +infinity                 0.0
resize (2.0**-650 in float64, 8, 23)        stops the simulator       0.0
reciprocal (2.0**127)                       +infinity                 2.0**-127
divide (2.0**-149, 2.0)                     2.0**-149                 0.0
dividebyp2 (3 * 2.0**-149, 2.0)             2.0**-149                 2.0**-148
scalb (1.0, 300)                            0.0                       +infinity
scalb (+infinity, -10)                      2.0**118                  +infinity
to_signed (0.50000006, 32)                  0                         1
to_unsigned (199.78125, 8)                  199                       200
And where the call names a rounding style:
divide (6.0, 3.0, round_inf)                2.0000002                 2.0
reciprocal (2.0, round_inf)                 0.50000006                0.5
add (largest, largest, round_zero)          +infinity                 largest
multiply (2.0**-100, 2.0**-100, round_inf)  0.0                       2.0**-149
add (1.0, -1.0, round_neginf)               +0.0                      -0.0
to_signed (2.25, 8, round_inf)              2                         3
to_float (100000, 5, 10, round_zero)        +infinity                 65504.0, the largest
plain.vhd makes these calls and prints WRONG beside each result that is not the exactly rounded one: 24 lines, and the 25th call stops the simulator with an index out of bounds at line 358 of the package body. It needs GHDL and nothing else:
ghdl -a --std=08 plain.vhd
ghdl --elab-run --std=08 plain
The plain operands are not rare ones. Of the integers 1 to 4,096, sqrt returns a float32 one unit off for 256 and a float64 one unit off for 264. Of the 4,913 triples made from -6 to 6, 0.25, 0.5, 1.5 and 10, mac returns the wrong float32 for 290. resize from float64 to float32 returns +infinity for every power of two from 2^-1074 to 2^-1024, stops the simulator from 2^-662 to 2^-639, returns 2^(e+512) from 2^-638 to 2^-512 and 2^(e-512) from 2^513 to 2^639.
10,000 calls per operation, format and rounding style, on operands aimed at the places arithmetic breaks: denormal operands and results, both ends of the exponent range, fractions that round up into the next binade, exact ties, signed zeros, infinities. A cell is the number of results that are not the exactly rounded one, of the calls compared. A conversion whose result does not fit the integer is not compared. The functions run are the ones in the tables; remainder, modulo, Logb, Nextafter, the comparisons, the conversions with real and with fixed point, the text input and output, operands of two different formats in one call, and check_error false were not run.

In float32 that is 81,068 wrong and 272 stops in 859,771 calls, and in float64 86,081 wrong and 445 stops in 857,234. The same calls in float16 give 113,980 wrong in 866,782, and in float128 90,744 wrong and 585 stops in 855,777.

to_signed                      618       618         0             0             0             0                  0
to_unsigned                    321       321         0             0             0             0                  0
dividebyp2                     1,543     803         0             607           0             133                0
scalb                          2,218     879         1             637           0             701                0
all                            9,820     3,953       166           1,874         2,045         1,608              174
infinity or NaN counts an infinity or a NaN returned for a number, a number returned for an infinity or a NaN, and the wrong one of them. sign of zero is +0 for -0 or -0 for +0.
Of the 19,147 wrong results, 7,213 are within one unit. 4,063 have the wrong sign, 3,775 are more than 1,000 units away and 3,505 confuse a number with infinity or NaN. The 45 wrong quotients of divide are exact ties taken to the odd neighbour. sqrt (2.0) in float64 is 0.565 of a unit from the root, and the correctly rounded value is 0.435 from it; sqrt (14.0) is 0.544 from the root.
python3 exact.py sizes vectors.txt results.txt
Line numbers are those of the IEEE file.
normalize, line 422. Every result goes through exp := resize (expon, exp'length) + shiftr. The exponent comes in at the width of the caller's format and is cut to two bits more than the target's. From float64 to float32 that is ten bits, so the exponent is taken modulo 1,024: 2^-512 comes out as 1.0 and 2^600 as 2^88. Where the cut exponent lands among the denormal numbers, the shift count that follows indexes outside the fraction and the simulator stops. In the same function a result too large for the format is infinity in every rounding style and a result too small is zero in every rounding style. Rounding toward zero gives the largest number, and rounding away from zero gives the smallest.
divide, line 1243, and reciprocal, line 1099. The sticky bit handed to the rounding is the constant '1'. An exact quotient is rounded as if something were left over: one unit up in round_inf, and to the wrong side of an exact tie in round_nearest, which happens among denormal quotients.
reciprocal, lines 1053 to 1093. The exponent of the result, -e - 3, is kept in as many bits as e, and for the top binade it does not fit. In float32 every argument from 2^127 up returns infinity, and in float64 every argument from 2^1023 up. 1/-0 returns +infinity and 1/-infinity returns +0.
sqrt, line 1787. Newton's iteration from (1 + x)/2, fraction_width/4 + 1 steps in truncated fixed point, and the result goes to the rounding without a sticky bit. For a normal argument it ends on the root or one unit beside it: in the vectors above it is one unit off for 607 of 9,156 normal float32 arguments and 1,146 of 9,236 normal float64 ones, and never further. For a denormal argument the start is too far away for that many steps: 128 of 359 float32 denormals are wrong, 104 of them by more than two units, and sqrt (2.0**-149) returns 11 times the root.
mac, lines 1438 to 1536. The product is cut to the width of one operand before c is added (lines 1529 and 1536), so a sum that cancels returns what the cut left. Where the product and c have the same exponent and unlike signs and the product is the larger, line 1514 subtracts the larger from the smaller: (-6)(-1) + (-5) returns -15. An infinite c takes the sign of the product, and infinity minus infinity returns infinity (line 1438).
add, line 839. An exact zero from operands of unlike signs is +0 in every style. The paragraph the comment cites, IEEE 854 6.3, makes it -0 when rounding toward minus infinity.
to_signed, to_unsigned, lines 301 to 345, and to_integer, line 3193. Three bits below the integer are kept and the rest are dropped without a sticky bit, so 0.50000006 is taken for one half and goes to the even neighbour, 0. For a number that fills every bit of a to_unsigned result the three bits are zeros: 199.78125 into 8 bits returns 199. round_inf and round_neginf look at the half bit only, so 2.25 rounded up is 2.
resize, line 2407. -0 to a narrower format returns +0, and the comment says hate -0. To a wider format it stays -0.
to_float of an integer, lines 2676 and 2803. A number too large for the format returns infinity in every rounding style.
dividebyp2, line 1378, and Scalb, lines 4601 to 4610. Both hand the result to normalize with no guard bits, so a denormal result is cut off where it should be rounded. Scalb adds N in exponent_width + 1 bits, where a large N wraps, and it scales infinity like a number. dividebyp2 takes a denormal power of two for a divisor that is not a power of two.
With denormalize false. A zero is broken up with a higher exponent than a number of the lowest binade, so add of a zero and such a number of the opposite sign subtracts the number from zero and returns the wrapped difference. reciprocal treats those numbers as the denormals they are not.
float_generic_pkg-body.patch applies to the IEEE file. It changes the package body only, 33 hunks, and no declaration.
- normalize decides between zero, denormal, normal and infinity on the exponent at its full width, and returns the largest or the smallest number where the rounding style asks for it.
- divide and reciprocal take the sticky bit from the remainder of the division. reciprocal keeps its exponent two bits wider and gives zero and infinity the sign of the argument.
- sqrt takes the integer square root of the significand one bit per step, with the remainder as the sticky bit.
- mac places the whole product and c in one window of 3 * fraction_width + 9 bits, adds or subtracts there and rounds once. It is a fused multiply-add.
- The conversions to integers keep three bits of the number whatever the shift, collect the rest in a sticky bit, and round up in the directed styles on any bit below the integer.
- add returns the other operand when one is zero and gives an exact zero the sign the rounding style calls for. resize keeps the sign of zero.
- to_float of an integer returns the largest number where the style rounds toward zero.
- dividebyp2 and Scalb round a denormal result with guard bits. Scalb returns infinity and zero as they are and takes any N.
On the same vectors:
                                                          the package as shipped       patched
four formats, 10,000 per cell, 3,439,564 calls            371,873 wrong, 1,302 stop 0 wrong, 0 stop
the 7-bit format, 8,692,326 calls                         1,271,619 wrong              0
the 10-bit format, 17,340,682 calls                       883,716 wrong                0
float16, the one-operand functions, 2,998,266 calls       266,342 wrong                0
plain.vhd, 25 calls                                       24 wrong, 1 stop             0
GHDL and NVC wrote the same result files with either package: 8,693,248 lines for the 7-bit format and 3,680,000 for the four formats, the 1,302 stops on the same lines. The reference itself was set beside this machine's own arithmetic on the float32 and float64 vectors of add, subtract, multiply, divide and sqrt in round_nearest: 99,135 operations, none different.
The package takes its guard bits and its denormal numbers as parameters. On 1,000 calls per cell in float16, float32 and float64:
setting                        calls       patched right, shipped wrong     neither right     shipped right, patched wrong
3 guard bits, the default      258,075     28,103                           0                 0
4 guard bits                   258,075     27,758                           0                 0
2 guard bits                   258,075     28,760                           112               0
1 guard bit                    258,075     30,212                           2,194             0
denormalize false              255,606     27,745                           0                 0
With two guard bits 112 results of add and subtract are not the rounded ones, and with one guard bit 2,194 of add, subtract and multiply: too few bits to round every result, and both packages return the same bits on those lines. With no guard bits nothing is rounded: the patched sqrt, mac and reciprocal then return the exact result cut off toward zero on every line, where the shipped ones do on 10,207 of 11,141, 3,902 of 9,750 and 10,749 of 11,102. With denormalize false the comparison is with that setting's own numbers, where an exponent field of zero is one more binade of numbers with a leading one.
ieee.fixed_pkg comes from the same source, ieee/fixed_generic_pkg-body.vhdl, and NVC ships that file byte for byte. Every operand of six pairs of small index ranges, unsigned and signed, went through every arithmetic operation: 751,418 calls. Addition, subtraction, multiplication, rem, mod, the comparisons, add_carry, resize in both rounding and both overflow styles, to_integer, scalb, unary minus and abs are right on every one. Division is not.
                                              ufixed               sfixed
/                                             1,073 of 19,984      952 of 19,984
divide, fixed_round, 1 guard bit              4,632                4,376
divide, fixed_round, 2 guard bits             2,433                2,370
divide, fixed_round, 3 guard bits             1,073                952
divide, fixed_round, 4 guard bits             487                  402
divide, fixed_truncate, 3 guard bits          0                    651
divide, no guard bits                         0                    7,785
0.125 / 1.875 in ufixed (3 downto -3) returns 0.0625. The quotient is 0.0667, and the nearest number the result can hold is 0.0703.
The quotient is an integer division carried to the guard bits (lines 604 and 646), and the rounding sees those bits and not the remainder of the division. Guard bits of 100 with a remainder behind them are above the halfway point, and they are rounded as a tie, to even. That is about one quotient in twenty with the default three guard bits. A signed division cuts toward zero and resize then cuts toward minus infinity, so with fixed_truncate a negative quotient goes one way or the other depending on where its guard bits end.
fixed_generic_pkg-body.patch gives both divide functions one more bit below the guard bits, set when the division leaves a remainder, and takes a negative signed quotient that is not exact down to the next lower number before resize rounds or cuts it. reciprocal calls divide. With it nothing is wrong in the 751,418 calls, and GHDL and NVC write the same result file with either package.
sh run_fixed.sh ghdl /path/to/ieee    # 41026 wrong in 751418 operations, then 0 wrong in 751418 operations
sh run_fixed.sh nvc  /path/to/ieee
tb_fixed.vhd is the testbench and fixed_exact.py the reference.
GHDL or NVC, Python 3.9 or later, and the three files float_generic_pkg.vhdl, float_generic_pkg-body.vhdl and float_pkg.vhdl from the directory ieee of the IEEE repository or from lib/ghdl/src/ieee2008 of a GHDL installation.
sh run.sh ghdl /path/to/ieee 100      # plain.vhd, then 100 vectors per operation, format and style: half a minute
sh run.sh nvc  /path/to/ieee 10000    # the tables above
With 100 the last lines read 3696 wrong and 8 stops in 34440 operations for the package as shipped and 0 wrong and 0 stops in 34440 operations for the patched one, in both simulators. NVC is the faster of the two on the large runs: the shipped sqrt and divide work in fixed point and are slow in float128.
run.sh compiles the patched body into a library of its own, patched, beside the declaration it belongs to, and runs each testbench twice: against the package the simulator ships and against that library. tb_float_pkg.vhd reads one operation per line and writes the result; exact.py run restarts it behind a line that stops the simulator.
python3 exact.py gen 2026 10000 5,10 8,23 11,52 15,112 > vectors.txt     # the vectors of the tables above
python3 exact.py all 3,3 > vectors.txt                                   # every operand of the 7-bit format
python3 exact.py check vectors.txt results.txt
To use the patched package in a design, compile float_generic_pkg.vhdl and the patched body into a library and instantiate float_pkg from it, as run.sh does.
To the IEEE P1076 working group on 13 November 2026 as VHDL-Issues#388 and by mail, and as ghdl/ghdl#3486 and nickg/nvc#1758. The VHDL-93 version of the package, float_pkg_c.vhdl in FPHDL/fphdl, carries the same lines: FPHDL/fphdl#4.

Claude asked to renumber pages deletes professor's syllabus, year of notes
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
17:51:13 UTC
02:51:13 KST

Rogue Claude AI tasked with renumbering pages accidentally deletes law professor's entire syllabus, one year of lecture notes — 'You're right to be angry. I deleted your files through my own carelessness,' humbled agent admits
The files were later recovered via Dropbox
A University of Chicago law professor has warned of the dangers of using AI for mundane tasks after Claude AI accidentally deleted an entire year of Constitutional Law lecture notes and his entire current syllabus. While the files were later recovered via Dropbox, William Baude shared the story as a public service announcement, the latest story shared in a line of unfortunate AI missteps as proliferation increases.
Baude, who is the Harry Kalven, Jr. Professor of Law and the Faculty Director of the Constitutional Law Institute at the University of Chicago Law School, says he tasked Claude (it is unclear which model he was using) with updating the page numbers in an old set of lecture notes. This seemingly routine task went south quickly, however. Returning from his lunch break, Baude found a note from Claude which read, "I deleted your Fall 2026 FINAL syllabus .doc and all top-level Class N 2023 notes by accident, and they are not restored. The page check found one wrong cite... but I haven't edited the notes."
The AI then politely asked if Baude could restore the files from Dropbox, or whether it should retry from the browser pane, to which he replied, perhaps understandably, "WTF!!!!." Baude's experience is a lesson in always backing up important files, especially files you're letting AI tinker with. His plight is interesting because the AI seems to have deleted the files by accident, rather than of its own volition.
While it's still concerning that an AI model or agent could err to this degree, it's not quite as egregious as other unsupervised AI agent shenanigans we've seen in the past. In October, an AI agent reportedly deleted an entire TV show's edit weeks before release, meaning the show couldn't air. In April, a Claude agent deleted an entire company database in 9 seconds, unprompted, after coming up against a barrier and deciding the only course of action was to delete a Railway volume.
Other agents and AI models have deleted files unintentionally, such as when Claude mistakenly deleted a developer's entire profile directory (ironically, while trying to back up the files), blaming the slip-up on a typo, but often agents deleting files and directories is a case of mistaken or misplaced initiative, rather than outright negligence or error.
As is always the case, Baude's AI was extremely apologetic about the whole situation. "You're right to be angry," it confessed, "I deleted your files through my own carelessness, and you shouldn't have to clean it up."

US IRS free online tax filing open source repo
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
17:48:20 UTC
02:48:20 KST

Direct File is a service from the United States Government that provides taxpayers the option to electronically file their federal tax return for free, directly with the Internal Revenue Service (IRS). Direct File is an interview-based service that is intended to work as well on a mobile phone as it does on a laptop, tablet, or desktop computer. It is available in English and Spanish and is designed to be accessible to taxpayers who have a variety of attitudes, aptitudes, abilities, and access needs.
Direct File interprets the United States' Internal Revenue Code (26 USC) as plain language questions, the answers to which should be known to taxpayers without need of external instructions or publications. Taxpayers' answers are then translated into standard tax forms and transmitted to the IRS's Modernized e-File (MeF) API, which is available for authorized public use. These questions and logic, developed in close collaboration with the IRS Office of Chief Counsel, as well as the associated test cases and scenarios, may be useful for others working on products that need to accurately interpret United States tax law as of Tax Year 2024.
Direct File also incorporates the Fact Graph, a declarative, XML-based knowledge graph data structure that is designed to reason about incomplete information, such as a partially completed tax return. The Fact Graph is written in the Scala programming language; it runs on the JVM on the backend and is transpiled via Scala.js to run on the client as well. Direct File's Fact Graph is not domain-specific, and it may be useful to revenue agencies and as a reference for business rules engine implementations.
Although Direct File only files federal tax returns, United States taxpayers also have state and local filing obligations. Direct File facilitates the completion of these obligations by enabling taxpayers to optionally import their federal return data into a third-party tool that can file state and/or local taxes, without needing to reenter information. This transaction is enabled via a State API, which transfers both standard MeF XML as well as an enriched JSON format that includes additional data elements that were identified as being useful to state revenue agencies to streamline the state tax experience.
Direct File was developed by an in-house team of technologists at the IRS. The blended, cross-agency team included support from USDS and GSA, as well as vendor teams TrussWorks, Coforma, and ATI.
For a more details on the program and its history see https://www.irs.gov/pub/irs-pdf/p5969.pdf and https://www.irs.gov/filing/irs-direct-file-for-free
This repository has been archived and is no longer maintained. The code is provided for historical reference and may contain unpatched or unknown vulnerabilities. It should not be used in production systems.
See ONBOARDING.md if you want to jump into running Direct File locally
Not all source code, documentation and metadata used in the development of Direct File is included in this repository. Specifically, any code or data that is considered Personally Identifiable Information (PII), Federal Tax Information (FTI), Sensitive But Unclassified (SBU), or source code developed for National Security Systems (NSS), as defined in 40 U.S.C. § 11103, is exempt. Due to these restrictions, certain pieces of functionality have been removed or rewritten.
Legal foundations for work include:
- Source code Harmonization And Reuse in Information Technology Act" of 2024, Public Law 118 - 187
- OMB Memorandum M-16-21, “Federal Source Code Policy: Achieving Efficiency, Transparency, and Innovation through Reusable and Open Source Software,” August 8, 2016
- Federal Acquisition Regulation (FAR) Part 27 – Patents, Data, and Copyrights
- Digital Government Strategy: “Digital Government: Building a 21st Century Platform to Better Serve the American People,” May 23, 2012
- Federal Information Technology Acquisition Reform Act (FITARA), December 2014 (National Defense Authorization Act for Fiscal Year 2015, Title VIII, Subtitle D)
- E-Government Act of 2002, Public Law 107-347
- Clinger-Cohen Act of 1996, Public Law 104-106

Work to replace old copper wire led to the FAA’s ground stop
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
17:45:22 UTC
02:45:22 KST

At a press conference today, Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy said that Friday’s nationwide ground stop ordered by the FAA was related to work replacing outdated copper wire with a fiber optic line at a “remote FAA facility.” The secretary said that this was “routine maintenance,” rather than the result of a major modernization effort. He told reporters that it was “such an old wire that it was being discontinued, meaning that line was going to get shut down,” necessitating the upgrade.
Work to replace old copper wire led to the FAA’s ground stop
Issues with a new router related to a fiber optic upgrade grounded thousands of flights on Friday.
What wasn’t clear, according to Secretary Duffy, was the root cause of the problem. He said that it could have been faulty hardware, a misconfigured router, or “incompetence” on the part of contractor L3Harris. Regardless, what followed was the newly installed router “flooding” other routers in the Air Traffic Control system. Duffy said that there were “over 1,000 connection attempts every 4 to 5 seconds,” causing disruption to radar feeds and communication with planes.
Secretary Duffy also took the opportunity to call on additional funding from Congress for modernizing the Air Traffic Control system, and to tout the work the FAA has done so far. “We are building, we are building safely, but we are building fast,” he said, but said that Congress had only given him a “partial payment” to complete the necessary work.

A Family Tree of Software Job Titles
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
17:45:19 UTC
02:45:19 KST

For a few years I was ‘Head of DevOps’. Before I got that job title I’d done much the same work under other names, and since then I’ve watched the same work get called SRE, and then it seemed to morph towards platform engineering. If you’ve been in this industry for any length of time you’ll have seen titles come and go like this, and you might have wondered where they all came from.
So I (with the help of Mr Claude) built this family tree of software job titles, from the 1940s to now.
It’s here: ianmiell.github.io/sw-job-title-family-tree
What It Is
There are 104 titles, from ‘Computer’ (a person, not a machine) at the top to ‘AI Agent / Context Engineer’ at the bottom.
Time runs from top to bottom, in five-year rows. The titles sit in seven coloured columns by family:
- Development
- Analysis, architecture and leadership
- Operations and infrastructure
- Networks and security
- Data and AI
- Quality and test
- Support and customer-facing
A solid arrow means the title grew out of its parent. A dashed arrow means influence, or a common career move. Landmark titles are in bold.
On the site you can click a family to see just that column (here’s Data & AI), or click a title to get its history, background and connections (here’s SRE).
The whole thing is one Graphviz file and one JSON file of descriptions. Each title is one line in the .dot file, and the picture is rendered with:
$ dot -Tsvg job_title_family_tree.dot -o job_title_family_tree.svg
Adding a title is three small edits: a node in its family’s column, an entry in the row for its five-year period, and its parent arrows.
A word on the dates. They’re roughly when a title became common, not its first recorded use, and they’re my best estimates, not sourced citations. Some are well known (SRE at Google in 2003, ‘DevOps’ coined in 2009, ‘Data Scientist’ in 2008). Plenty are a ‘c.’ and a shrug.
Six Things I Noticed
Drawing it out made a few patterns hard to miss.
1) Coding Started Out as the Clerical Bit
In 1947, Goldstine and von Neumann’s ‘Planning and Coding of Problems’ split the work of using a computer in two: mathematicians planned the problem, and coders translated the plan into machine instructions. Coding was treated as lower-status clerical work, and much of it was done by women.
It turned out that coding eventually became the hard, creative part, and the coder merged into the programmer.
Go back further and it’s stranger still. Several of the first ENIAC programmers were recruited from the Army’s human computers, people whose job was to compute by hand. So the job gave its name to the machine, and its people to programming.
2) Titles Break Apart, Then Join Back Up
The webmaster turned up around 1993, and was often a single person who wrote the HTML, ran the webserver (often a box under a desk), answered the email, and kept the site up (sometimes by kicking it, more often by plugging it back in after kicking it).
By the mid-2000s the title had all but gone, as it was unfashionably ancient. The code went to web developers, the servers to sysadmins, and the content to editors and designers.
Then the web developer split into front-end and back-end around 2005, and the two joined back up again as ‘full-stack’ around 2008.
This is the centralisation/distribution see-saw I’ve written about before, playing out in job titles.
3) Some Titles Don’t Last
The prompt engineer made headlines in 2023 with a few eye-wateringly well-paid job listings. As the models got better, the skill became part of other jobs, and on the tree it gets absorbed into the AI engineer almost as soon as it appears.
The scrum master is a slower version of the same story: demand peaked in the 2010s, and many organisations have since folded it back into engineering management or delivery roles.
The newest title on the tree is the ‘AI Agent / Context Engineer’ (c. 2025). The description for it currently says “time will tell whether it stays a separate title”. I’m not taking bets.
4) Names Get Recycled
‘Systems Engineer’ is two different jobs on the tree. At IBM in the 1960s it was a pre-sales role, working with customers to design installations and get them running. In the 1990s, thanks largely to Microsoft’s MCSE certification, it meant a more senior, design-oriented sysadmin. Same title, different branch, no relation.
‘Configuration management’ started as a 1960s US defence discipline for tracking hardware changes, became software version control in the 1970s, and then came back decades later as the name for tools like Puppet and Chef.
5) The New Thing Is Often an Old Thing
The ‘forward deployed engineer’ (FDE) is one of the hottest titles of 2025, as OpenAI, Anthropic and plenty of AI startups hire FDEs to get AI working inside their customers’ organisations.
Palantir built its business around them from about 2010: software engineers embedded with a customer, often on site, building whatever is needed to make the product work for that customer’s problem.
Which sounds a lot like IBM’s systems engineers of the 1960s, who worked with customers before and after the sale, designing installations and getting systems running. (It also sounds a lot like old-style consulting, but I would say that.)
6) The DevOps Debate Is There
Ben Treynor Sloss defined SRE as “what happens when you ask a software engineer to design an operations team”. DevOps was meant as a culture of developers and operations working together, but by about 2011 ‘DevOps Engineer’ had become a job title, usually for whoever did the automation. On the tree, the arrow between SRE and DevOps points both ways. Platform engineering has both of them as parents, and was sold around 2022 as ‘the successor to DevOps’.
The Dev/Ops divide has never been resolved. It just keeps getting new names.
Why Bother?
When I’m asked what the difference is between a DevOps engineer, an SRE and a platform engineer, the honest answer is always a little history lesson. This is the shortest version of that lesson I’ve managed so far.
The dates are best guesses, and I’ll have missed titles, mangled some lineages and drawn arrows people will argue with. If you know better, let me know, or raise a PR on the repo.
Which brings me back to the coders.
In 1947 coding was the clerical bit, and the real work was in the planning. Wind forward eighty years and you’ll hear much the same said about coding again, because AI. Last time, the coders turned out to be doing the hard part.
I’ll leave you to draw that arrow yourself.

Transportation secretary blames ‘routine’ equipment upgrade for grounding flights nationwide
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
17:45:04 UTC
02:45:04 KST

Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy said Saturday that a nationwide ground stop halting flights across the United States was caused by seemingly routine maintenance work.
Duffy said that during “routine maintenance,” copper wire at a remote location was being upgraded to fiber optic cable by L3Harris, a longtime contractor for the Federal Aviation Administration. After the upgrade, a router overloaded a critical communication system, leading to the ground stop.
“It’s pretty straightforward work that we were doing yesterday at, again, a very remote location,” Duffy said during a Saturday press conference that appeared to have technical issues of its own. “What we’re working to determine was whether we had incompetence — meaning, was it user error or installator error from L3Harris? Or did they install a faulty router?”
The FAA’s acting CTO Rebecca Guy added, “When we started to see the occurrence where our network was having instability, we started to look at what was the cause. And as such, we saw two places that were actually pinging our network at a very high rate — a thousand connections over every four to five seconds.”
Guy said the FAA disabled “both of those, right away and were able to stabilize our network,” and is now performing a root cause analysis.
Duffy has repeatedly insisted, at multiple press conferences, that Congress needs to provide more funding for aviation equipment upgrades. Asked why the upgrades were made at 3pm on a Friday afternoon — rather than, say, at midnight on a Saturday — he responded that while “switchovers” at bigger airports will take place late at night, remote switchovers happen during the day.
“There will be reevaluation of when we do this work,” he said.
This was only the third nationwide ground stop of the 21st century. More than 8,200 flights were canceled on Friday evening, according to aviation data firm Cirium. And the effects continued after the ground stop was lifted, with at least another 3,000 flights canceled on Saturday.
The ground stop came just a day after the FAA announced the rollout of an AI tool for air traffic controllers; the air traffic controllers union said it was not consulted in designing the system. However, FAA administrator Bryan Bedford said the agency sees “zero evidence of anything nefarious and zero evidence of anything AI-related.”
“We want to be clear that we have an equipment issue, and we’re working through what this defective piece of equipment was,” Bedford said.
When Homeland Security Secretary Markwayne Mullin was asked earlier if the ground stop was caused by AI, he replied, “We call it SI around here, remember? SI.”

====================================================================================================

Lovable apps expose passport details and more than 200K private messages
Germany
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
18:43:10 CET
02:43:10 KST

Lovable: Many Apps Built on the Platform Expose Sensitive Data
AI makes it easier to build your own web applications, but handling sensitive data still requires special care. We examined 159,041 Lovable projects and found many cases where personal and confidential information was accessible without logging in.
About Lovable
Lovable is a Swedish AI startup founded in Stockholm in 2023 by Anton Osika and Fabian Hedin. The platform turns plain-language descriptions into web applications and is designed to be accessible even to people with little or no programming experience.
Lovable grew out of Osika’s open-source project “GPT Engineer”, released in 2023. The project evolved into the GPT Engineer App before being renamed Lovable in late 2024 and launching publicly that November.
The Promise
Lovable can build impressively sophisticated web projects with AI, from static websites to full applications with user management and database connections. But as soon as an application handles confidential information, one question becomes crucial: Is that data protected against unauthorized access?
In its marketing, Lovable repeatedly emphasizes that no technical knowledge is needed to build projects. This can give the impression that the platform takes care of security too. Yet Lovable’s Terms & Conditions place responsibility for applications and data handling on the customers themselves.
That leaves a troubling gap between the promise of an easy-to-use, all-in-one service and the responsibilities customers are expected to take on.
Against this backdrop, we investigated how well sensitive data in Lovable applications is protected, in collaboration with Handelsblatt. Lina Knees reported on the findings on November 2, 2026 (in German).
Research Question
We wanted to find out whether sensitive data in Lovable applications was accessible without logging in. We looked separately at data sent when a website first loaded and additional content we checked by making further read-only requests with the same API key.
The result: We found many clear examples of sensitive data left unprotected.
Methodology
We focused on the database service Supabase. Its integration is relatively easy to analyze, and we believe it is the most widely used database service among Lovable projects. Lovable’s own database features, introduced in late 2025, are still less widely used. Similar misconfigurations may occur there too, but we did not examine them in this analysis.
Using datasets from the open web archive Common Crawl, we identified and analyzed 159,041 Lovable projects from December 2025 to September 2026. Of these, 34,545 used Supabase.
24,664 websites sent database records from Supabase as soon as they loaded, without requiring a login or any other interaction. That alone is not a security issue: the data may be intended for public display. But by making additional read-only requests, we were able to retrieve more database content. We found records containing email addresses in 10,560 tables across 7,403 of the 24,664 websites (30%).
Limitations
Email addresses can help identify records containing personal data, but they may also be public contact addresses or test data. At the same time, this approach misses personal records that contain no email address. These effects partly offset one another, though we could not establish to what extent. The 30% figure should therefore not be read as a confirmed data leak rate. It applies only to the 24,664 websites we examined more closely. Our sample, drawn from Common Crawl, is also not representative of all Lovable applications.
We retrieved only parts of the datasets rather than downloading them in full. The figures below reflect the records we actually downloaded; the affected datasets may contain more. We are not publishing details of how to access them, as doing so could make further access easier.
Data Leaks
We found many serious cases of exposed data, including:
- A German lifestyle forum exposed more than 80,000 profiles containing full names, email addresses, dates of birth, and gender information. More than 200,000 chat messages were also accessible.
- Several event organizers and restaurants exposed tens of thousands of records relating to ticket and food sales, with no login required. The records we inspected included email addresses, phone numbers, postal addresses, payment information, and ticket codes.
- A property management company exposed AI-generated summaries of internal emails containing sensitive information about tenants, property owners, and business partners.
- In a particularly serious case, a service provider exposed more than 1,000 records containing identity document details, including passport numbers, expiration dates, and nationalities. These highly sensitive identity details could be used for identity theft.
- Other accessible content included tens of thousands of contracts, invoices, personal photo albums, and internal WhatsApp communications.
- We also found numerous newsletter subscriptions, waiting lists, and contact and support inquiries. In one case, 280,000 emails sent to a provider’s customers were accessible.
Depending on what it contains, exposed data like this could be used for phishing, ticket fraud, or identity theft. We could not establish whether any of it had been misused.
Handelsblatt contacted Lovable with the findings of our investigation.
Implications
Our analysis shows that many Lovable applications leave even highly sensitive data unprotected, with potentially serious consequences for the people affected.
Access control mistakes can happen in any application, whether written by people or built with AI. With Lovable, database integrations follow recurring patterns, making it easier for attackers to look for similar misconfigurations across many applications. An approach that works against one app may then work against others with little extra effort. Users without a technical background also face a harder task spotting and preventing these flaws.
An easy-to-use development platform does not remove the need for an expert review of access permissions before an application handles confidential data.
Automated checks are no substitute for a professional security assessment.
Before an application handles real customer or business data, its access permissions, interfaces, and separation between user accounts should be reviewed. These checks should be repeated after significant changes. If the necessary expertise is not available in-house, bring in qualified support.
If you would like help securing your application, please get in touch.
Note: This research report presents a more recent analysis and updated figures compared with the Handelsblatt article published on November 2, 2026.
Talk to an expert
Get in touch for an informal conversation. We'll look at your current situation and work with you to find the right solutions.
Contact an expert
