Asian governments react to Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang’s exit
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
16:34:15 UTC
01:34:15 KST

Jensen Huang’s departure from Nvidia has drawn a range of responses across Asia, with some governments moving to confirm chip supply commitments, while others are monitoring the impact on local projects or addressing ongoing investigations.
On Sunday, November 8, Nvidia’s board launched an independent investigation after reports that Huang was targeted by a Chinese spy operation; Huang admitted to an affair with Alice Huang, a former executive at Singapore-based chip company Megaspeed.
Nvidia’s influence extends far beyond the United States. The chip giant has built close ties with companies worldwide, particularly in Asia, where many of its key partners play important roles in the global AI industry.
China
China’s foreign ministry rejected allegations on November 10 that a Chinese businesswoman linked to former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang was a spy, calling them groundless and a smear against Beijing. On Friday, a spokesperson reiterated opposition to export controls that disrupt global supply chains and stated the ministry was not aware of reports linking restricted Nvidia chips to a Shanghai data center associated with Alice Huang.
Huang’s exit has also raised uncertainty over more than $3 billion in Chinese AI computing projects reliant on Nvidia’s restricted processors, according to Reuters’ review of procurement notices, investment plans and corporate disclosures. Many of these chips are subject to U.S. export controls, making them difficult for Chinese companies to obtain.
Singapore
In Singapore, authorities seized the passport of Alice Huang, former CEO of Singapore-based cloud computing company Megaspeed, in June 2025 as part of an investigation into possible false declarations regarding server purchases, according to the Ministry of Home Affairs.
The Singapore ministry said police took her passport to ensure she remained available for questioning, as she was not based in Singapore. The investigation examined whether servers shipped from Singapore to Malaysia were later sent to other countries without proper declaration.
In February 2026, police found insufficient evidence to establish that Alice Huang had committed offenses related to the suspected false declarations, according to the ministry. However, authorities said she had knowingly provided inaccurate information about her role at Megaspeed. She was issued a warning and barred from re-entering Singapore. Her passport was returned that month. Singapore also said it received limited assistance from U.S. authorities despite repeated requests for information about U.S. server maker Supermicro. Singapore’s Ministry of Home Affairs said its investigation into Megaspeed and six other individuals remains ongoing. The ministry did not identify the six individuals or specify their alleged roles in the case.
South Korea
South Korea has close ties to Nvidia, with major chipmakers Samsung Electronics and SK Hynix playing important roles in supplying memory chips used in its AI processors. Following Huang’s exit, South Korea’s Science Ministry said Nvidia had reaffirmed its plan to supply 260,000 advanced GPUs to the country and to continue joint AI projects. Apart from that, South Korea’s government AI strategy council held an emergency meeting on Friday to assess the potential impact of Huang’s departure on the country’s AI industry, per a local media report.
Taiwan
Taiwan’s Economy Minister Kung Ming-shin said Huang’s departure was unlikely to significantly change the growth of the global AI industry, according to local media. Meanwhile, Taipei’s city government said Nvidia’s planned overseas headquarters in the city remained on track despite the leadership change. The company has met its contractual obligations so far, and officials said they would continue to support the project.
Japan
In Japan, the Industry Ministry said the government would closely monitor Nvidia following Huang’s resignation, describing the chipmaker as an important player in Japan’s AI semiconductor industry, Reuters reported. Deputy chief cabinet secretary Masanao Ozaki said it was too early to assess the impact on the global AI industry, while stressing Japan’s commitment to ensuring a stable supply of semiconductors.

Creating a self booting ZIP archive
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
16:29:05 UTC
01:29:05 KST

So, after a debugging session the other day, I was having some shower thoughts regarding early userspace and initrd.
Linux initramfs supports unpacking CPIO, a stupid, simple, standardized format. In the discussion on whether to support TAR instead, much emphasize has been put on the fact that CPIO is standardized and less fragmented.
Luckily, Linux (the kernel) includes its own tool to produce the specific variant of the standard that it happens to implement. The standard CPIO tool is possibly the most cursed archiver under the SUN.
In the old DOS/WinDOS world, ZIP was probably the most wide spread and well known archive format, being displaced to some extend by the rise of RAR during the 2000s.
Because of the design of the format, ZIP lends itself to all kinds of polyglot trickery. Way back when, self extracting ZIP archives where a thing, EXE/ZIP polyglots that can be run and unpack themselves, which is useful if you don't have an archiver installed.
I don't think I need to explain where I am going with this, here is the resulting ZIP archive I built:
Download boot.zip
It is a completely normal ZIP archive, containing its own source code needed to build itself from scratch:
$ unzip -t boot.zip
Archive:  boot.zip
    testing: boot/ZIPHEAD.COM         OK
    testing: boot/MESSAGE.TXT         OK
    testing: common/asm.h             OK
    testing: common/multiboot.h       OK
    testing: common/zip.h             OK
    testing: multiboot/console.c      OK
    testing: multiboot/console.h      OK
    testing: multiboot/multiboot.c    OK
    testing: multiboot/string.S       OK
    testing: setup/a20.S              OK
    testing: setup/disk.S             OK
    testing: setup/entry16.S          OK
    testing: setup/entry32.S          OK
    testing: setup/mbload.S           OK
    testing: setup/mem.S              OK
    testing: setup/setup.ld           OK
    testing: util/fixhdr.c            OK
    testing: util/zipglue.c           OK
    testing: util/zippack.c           OK
    testing: mk.sh                    OK
    testing: README.txt               OK
    testing: MESSAGE.txt              OK
    testing: LICENSE.txt              OK
No errors detected in compressed data of boot.zip.
    A particularly interesting file here is boot/ZIPHEAD.COM, that contains executable code that is run when booting the archive.
You need to fix the execute permissions of mk.sh after unpacking, and you will need a copy of Zlib installed for the tools. Running mk.sh from it's directory should leave you with a fresh copy of the bootable ZIP archive.
The simplest way to run it, is to just pass it to Qemu direct kernel boot:
$ qemu-system-i386 -kernel boot.zip
"That's easy" you might say, "Qemu supports Multiboot, that ZIPHEAD.COM is uncompressed, at the beginning of the archive and has a Multiboot header in it!"
Yes, that's all true! But this ZIP archive also brings it's own boot loader along. You can try putting it on a floppy and throw that in front of Qemu:
$ dd if=/dev/zero of=floppy.img count=2880
$ dd if=boot.zip of=floppy.img conv=notrunc
$ qemu-system-i386 -fda floppy.img
The first 512 bytes of the archive contain a valid boot sector that loads some more code from the second sector and then bootstraps itself into highmem with the A20 enabled and 32 bit protected mode set up.
You can also put it on a hard drive too, it detects what INT 13h drive it's been loaded from and dutifully queries the disk geometry.
If you care to try, you can also dump it on a real floppy and stuff it into a PC:
That's a Pentium MMX by the way, but I also tried an i486 DX2 and an Am386DX-40, just to be sure. My 486 had me hold my breath for a moment, it has a spectacularly slow floppy drive. The text on screen is from the file boot/MESSAGE.TXT
"But that's not special either" you might respond, "ZIPs are read from the back, you can just put anything you want in front, so you put a boot sector there!"
That is sadly not quite true anymore, I'll get to that. But for now, lets take a look at the first couple bytes of that archive together and we'll see the real party trick:
$ xxd -g 1 -l 128 boot.zip
00000000: 50 4b 03 04 0a 00 02 80 00 00 eb 44 e4 2e 41 0f  PK.........D..A.
00000010: 01 8f 0a 09 00 00 0a 09 00 00 10 00 00 00 62 6f  ..............bo
00000020: 6f 74 2f 5a 49 50 48 45 41 44 2e 43 4f 4d 00 90  ot/ZIPHEAD.COM..
00000030: 02 b0 ad 1b 03 00 01 00 fb 4f 51 e4 30 00 10 00  .........OQ.0...
00000040: 00 00 10 00 bd 72 10 00 bd 72 10 00 31 03 10 00  .....r...r..1...
00000050: fa fc 31 c0 89 c4 8e d8 8e c0 8e d0 ea 61 7c 00  ..1..........a|.
00000060: 00 bf 00 06 b9 00 02 f3 aa 88 16 98 06 be 0f 7d  ...............}
00000070: e8 8c 00 b8 00 f0 89 c1 50 9d 9c 58 a8 02 74 4a  ........P..X..tJ
The ASCII P stands for Phil ( not Picard ), as in Phil Katz, followed by 03h and 04h. What you are looking at, is a completely valid ZIP local file header for boot/ZIPHEAD.COM
Oh, and those other bytes there after the ZIP header: 02 b0 ad 1b, if you flip them around to human endian, it reads 1BADB002. That's the Multiboot magic :-)
The 16 bit real mode code starts right after the Multiboot header, at offset 00000050. If you are feeling a little confused, so is the file utility:
$ file boot.zip
boot.zip: DOS/MBR boot sector; partition 1 : ID=0xd, active 0xcd, start-CHS (0x7
2,19,7), end-CHS (0x388,254,6), startsector 2147916342, 243810273 sectors; parti
tion 2 : ID=0x60, active 0x9c, start-CHS (0x366,6,3), end-CHS (0x3f7,49,18), sta
rtsector 2298911798, 4154016209 sectors; partition 4 : ID=0xf8, active 0xe1, sta
rt-CHS (0x2,184,1), end-CHS (0x313,6,13), startsector 3277940231, 0 sectors
If you take a closer look at the first line of the hex dump, you might be able to spot the jump instruction that makes this little magic trick possible. Luckily, Phil Katz' initials are valid opcodes. The Multiboot entry point is much further into the file at offset 00000331.
Here's a picture you probably recognize from the Wikipedia article about the ZIP format . This here is a local copy, Wikimedia Commons hosts the original with attribution and licensing information .
Much like a TAR or CPIO archive, a ZIP file is an amalgamation of tightly packed files with a short header in front, specifying the full file path and other meta data:
typedef struct {
	uint32_t magic;
	uint16_t version;
	uint16_t flags;
	uint16_t method;
	uint16_t modtime;
	uint16_t moddate;
	uint32_t checksum;
	uint32_t compsize;
	uint32_t origsize;
	uint16_t namelength;
	uint16_t extralength;
	char name[namelength];
	uint8_t extra[extralength];
} __attribute__((packed)) zip_entry_t;
The timestamps are DOS datetime, the checksum is a CRC-32, there are no attributes or permission bits, we are thoroughly in DOS land. Directories are implied by the file paths.
Unlike TAR or CPIO, there is a central directory in the end, with a listing of all of the file headers and back references to the file in the archive. Unlike traditional file formats that have are started by a header, ZIP is terminated by a footer.
The reason for this is twofold:
That last part was especially important in the days when ZIP archives would be split over several floppies. To access a file, just scan the directory on the last floppy, then tell the user what floppy to insert to get the file.
By comparison, accessing a file in a tarball requires linearly scanning over the whole thing (which, on the other hand, is preferable for tape drives , where TAR originates).
ZIP archives are meant to be read from the back, the footer points to the central directory, the directory points to the files. In theory, the archiver shouldn't care if there happens to be a gap between two files, containing something else or if there is something else in the front of the archive, like a file header for a different file format.
The local file headers also supports up to 64k of "extra" stuff between the header and the file data, the footer has "comment" at the end, also up to 64k.
If you look at the footer for a second, you might spot an interesting design flaw:
typedef struct {
	uint32_t magic;
	uint16_t disknum;
	uint16_t diskstart;
	uint16_t num_dir_entries;
	uint16_t total_dir_entries;
	uint32_t dir_size;
	uint32_t dir_start;
	uint16_t comment_length;
	uint8_t comment[comment_length];
} __attribute__((packed)) zip_end_t;
it is variable size, an archiver needs to find it looking from the back, but the magic and the size are both stored before the variable sized comment field. Ask yourself this for a moment: how would you implement this in an archiver? Demanding that there is no comment? Reading the last 64k plus some change and scanning it? From which direction? What if there is another valid footer inside the comment field of the footer? Depending on how an implementation deals with this, a specifically crafted archive can appear to contain different files when viewed with different archivers.
Trying to make the format extensible ended up building a whole lot of foot guns in there. And we are not even talking about the plethora of extensions that have been tacked on to the official specification over the years.
Many modern implementations decided to save themselves a lot of headaches and just treat ZIP archives the same way as tarballs, linearly scanning them from the front instead. They expect the archive to start with a local header, skipping by the size MUST lead to the next entry or finally, the central directory. They reject any archives where this is not possible. This effectively prevents a lot of polyglot trick fuckery, but at the cost of rendering possibly valid archives unreadable.
For the self booting ZIP archive, the challenge thus becomes: the archive MUST start with a local file header, there cannot be any gaps, everything must be properly contained inside files.
Anybody who has ever looked at a DIY toy OS tutorial has probably stumbled across The Multiboot Specification , the possibly easiest and most beginner friendly way to get a program running on bare metal x86.
The multiboot spec originates in the GNU/FSF ecosystem and is implemented by boot loaders like GRUB or Qemu direct kernel boot. SYSLINUX supports it via a plugin. There is a draft for an over engineered 2nd version, that also supports MIPS, but this is of no concert to us here.
A Multiboot 1 compliant kernel simply contains the magic value 0x1BADB002 somewhere in the first in the first 8k. This is followed by flags and a checksum (all 3 must add up to zero). Depending on the flags, additional fields may follow.
The kernel itself can be an ELF file from which the loader extracts load address and entry point. The kernel is run in 32 bit protected mode with flat segments (base=0, limit=4G) and the A20 gate enabled.
Alternatively to a plain old ELF file, the kernel can also specify the memory layout manually (indicated via a flag), allowing flat binaries or other file formats to be loaded. The extended header looks like this:
typedef struct {
	uint32_t magic;
	uint32_t flags;
	uint32_t checksum;
	uint32_t hdr_addr;
	uint32_t load_addr;
	uint32_t load_end_addr;
	uint32_t bss_end_addr;
	uint32_t entry_point;
} mb_header_t;
The hdr_addr specifies the in-memory address of the header after loading the file, using this and the file offset where the header was found, the other addresses can be converted back to file offsets by the loader.
This way, the load_addr and load_end_addr are used to figure out what parts of the file to load where into memory. If the bss_end_addr is past that, the loader zero-fills it, before finally jumping to entry_point to run the kernel.
The Multiboot compliant loader also sets up a structure with system information (e.g. memory map, APM tables), kernel command line, etc… and passes the pointer to the kernel (via register EBX, magic value 0x2BADB002 in EAX).
Making a Multiboot bootable ZIP archive is trivial. Simply pack a flat binary with a Multiboot header as uncompressed file binary, make sure it is at the beginning and the addresses line up. An external tool can then fix up the headers, so that the whole archive is loaded.
For any practical applications, it would make sense to stop right here, but we're not here for "practical" or "sane".
On an IBM compatible machine, the BIOS iterates over the installed drives, loads the first sector from each (to location 0x7c00), until it finds one where the first sector ends with 0xaa55. It then jumps to the first byte.
Disks that are formatted with a filesystem, typically have a header in the beginning that starts with a jump instruction across the header into the bootstrap code that follows. The DOS MBR format opts to instead store the partition table at the end of the first sector and all the code in the front.
If we stuff a BIOS boot signature into the ZIP archive, at least parts of the ZIP local file header will be executed, the first few bytes are a mandated magic value and we cannot just put a jump instruction there.
The puzzle exercise now becomes, filling the header fields with values that make sense but also happen to be valid 8086 opcodes. At least, until we hit something that can be abused as a free-form field that we can stuff a jump into.
Let's take a look at that hex dump again:
I've tried to highlight the relevant header up to the jump instruction: 0xeb is a relative jump with a single byte immediate argument.
If we take a look again at the header structure, we can make out what fields those bytes correspond to:
typedef struct {
	uint32_t magic;
	uint16_t version;
	uint16_t flags;
	uint16_t method;
	uint16_t modtime;
	………
The timestamp is the first best place where we can put a jump instruction. The 0xeb relative jump works out to 8:30-ish in the morning, give or take the jump distance.
The two bytes in front of the modtime are compression method. We really, really want to have 0x0000 here, i.e. uncompressed, so that an archiver doesn't choke on the file data.
The first four bytes are the magic value of the format, we don't have any choice what to put here either, it has to be "PK\03\04". The version and flags fields are the only places where we have some creative freedom.
If we run the file through a dissassembler and take a look at the first few bytes, we'll end up with this:
$ objdump -D -m i8086 -b binary ./boot.zip
./boot.zip:     file format binary
Disassembly of section .data:
00000000 <.data>:
       0:       50                      push   %ax
       1:       4b                      dec    %bx
       2:       03 04                   add    (%si),%ax
       4:       0a 00                   or     (%bx,%si),%al
       6:       02 80 00 00             add    0x0(%bx,%si),%al
       a:       eb 44                   jmp    0x50
    This is AT&T syntax, source operands are left, destination on the right. We use GNU tools in this household, deal with it.
Anything that just fiddles around with a register doesn't really hurt us. Same goes for memory reads. We don't have memory protection, no matter what we access, there won't be any faults, the only memory mapped hardware around should be the frame buffer.
Memory writes are another story. Worst case, we could shoot up the interrupt vectors, the BIOS RAM areas (BDA, EBDA), or shadowed option ROMs.
The P for Phil also happens to also decode to push %ax. If the BIOS leaves us with a somewhat sane stack pointer, the push instruction should hopefully not mess anything up. It is part of the ZIP magic and thus unavoidable.
The version field is itself actually structured, containing a decimal encoded version number, followed by an OS ID byte. If we set it to 0x000a (stored as little endian), this decodes to version 1.0 on MS DOS.
0x0a is an orb, version 1.1 would be double byte sized, version 0.8 and 0.9 would be register direct and not touch the memory. An address of BX + SI is the result of a mod-reg-r/m field with all zeros.
We could try to code golf our way into consuming at least part of the flags as well, but low-valued, "harmless" opcodes that result in sane versions are sparse. Also, some version numbers change the meaning how an archiver is supposed to interpret the file. Also also, mod-reg-r/m combinations in the OS code field quickly end up as an undefined OS ID. Also also also, we have the method as well, that has to be 0x0000, which would result in a memory write if executed. We have to consume that somehow, rather than running it.
So for the flags, I came up with 0x8002 (stored as little endian). The lowest, set bit has a compression method dependent meaning, currently only defined for LZMA (14), deflate (8) and implode (6). For uncompressed (method 0) there is no defined meaning (the specification just ignores that part, it doesn't use nasty words like undefined). For deflate it would mean that maximum compression was used.
The MSB is specified as "reserved for use by PKWARE", whatever they chose to put there, if they ever chose anything at all. Most imporanly, the opcode 0x02 decodes to a single byte, indirect add, and in the mod-reg-r/m, the MSB tells the CPU to consume another 2 bytes of immediate DISPlacement. This is how we can get rid of the compression method.
At this point, we've made it! We reached the modtime field that we can just stuff a jump into.
Once we've made it past the header, we are in 16 bit real mode. We just need to go through all of the neccessary steps to set up the environment that the Multiboot code expects:
This might sound like a lot, but in the current version, the actual code boils down to 817 bytes. That includes all the headers (ZIP, multiboot), the rather bulky GDT. But it is still quite a lot and outside the scope of this post anyway, I might pick some interesting parts out of it for some follow-up posts (watch this space?).
The Multiboot entry code re-loads the GDT, but this time using the highmem location, and sets up a stack after the archive. The size is extracted from the Multiboot header. Knowing the start address, the C code can then iterate over itself to locate and access files in the archive.
Some linker script trickery is used to tie it all into one blob, but with the GDT and Multiboot header visible from both lomem and highmem code at different locations.
At this point, you might be inclined to actually read the source code in the ZIP archive. There are lots of comments trying to explain what is going on.
There's a README that points out the structure of the code with suggestions where to start reading. I decided to keep it readable rather than stuff it all into a single sector. Self-modifying trickery is kept to a minimum (to safe some space, the function to enter unreal mode modifies itself, the code is run twice, courtesy of a misdirected ret, entering protected mode the first time round, leaving it again and restoring the segment registers on the second run, before returning for real).
Code in the archive and this blog posts have been written with assistance from:
If you poke around in the code and have any suggestions for improvements, or otherwise do anything interesting with it, please let me know.
Have fun!

"Thanksgiving Slaw" Is the Best Veg Side on My Holiday Table

Move Over Salad — “Thanksgiving Slaw” Is the Only Vegetable I Serve on the Holiday
Like many, I’ve been to — and hosted — my fair share of Thanksgiving dinners. And every year, I eagerly await the big meal because it’s the one day I get to tuck into classic Thanksgiving fare. Roasted turkey! Green bean casserole! Herbed stuffing! But the one dish I tend to skip whenever it’s on the table? Salad. Don’t get me wrong; I love salad any other day of the year. However, come Thanksgiving, I want nothing to do with it. That’s because the salad generally on offer is either boring, wilted, or straight-up soggy. This year, I found the perfect solution to my wilty salad conundrum. Meet: Thanksgiving slaw! 
Get the recipe: Thanksgiving Slaw
What Makes Thanksgiving Slaw So Good
A successful Thanksgiving hinges on planning and timing. Salad ends up suffering the day-of because it’s tossed too far in advance and ends up sitting on the table while guests mingle and find (or fight over) their seats. This year, I decided to eschew all that and go with a slaw because 1) it benefits from being tossed in advance, and 2) it will stay crunchy throughout the meal.
Made with shredded green cabbage, toasty almonds, and sweet cranberries, Thanksgiving slaw is light, crunchy, and refreshing. The best part? With other dishes competing for your attention on the big day, this one makes the heavy lift so much easier. You can opt to make the dressing and shred the cabbage the day before, or toss it all together and let it sit for up to two hours before serving. And if you don’t want to bother with shredding any cabbage, store-bought coleslaw mix will work, too.
The dressing comes together just as easily. Simply whisk finely chopped red onion, vegetable oil, apple cider vinegar, maple syrup, Dijon mustard, and salt together. Letting it sit for 10 minutes allows the flavors to meld. It’s bright, tangy, and the perfect accompaniment to this fall-forward slaw. No wilty lettuce here.
Get the recipe: Thanksgiving Slaw

We have spent the last decade convincing ourselves that the cloud is a magical, weightless place. But it isn't. The cloud is a building. It has walls, cooling pipes, and power cables. And sometimes, that building gets too hot.
When a single AWS data center overheated last May, it didn't just take down some servers. It exposed the terrifying fragility of the modern internet. A broken air conditioner in Virginia meant Coinbase went dark, and a single typo in a Cloudflare script made thousands of websites invisible.
We traded the messy, decentralized web for the convenience of a few massive providers. Now, we are paying the price. This is a story about the hidden economics of the cloud, the illusion of resilience, and why the smartest engineers are quietly moving back to bare metal.

Formalizing US Tax Code (Using Lean)
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
15:41:20 UTC
00:41:20 KST

Formalizing the US tax code
Yesterday, we verified that known tax loopholes could be identified using a Lean search. Today, we set out to formalize the entire Internal Revenue Code to hunt for unknown loopholes (bugs).
Pointing a proof checker at statutes
Lean is a programming language whose type checker can also check mathematical proofs: if Lean accepts a proof, every step follows from the definitions, with nothing taken on trust. An earlier post discussed how far that kind of formalisation can reach in law,[1] and yesterday we used it on the retirement-account rules, where a blind search rediscovered the backdoor Roth.[2]
This work is part of global-law, a CC0 repository of versioned legislation and case law.[3] Our argument is that pointing AI and a proof checker at a versioned record of the law finds the contradictions and mechanical mistakes that people miss. In particular, we can catch mistakes in the text, and see what the rules allow, including what they allow by accident. To do so, we translated each section of the law into Lean, staying close to the statute’s own words, and tested it against the worked examples the IRS prints in its forms and instructions.
1. The tax law’s text has 138 drafting mistakes
The tax law is Title 26 of the US Code. Its official text is published as structured data by the Office of the Law Revision Counsel, the office of the House of Representatives that compiles the Code.[4] We used the release current to 3 November 2026.
A parser then reads every cross-reference, including the ones written in words (“paragraph (2) of subsection (b)”, “such subparagraph”, “sections 922 through 927”), which carry no hyperlink. Lean checks each one. Does the provision it points to exist? Is it still in force? (verifiable via our versioned history) The checks run as a test suite, which fails whenever a new release adds a fault the last one did not have.
The suite reports 257 findings, each checked by an AI agent against the official text and its editorial notes: 98 intended (rules for past periods, and provisions enacted but not yet in effect), 21 false alarms in the editors’ own hyperlinks, and 138 real faults:
Each of the 138 has a certificate: a short Lean statement that carries its evidence from the official XML and proves the fault. For a dead reference, the evidence is the full list of what does exist under the cited provision. Lean re-checks all 138 in about two and a half minutes.
Our record of the US Code is a git history, one commit per public law,[5] so each fault can be traced to the law that wrote it. For Title 26 the record reaches back to the 1994 edition.
This check is cheap to repeat. Run against each new release of the Code, it flags a new mistake as soon as it appears, and names the law that caused it.
2. Rewriting the income tax as code that can be checked
Next we wrote down what the law calculates: the 2025 federal income tax for one household, meaning the numbers on Form 1040, including the changes made by the One Big Beautiful Bill Act in July 2025.[6],[7] We did it in four parts:
- Income, down to adjusted gross income (AGI). AGI is total income minus a few items, such as IRA contributions and student-loan interest. Most other tax rules depend on it.
- Deductions: the standard deduction or itemized deductions, and the new deductions for tips, overtime, car-loan interest and people over 65.
- The tax itself, including the alternative minimum tax (AMT), a second calculation that stops high earners from cutting their tax too far with deductions.
- Credits, such as the child tax credit and the earned income tax credit (EITC), a credit for people on low and moderate wages.
We used AI to translate the statute into Lean. This is the lossy step, therefore we reinforce it with the following measures:
- Each part is tested against figures the IRS publishes (the Tax Table, the EITC table, the worksheets and the examples in its publications), and checked against three independent tax programs on 5,000 households (section 3).
- A second agent, which did not write that part, reviews it adversarially, looking for errors and for cheating, such as code written to pass the tests rather than to follow the statute.
What Lean ultimately adds is proofs that cover every input instead of a sample. For example, it proves that the SALT cap and the car-loan and overtime deductions never grow when income rises, at every income and for every filing status.
The comparison with other tax software helps us catch logical errors. That is how we caught two mistakes in our own model. It is also how we found bugs in the other tools that were logical rather than arithmetic: Tax-Calculator gives the EITC to separate filers without checking whether the spouses lived apart, and UsTaxes never attaches Schedule C to the 2025 return, so self-employment income disappears.
Writing the model turned up five things:
- The published Code mixes 2025 and 2026 law. In six places the consolidated Code already shows the 2025 Act’s amendments that start in 2026. Read without the effective-date notes, it gives the wrong 2025 tax: the AMT phase-out at 50% instead of 25%, a charity floor and a cap on itemized deductions that do not yet apply.
- The same Act rounds in opposite directions. The new tips and overtime deductions shrink by $100 for each full $1,000 of income over the limit, so one more dollar of income can cost $100 of deduction. The new car-loan deduction shrinks by $200 for each $1,000 “or portion thereof”, which rounds the other way, so it runs out $999 sooner than the round numbers suggest.
- A married couple can deduct more state and local tax by filing separately. The deduction for state and local taxes (SALT) is capped, and the cap shrinks as income rises: by 30 cents for each dollar over $500,000 on a joint return. On a separate return, the law halves the cap after shrinking it, so it shrinks by only 15 cents a dollar. A couple earning $300,000 each can deduct up to $25,000 on two separate returns, but only $10,000 on one joint return.
- The Tax Table is not the tax formula. Most people with taxable income under $100,000 look up their tax in the IRS Tax Table instead of working it out. The table differs from the formula in the law by between −$5 and +$6, and Lean proves that range for every income under $100,000 and every filing status.
- One dollar can cost $8,046. Some benefits switch off at a line instead of fading out. A family with three children can lose its whole EITC, up to $8,046, because its investment income is one dollar over $11,950. The saver’s credit, a credit for retirement saving, works the same way: a couple who each put $2,000 into retirement accounts gets $2,000 at an income of $47,500, and $800 at $47,501.
3. Testing it against tax software people already use
A model is only useful if it gives the right answers. To check ours, we built 5,000 sample households and worked out each one’s 2025 tax four ways: with our model, and with three tools built by other teams, independently of each other and of us:
- PolicyEngine-US, an open-source model that researchers and policymakers use to estimate how tax and benefit changes affect households;[8]
- Tax-Calculator, the Policy Simulation Library’s open-source model of the federal income tax;[9]
- UsTaxes, a free, open-source app for preparing your own tax return.[10]
Two thousand households were ordinary. The other 3,000 were deliberately awkward: couples filing separately, widows and widowers, students, people with tips or overtime, IRAs and health savings accounts, education and saver’s credits, and incomes over $1 million with large investment gains.
Our model and PolicyEngine-US agreed on every figure for 4,993 of the 5,000 households. The other seven differ by at most $11, all in the EITC: our model reads the IRS’s printed EITC table, while PolicyEngine-US uses the formula behind it.[11]
Tax-Calculator gave the EITC and the child and dependent care credit to married people filing separately without checking that the spouses lived apart, which the law requires. It also computed no AMT for one high-earning separate filer, where two other calculators computed $14,881.
UsTaxes had 14 bugs, each confirmed by recalculating an affected household. It drops self-employment income without a warning, because its 2025 return never attaches Schedule C, the form for business income. Its child-care credit never reaches the return. Its age test for the EITC always passes. And its self-employment tax counts a spouse’s wages against the cap on Social Security tax. Together, these bugs affect about one ordinary household in six.
The comparison caught two mistakes in our own model too. We had not counted self-employment profit toward the 20% deduction for small-business income. And we always took the larger of the standard and itemized deductions, although the law lets you choose to itemize a smaller amount, which can pay when it lowers the AMT. With that fixed, one household owed $2,070 less. Both mistakes showed up because two independent tools agreed with each other and not with us.
4. A search found the loopholes tax advisers already use
With a model that matches the reference software, we used it as we had used the retirement rules: as the oracle for a search that knows no tax planning. The first search tried ordinary choices for each of the 5,000 households, 195,787 returns in all:
- file separately, and which spouse holds which investments, pays which bills and claims which children;
- contribute to an IRA;
- earn a little less;
- realize more or less gain.
The second search used a multi-year family model, which adds the kiddie tax, trust taxation, Roth conversions, required withdrawals, gifts and death. It tried about 30,000 plans for four families over 34 to 47 years. Everything large that the two searches found is already in the planning literature:
As with the backdoor Roth, none of these was described to the search. It found them because the rules, written down plainly, permit them.
5. Novel loopholes we found
Three results did not turn up in practitioner sources, IRS guidance or tax-software forums.
Filing separately can cut tax on Social Security, even for a couple living together. The standard advice is that a couple living together should never file separately when one draws Social Security.[16] A separate filer who lived with their spouse gets no base amount at all. But taxable benefits are capped at the lesser of 85% of benefits or 85% of provisional income,[17],[18] and the search found couples for whom that cap makes separate returns cheaper. On a joint return the working spouse’s wage counts in provisional income, so a moderate wage already pushes the benefits to the 85% maximum. On a separate return the retiree’s benefits are measured against the retiree’s income alone. With $48,000 of benefits and a $60,000 wage, the couple owes $7,050 jointly and $5,568 separately, $1,482 less. A second reviewer recomputed it by hand and got the same answer. At a $100,000 wage the result flips, so the window is real but narrow.
Wages on one return, investments on the other. The 3.8% tax on investment income applies to the smaller of the investment income and the excess of income over $250,000 jointly, or $125,000 on a separate return.[19] On a joint return, wages use up that threshold first. On separate returns the investing spouse’s income excludes the other’s wages, and their gains start in their own 0% band. With about $200,000 of each, that saves $1,621. Every explainer gives the threshold. We found none that describes the split or where it pays.
Schedule 8812 pays more than the statute allows. This is a gap between the IRS form and the law, not a plan. The statute caps the refundable child credit per qualifying child, computed without the $500 credit for other dependents. The form caps the combined credit instead, so in the phase-out range, with no tax left to absorb it, it refunds the $500 credits in cash.[20],[21] Example: one child and one other dependent, $240,000 of income, no tax left. The statute gives $200, the form $700. Lean proves the form is never below the statute and agrees with it exactly when there are no other dependents.
6. Why one year of the tax code holds no big loopholes
The backdoor Roth is big because two provisions combine to remove an income limit entirely, and the money then compounds tax-free for decades. A single year of the individual income tax is full of cliffs and per-return thresholds, but each is bounded: the largest swing we found was a few thousand dollars. Over decades, the large gains come from moves that are already published. The one combination we could not find written up was paying the children in the same years as Roth conversions, which adds about 0.5%. Our guess is that anything Roth-sized and unpublished lives where value moves between entities and their owners: partnerships, S corporations and C corporations.
7. Most of this can now run automatically
“The long-term problem is how this kind of bug-hunting can be done routinely, e.g. as part of a test suite. Some approaches aren’t really automatable, because they require a lot of both human and AI agent work.”
What still needs a person is deciding what a finding means. Some cliffs are deliberate. Others are in the IRS’s forms, not in the law, whilst others come from the way the official text is compiled, yet:
- The text checks are routine. A new release is one download and one command, which fails on any fault the last release did not have, and git blame names the Act responsible.
- The model is a one-off cost. Once written and proved, it is a build: if a later change breaks a proved property, the build fails. When the law changes, only the changed provisions need rewriting.
- The comparison is routine once the input mappings are right. Getting them right was the hard part, and the same comparison found every mapping error.
Limits
- Coverage. The model covers the 2025 individual income tax for a household record. It leaves out the kiddie tax on the single-year return, 28% and §1250 gain, foreign income, passive activity rules, and most AMT preferences. The multi-year model holds 2025 law constant.
- Who checked what. The drafting triage and the reviews were done by AI agents against the official text, and have not been checked by a lawyer or CPA.
- Agreement is not truth. Where all four calculators agree they may share a misreading. The theorems and the IRS-example tests are the check on the model itself.
The code, the certificates, the comparison and the searches are in the lean-proofs folder of global-law.[3]
References

- Internal Revenue Service, Rev Proc 2024-40 <www.irs.gov/pub/irs-drop/rp-24-40.pdf>
- An Act to provide for reconciliation pursuant to title II of H. Con. Res. 14, Pub L 119-21 (4 July 2025) <www.congress.gov/bill/119th-congress/house-bill/1>

Formalizing US Tax Code (Using Lean)
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
15:41:20 UTC
00:41:20 KST

Formalizing the US tax code
Yesterday, we verified that known tax loopholes could be identified using a Lean search. Today, we set out to formalize the entire Internal Revenue Code to hunt for unknown loopholes (bugs).
Pointing a proof checker at statutes
Lean is a programming language whose type checker can also check mathematical proofs: if Lean accepts a proof, every step follows from the definitions, with nothing taken on trust. An earlier post discussed how far that kind of formalisation can reach in law,[1] and yesterday we used it on the retirement-account rules, where a blind search rediscovered the backdoor Roth.[2]
This work is part of global-law, a CC0 repository of versioned legislation and case law.[3] Our argument is that pointing AI and a proof checker at a versioned record of the law finds the contradictions and mechanical mistakes that people miss. In particular, we can catch mistakes in the text, and see what the rules allow, including what they allow by accident. To do so, we translated each section of the law into Lean, staying close to the statute’s own words, and tested it against the worked examples the IRS prints in its forms and instructions.
1. The tax law’s text has 138 drafting mistakes
The tax law is Title 26 of the US Code. Its official text is published as structured data by the Office of the Law Revision Counsel, the office of the House of Representatives that compiles the Code.[4] We used the release current to 3 November 2026.
A parser then reads every cross-reference, including the ones written in words (“paragraph (2) of subsection (b)”, “such subparagraph”, “sections 922 through 927”), which carry no hyperlink. Lean checks each one. Does the provision it points to exist? Is it still in force? (verifiable via our versioned history) The checks run as a test suite, which fails whenever a new release adds a fault the last one did not have.
The suite reports 257 findings, each checked by an AI agent against the official text and its editorial notes: 98 intended (rules for past periods, and provisions enacted but not yet in effect), 21 false alarms in the editors’ own hyperlinks, and 138 real faults:
Each of the 138 has a certificate: a short Lean statement that carries its evidence from the official XML and proves the fault. For a dead reference, the evidence is the full list of what does exist under the cited provision. Lean re-checks all 138 in about two and a half minutes.
Our record of the US Code is a git history, one commit per public law,[5] so each fault can be traced to the law that wrote it. For Title 26 the record reaches back to the 1994 edition.
This check is cheap to repeat. Run against each new release of the Code, it flags a new mistake as soon as it appears, and names the law that caused it.
2. Rewriting the income tax as code that can be checked
Next we wrote down what the law calculates: the 2025 federal income tax for one household, meaning the numbers on Form 1040, including the changes made by the One Big Beautiful Bill Act in July 2025.[6],[7] We did it in four parts:
- Income, down to adjusted gross income (AGI). AGI is total income minus a few items, such as IRA contributions and student-loan interest. Most other tax rules depend on it.
- Deductions: the standard deduction or itemized deductions, and the new deductions for tips, overtime, car-loan interest and people over 65.
- The tax itself, including the alternative minimum tax (AMT), a second calculation that stops high earners from cutting their tax too far with deductions.
- Credits, such as the child tax credit and the earned income tax credit (EITC), a credit for people on low and moderate wages.
We used AI to translate the statute into Lean. This is the lossy step, therefore we reinforce it with the following measures:
- Each part is tested against figures the IRS publishes (the Tax Table, the EITC table, the worksheets and the examples in its publications), and checked against three independent tax programs on 5,000 households (section 3).
- A second agent, which did not write that part, reviews it adversarially, looking for errors and for cheating, such as code written to pass the tests rather than to follow the statute.
What Lean ultimately adds is proofs that cover every input instead of a sample. For example, it proves that the SALT cap and the car-loan and overtime deductions never grow when income rises, at every income and for every filing status.
The comparison with other tax software helps us catch logical errors. That is how we caught two mistakes in our own model. It is also how we found bugs in the other tools that were logical rather than arithmetic: Tax-Calculator gives the EITC to separate filers without checking whether the spouses lived apart, and UsTaxes never attaches Schedule C to the 2025 return, so self-employment income disappears.
Writing the model turned up five things:
- The published Code mixes 2025 and 2026 law. In six places the consolidated Code already shows the 2025 Act’s amendments that start in 2026. Read without the effective-date notes, it gives the wrong 2025 tax: the AMT phase-out at 50% instead of 25%, a charity floor and a cap on itemized deductions that do not yet apply.
- The same Act rounds in opposite directions. The new tips and overtime deductions shrink by $100 for each full $1,000 of income over the limit, so one more dollar of income can cost $100 of deduction. The new car-loan deduction shrinks by $200 for each $1,000 “or portion thereof”, which rounds the other way, so it runs out $999 sooner than the round numbers suggest.
- A married couple can deduct more state and local tax by filing separately. The deduction for state and local taxes (SALT) is capped, and the cap shrinks as income rises: by 30 cents for each dollar over $500,000 on a joint return. On a separate return, the law halves the cap after shrinking it, so it shrinks by only 15 cents a dollar. A couple earning $300,000 each can deduct up to $25,000 on two separate returns, but only $10,000 on one joint return.
- The Tax Table is not the tax formula. Most people with taxable income under $100,000 look up their tax in the IRS Tax Table instead of working it out. The table differs from the formula in the law by between −$5 and +$6, and Lean proves that range for every income under $100,000 and every filing status.
- One dollar can cost $8,046. Some benefits switch off at a line instead of fading out. A family with three children can lose its whole EITC, up to $8,046, because its investment income is one dollar over $11,950. The saver’s credit, a credit for retirement saving, works the same way: a couple who each put $2,000 into retirement accounts gets $2,000 at an income of $47,500, and $800 at $47,501.
3. Testing it against tax software people already use
A model is only useful if it gives the right answers. To check ours, we built 5,000 sample households and worked out each one’s 2025 tax four ways: with our model, and with three tools built by other teams, independently of each other and of us:
- PolicyEngine-US, an open-source model that researchers and policymakers use to estimate how tax and benefit changes affect households;[8]
- Tax-Calculator, the Policy Simulation Library’s open-source model of the federal income tax;[9]
- UsTaxes, a free, open-source app for preparing your own tax return.[10]
Two thousand households were ordinary. The other 3,000 were deliberately awkward: couples filing separately, widows and widowers, students, people with tips or overtime, IRAs and health savings accounts, education and saver’s credits, and incomes over $1 million with large investment gains.
Our model and PolicyEngine-US agreed on every figure for 4,993 of the 5,000 households. The other seven differ by at most $11, all in the EITC: our model reads the IRS’s printed EITC table, while PolicyEngine-US uses the formula behind it.[11]
Tax-Calculator gave the EITC and the child and dependent care credit to married people filing separately without checking that the spouses lived apart, which the law requires. It also computed no AMT for one high-earning separate filer, where two other calculators computed $14,881.
UsTaxes had 14 bugs, each confirmed by recalculating an affected household. It drops self-employment income without a warning, because its 2025 return never attaches Schedule C, the form for business income. Its child-care credit never reaches the return. Its age test for the EITC always passes. And its self-employment tax counts a spouse’s wages against the cap on Social Security tax. Together, these bugs affect about one ordinary household in six.
The comparison caught two mistakes in our own model too. We had not counted self-employment profit toward the 20% deduction for small-business income. And we always took the larger of the standard and itemized deductions, although the law lets you choose to itemize a smaller amount, which can pay when it lowers the AMT. With that fixed, one household owed $2,070 less. Both mistakes showed up because two independent tools agreed with each other and not with us.
4. A search found the loopholes tax advisers already use
With a model that matches the reference software, we used it as we had used the retirement rules: as the oracle for a search that knows no tax planning. The first search tried ordinary choices for each of the 5,000 households, 195,787 returns in all:
- file separately, and which spouse holds which investments, pays which bills and claims which children;
- contribute to an IRA;
- earn a little less;
- realize more or less gain.
The second search used a multi-year family model, which adds the kiddie tax, trust taxation, Roth conversions, required withdrawals, gifts and death. It tried about 30,000 plans for four families over 34 to 47 years. Everything large that the two searches found is already in the planning literature:
As with the backdoor Roth, none of these was described to the search. It found them because the rules, written down plainly, permit them.
5. Novel loopholes we found
Three results did not turn up in practitioner sources, IRS guidance or tax-software forums.
Filing separately can cut tax on Social Security, even for a couple living together. The standard advice is that a couple living together should never file separately when one draws Social Security.[16] A separate filer who lived with their spouse gets no base amount at all. But taxable benefits are capped at the lesser of 85% of benefits or 85% of provisional income,[17],[18] and the search found couples for whom that cap makes separate returns cheaper. On a joint return the working spouse’s wage counts in provisional income, so a moderate wage already pushes the benefits to the 85% maximum. On a separate return the retiree’s benefits are measured against the retiree’s income alone. With $48,000 of benefits and a $60,000 wage, the couple owes $7,050 jointly and $5,568 separately, $1,482 less. A second reviewer recomputed it by hand and got the same answer. At a $100,000 wage the result flips, so the window is real but narrow.
Wages on one return, investments on the other. The 3.8% tax on investment income applies to the smaller of the investment income and the excess of income over $250,000 jointly, or $125,000 on a separate return.[19] On a joint return, wages use up that threshold first. On separate returns the investing spouse’s income excludes the other’s wages, and their gains start in their own 0% band. With about $200,000 of each, that saves $1,621. Every explainer gives the threshold. We found none that describes the split or where it pays.
Schedule 8812 pays more than the statute allows. This is a gap between the IRS form and the law, not a plan. The statute caps the refundable child credit per qualifying child, computed without the $500 credit for other dependents. The form caps the combined credit instead, so in the phase-out range, with no tax left to absorb it, it refunds the $500 credits in cash.[20],[21] Example: one child and one other dependent, $240,000 of income, no tax left. The statute gives $200, the form $700. Lean proves the form is never below the statute and agrees with it exactly when there are no other dependents.
6. Why one year of the tax code holds no big loopholes
The backdoor Roth is big because two provisions combine to remove an income limit entirely, and the money then compounds tax-free for decades. A single year of the individual income tax is full of cliffs and per-return thresholds, but each is bounded: the largest swing we found was a few thousand dollars. Over decades, the large gains come from moves that are already published. The one combination we could not find written up was paying the children in the same years as Roth conversions, which adds about 0.5%. Our guess is that anything Roth-sized and unpublished lives where value moves between entities and their owners: partnerships, S corporations and C corporations.
7. Most of this can now run automatically
“The long-term problem is how this kind of bug-hunting can be done routinely, e.g. as part of a test suite. Some approaches aren’t really automatable, because they require a lot of both human and AI agent work.”
What still needs a person is deciding what a finding means. Some cliffs are deliberate. Others are in the IRS’s forms, not in the law, whilst others come from the way the official text is compiled, yet:
- The text checks are routine. A new release is one download and one command, which fails on any fault the last release did not have, and git blame names the Act responsible.
- The model is a one-off cost. Once written and proved, it is a build: if a later change breaks a proved property, the build fails. When the law changes, only the changed provisions need rewriting.
- The comparison is routine once the input mappings are right. Getting them right was the hard part, and the same comparison found every mapping error.
Limits
- Coverage. The model covers the 2025 individual income tax for a household record. It leaves out the kiddie tax on the single-year return, 28% and §1250 gain, foreign income, passive activity rules, and most AMT preferences. The multi-year model holds 2025 law constant.
- Who checked what. The drafting triage and the reviews were done by AI agents against the official text, and have not been checked by a lawyer or CPA.
- Agreement is not truth. Where all four calculators agree they may share a misreading. The theorems and the IRS-example tests are the check on the model itself.
The code, the certificates, the comparison and the searches are in the lean-proofs folder of global-law.[3]

PyPI's redesigned project pages with clearer security signals are now live
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
15:40:52 UTC
00:40:52 KST

PyPI's redesigned project pages with clearer security signals are now live
In July, we announced a series of planned updates to the PyPI user interface. Shaped by user research with members of the Python community, the updates aim to improve how we surface security signals and display package details.
We're pleased to share that this work is now complete and live on PyPI.
Context¶
With the rapid growth of the Python ecosystem, PyPI has become an increasingly attractive target for malicious actors. In response, the PyPI team has delivered many security features while taking a more active role in helping PyPI users assess and understand package security.
Digital attestations, introduced in 2024, are an example of this effort, providing a verifiable record of a release file's provenance (where it came from). However, provenance and attestations are still unfamiliar concepts for many PyPI users, and PyPI's interface did little to communicate their meaning, importance, or limitations.
To address this, PyPI partnered with Kabu Creative to bring clearer security signals to the PyPI UI, implementing the recommendations developed in the OpenSSF Attestations Style Guide.
Here's a look at what's changed, what it means for you, and what actions you can take.
The New Security Tab¶
The new security tab communicates release provenance states and displays per-file attestations, giving you a dedicated place to verify where the release files came from.
Provenance States¶
PyPI calculates each release's provenance state by checking whether each release file has an attestation. Typically, a release will be in one of two states:
Full Provenance¶
This means that 100% of the release files have attestations, carrying either or both of the supported attestation types:
PyPI Publish attestations confirm the file was published by a PyPI Trusted Publisher:
SLSA Build Provenance attestations link the file back to its exact source commit, build platform, and instructions:
You can review attestation metadata directly in the UI, or click on the transparency log link for more information.
No Provenance¶
"No provenance" simply means that no file in the release has an attestation. This is typical for older releases or projects that have not yet configured Trusted Publishing. No provenance does not necessarily indicate that a release or release file is malicious; it instead means that the file origin cannot be cryptographically verified.
When there is no provenance, you should rely on surrounding trust signals to assess security, such as maintainer history, release patterns, and repository activity. If you maintain the package, consider using Trusted Publishing with a publisher that generates attestations for your next release.
Provenance Warnings¶
Provenance warnings are designed to help you identify when a file may not have come from the project's maintainers, for example, if an attacker has taken over an account or a publishing workflow.
When provenance metadata looks unusual or conflicts with a project's history, PyPI surfaces a prominent warning. Many warnings have legitimate explanations, but each one is worth checking before you install.
Incomplete Provenance¶
This means that some files carry attestations while others do not (e.g., wheels were published via Trusted Publishing, but the source tarball was uploaded using an API token).
Exercise caution if you see this warning. Check release notes for build issues or ask in the project's issue tracker before installing.
Inconsistent Provenance¶
This warning indicates that files within the same release originate from different build systems or source repositories.
Check the attestation metadata to confirm whether all files came from sources controlled by the maintainers.
Change of Provenance¶
This warning tells you that the release files originated from a different repository or workflow than a previous release. While often legitimate (such as an organization name change or repository migration), it can also indicate a compromised pipeline or account takeover.
If you see this warning, check the project release notes or official channels to confirm that an infrastructure migration was planned.
Loss of Provenance¶
This means that a project that previously published with attestations has changed to uploading release files without them.
Check whether the maintainers intentionally changed their publishing process, for example by switching back to API tokens, or by changing their publishing configuration so that it no longer generates attestations. If this is unexpected, treat the release with skepticism until verified.
To learn more about provenance and attestations, including provenance warnings, see our user documentation.
Redesigned Project Detail Page¶
To support these changes, the project detail page has also been updated:
- Provenance warnings now appear at the top of the page, alongside status banners for yanked releases, archived projects, and pre-releases. If a project or release is quarantined, only the quarantine banner is shown, as it takes precedence over all other warnings and banners.
- When a release has full provenance or provenance warnings, its provenance state is displayed in the sidebar, linking through to the security tab for more information.
- Metadata is now ordered by utility and labeled as "PyPI data" or "Data verified by PyPI", replacing the old design where data was grouped and ordered by verification status.
Release Files and Release History¶
The redesigned release files tab helps you find the right file by searching and filtering long lists. You can also view the provenance state of each file within the table, and quickly identify late files, which are marked with an orange warning icon.
Late file warnings are shown when one or more files were uploaded more than 14 days after the initial release timestamp. These warnings only appear on releases prior to July 2026, when a policy was introduced to reject new files after 14 days.
The release history tab now also provides more details about each release, exposing file count, provenance state, and release status (i.e., quarantined, yanked, archived, or pre-release).
Getting Help¶
Alongside the interface updates, PyPI's user documentation has been extended to help you better navigate package security. Read our package security guide to learn about:
Looking Ahead & Acknowledgments¶
The new security tab gives PyPI a dedicated home for future attestation types and security notices. To help the wider open source ecosystem benefit from this work, we've also published a case study in the OpenSSF Attestations Style Guide detailing the design patterns and warnings developed during this project.
This initiative was funded by the Open Source Security Foundation and implemented by Kabu Creative. We would like to thank Dustin Ingram, Zach Steindler, and the members of the OpenSSF Securing Software Repositories Working Group for championing this effort. Special thanks to Mike Fiedler, Jacob Coffee, and members of the PyPI team for their thoughtful design feedback and code reviews, @cofiem for their contributions to the new release files tab, and our user interview participants and community testers for helping shape the final design.
Finally, we would like to thank the OpenSSF for its funding and continued partnership in securing the Python packaging ecosystem.

PyPI's redesigned project pages with clearer security signals are now live
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
15:40:52 UTC
00:40:52 KST

PyPI's redesigned project pages with clearer security signals are now live
In July, we announced a series of planned updates to the PyPI user interface. Shaped by user research with members of the Python community, the updates aim to improve how we surface security signals and display package details.
We're pleased to share that this work is now complete and live on PyPI.
Context¶
With the rapid growth of the Python ecosystem, PyPI has become an increasingly attractive target for malicious actors. In response, the PyPI team has delivered many security features while taking a more active role in helping PyPI users assess and understand package security.
Digital attestations, introduced in 2024, are an example of this effort, providing a verifiable record of a release file's provenance (where it came from). However, provenance and attestations are still unfamiliar concepts for many PyPI users, and PyPI's interface did little to communicate their meaning, importance, or limitations.
To address this, PyPI partnered with Kabu Creative to bring clearer security signals to the PyPI UI, implementing the recommendations developed in the OpenSSF Attestations Style Guide.
Here's a look at what's changed, what it means for you, and what actions you can take.
The New Security Tab¶
The new security tab communicates release provenance states and displays per-file attestations, giving you a dedicated place to verify where the release files came from.
Provenance States¶
PyPI calculates each release's provenance state by checking whether each release file has an attestation. Typically, a release will be in one of two states:
Full Provenance¶
This means that 100% of the release files have attestations, carrying either or both of the supported attestation types:
PyPI Publish attestations confirm the file was published by a PyPI Trusted Publisher:
SLSA Build Provenance attestations link the file back to its exact source commit, build platform, and instructions:
You can review attestation metadata directly in the UI, or click on the transparency log link for more information.
No Provenance¶
"No provenance" simply means that no file in the release has an attestation. This is typical for older releases or projects that have not yet configured Trusted Publishing. No provenance does not necessarily indicate that a release or release file is malicious; it instead means that the file origin cannot be cryptographically verified.
When there is no provenance, you should rely on surrounding trust signals to assess security, such as maintainer history, release patterns, and repository activity. If you maintain the package, consider using Trusted Publishing with a publisher that generates attestations for your next release.
Provenance Warnings¶
Provenance warnings are designed to help you identify when a file may not have come from the project's maintainers, for example, if an attacker has taken over an account or a publishing workflow.
When provenance metadata looks unusual or conflicts with a project's history, PyPI surfaces a prominent warning. Many warnings have legitimate explanations, but each one is worth checking before you install.
Incomplete Provenance¶
This means that some files carry attestations while others do not (e.g., wheels were published via Trusted Publishing, but the source tarball was uploaded using an API token).
Exercise caution if you see this warning. Check release notes for build issues or ask in the project's issue tracker before installing.
Inconsistent Provenance¶
This warning indicates that files within the same release originate from different build systems or source repositories.
Check the attestation metadata to confirm whether all files came from sources controlled by the maintainers.
Change of Provenance¶
This warning tells you that the release files originated from a different repository or workflow than a previous release. While often legitimate (such as an organization name change or repository migration), it can also indicate a compromised pipeline or account takeover.
If you see this warning, check the project release notes or official channels to confirm that an infrastructure migration was planned.
Loss of Provenance¶
This means that a project that previously published with attestations has changed to uploading release files without them.
Check whether the maintainers intentionally changed their publishing process, for example by switching back to API tokens, or by changing their publishing configuration so that it no longer generates attestations. If this is unexpected, treat the release with skepticism until verified.
To learn more about provenance and attestations, including provenance warnings, see our user documentation.
Redesigned Project Detail Page¶
To support these changes, the project detail page has also been updated:
- Provenance warnings now appear at the top of the page, alongside status banners for yanked releases, archived projects, and pre-releases. If a project or release is quarantined, only the quarantine banner is shown, as it takes precedence over all other warnings and banners.
- When a release has full provenance or provenance warnings, its provenance state is displayed in the sidebar, linking through to the security tab for more information.
- Metadata is now ordered by utility and labeled as "PyPI data" or "Data verified by PyPI", replacing the old design where data was grouped and ordered by verification status.
Release Files and Release History¶
The redesigned release files tab helps you find the right file by searching and filtering long lists. You can also view the provenance state of each file within the table, and quickly identify late files, which are marked with an orange warning icon.
Late file warnings are shown when one or more files were uploaded more than 14 days after the initial release timestamp. These warnings only appear on releases prior to July 2026, when a policy was introduced to reject new files after 14 days.
The release history tab now also provides more details about each release, exposing file count, provenance state, and release status (i.e., quarantined, yanked, archived, or pre-release).
Getting Help¶
Alongside the interface updates, PyPI's user documentation has been extended to help you better navigate package security. Read our package security guide to learn about:
Looking Ahead & Acknowledgments¶
The new security tab gives PyPI a dedicated home for future attestation types and security notices. To help the wider open source ecosystem benefit from this work, we've also published a case study in the OpenSSF Attestations Style Guide detailing the design patterns and warnings developed during this project.
This initiative was funded by the Open Source Security Foundation and implemented by Kabu Creative. We would like to thank Dustin Ingram, Zach Steindler, and the members of the OpenSSF Securing Software Repositories Working Group for championing this effort. Special thanks to Mike Fiedler, Jacob Coffee, and members of the PyPI team for their thoughtful design feedback and code reviews, @cofiem for their contributions to the new release files tab, and our user interview participants and community testers for helping shape the final design.
Finally, we would like to thank the OpenSSF for its funding and continued partnership in securing the Python packaging ecosystem.

Show HN: DeepSeek V3 in ~200 lines of pure Jax
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
15:04:15 UTC
00:04:15 KST

The architecture from the DeepSeek-V3 technical report in 190 lines of pure JAX, pretrained from scratch on a MacBook Air on public-domain detective fiction.
No Flax, no Equinox, no PyTorch. Params are plain dicts of arrays and every part of the model is a function you can read top to bottom in deepseek_v3.py.
Read the write-up: DeepSeek V3 in 190 lines of pure JAX, with diagrams, the code explained piece by piece, and the results.
One run of the default config on a MacBook Air M5 (24 GB):
With balancing on and off, validation loss at step 2000 was the same (3.921 vs 3.923). The bias evens out the experts without costing anything, which is the paper's claim.
Its own words, from the prompt "The fog came in off the":
The fog came in off the bay, and the great trees were spreading and the stars were shining.
This is an architecture demo, not a strong language model. See the write-up for what worked, what didn't, and what's missing.
The v1.0 release has the exact checkpoint and data from the published run. After installing (step 1):
mkdir -p data runs/main
curl -L -o runs/main/ckpt.pkl      https://github.com/Vaibhaav-Tiwari/deepseek-v3-jax/releases/download/v1.0/ckpt.pkl          # 138 MB
curl -L -o data/tokenizer.json     https://github.com/Vaibhaav-Tiwari/deepseek-v3-jax/releases/download/v1.0/tokenizer.json
.venv/bin/python sample.py --prompt "The fog came in off the" --num 3
The checkpoint is a Python pickle, which can run code when loaded. Only load checkpoints from sources you trust.
You need an Apple Silicon Mac, Python 3.12 and uv. The published run used 24 GB of memory. No Mac? See Running on CPU.
1. Install
git clone https://github.com/Vaibhaav-Tiwari/deepseek-v3-jax.git && cd deepseek-v3-jax
uv venv -p 3.12 .venv
uv pip install --python .venv/bin/python -r requirements.txt
Every version in requirements.txt is pinned. jax-metal 0.1.1 only works with jax==0.4.34, so don't upgrade JAX.
2. Check your setup
JAX_PLATFORMS=cpu .venv/bin/pytest tests.py -q    # 39 passed
.venv/bin/python smoke_metal.py                   # optional: what jax-metal supports on your Mac
In smoke_metal.py, "grad through 2-axis contraction" fails on jax-metal 0.1.1. That's expected, and the model is written to avoid it.
3. Build the dataset
.venv/bin/python prepare_data.py
This downloads the 207 books in sources.txt (~120 MB, about 7 minutes), cleans them, trains an 8,192-token BPE tokenizer and writes data/train.bin, data/val.bin and data/tokenizer.json. It should print:
train: 211 docs, 83.4M chars, 21.69M tokens
val: 4 docs, 1.1M chars, 0.30M tokens
The published run's data had these hashes:
shasum -a 256 data/train.bin data/val.bin data/tokenizer.jsond37aca97e707bb81b6e4d266cc1adfd1f3e3556457c6eb724e495118a734e0bb  data/train.bin
b7ac285b115e6793b5d36aa5b4bc6e81a5fccfc7af0908a306d3828ebca9c31c  data/val.bin
c5a142b4006a967c880dda44ad792719d53c16d947da3b6c2332d6132d9967cc  data/tokenizer.json
You will most likely get different hashes. The Hammett books are pinned to exact Standard Ebooks commits and come out identical, but Project Gutenberg files can't be pinned, and Gutenberg has since re-issued 28 of the 203 books with small edits (mostly accents stripped, like "José" → "Jose"). The token counts come out the same, so training should behave the same.
To train on the exact same bytes, skip prepare_data.py and download the published data instead:
mkdir -p data
for f in train.bin val.bin tokenizer.json; do curl -L -o data/$f https://github.com/Vaibhaav-Tiwari/deepseek-v3-jax/releases/download/v1.0/$f; done
shasum -a 256 data/*.bin data/tokenizer.json    # should match the hashes above
4. Train
.venv/bin/python train.py                                         # the main run: 12k steps, ~2.6 h
.venv/bin/python train.py --steps 2000 --out runs/bias_on         # balancing ablation, with the bias
.venv/bin/python train.py --steps 2000 --out runs/bias_off --no-bias   # ...and without it
Each run writes metrics.jsonl (loss, validation loss, per-expert load, biases, tokens/s) and ckpt.pkl (saved at every evaluation) into its --out folder, which defaults to runs/main. Seeds are fixed, but GPU math on Metal isn't bit-for-bit deterministic, so expect your numbers to land close to the ones above rather than match them exactly.
5. Generate
.venv/bin/python sample.py --prompt "The fog came in off the" --num 3
Defaults: temperature 0.8, top-k 50, 300 new tokens per sample (--temp, --top-k, --n).
6. Make the figures
.venv/bin/python plots.py                    # needs runs/main, runs/bias_on, runs/bias_off
JAX_PLATFORMS=cpu .venv/bin/python charts.py # the rest of the write-up's charts
Both write into post/figures/.
To redraw the figures without training, download the logs from the release: metrics-main.jsonl, metrics-bias_on.jsonl and metrics-bias_off.jsonl go into runs/main/, runs/bias_on/ and runs/bias_off/ as metrics.jsonl. charts.py also needs the checkpoint and the data.
Everything runs without jax-metal. It's much slower, so use the smaller config:
JAX_PLATFORMS=cpu .venv/bin/python train.py --config small
--config small (4 layers, 8 experts, top-2, 128-token context) is meant for 8–16 GB Macs too. It has only been checked for a couple of steps, not trained to the end.
"190 lines" counts the non-blank, non-comment lines of deepseek_v3.py after its docstring:
tail -n +5 deepseek_v3.py | grep -cvE '^\s*(#|$)'    # 190
- Dense MoE compute. Every expert runs on every token and the unpicked ones are zeroed. The routing math is exact, but there's no sparse dispatch or expert parallelism.
- No weight absorption in MLA. The latent is decompressed explicitly, which is easier to read and slower at inference.
- Not included: FP8 training, DualPipe, node-limited routing, YaRN context extension, and any post-training.
- Scale. 36.2M parameters vs 671B, 6 layers vs 61, a 256-token context vs 128K.
All training text is public domain in the United States: Dashiell Hammett's Red Harvest, The Dain Curse, The Maltese Falcon and Continental Op stories (CC0 editions from Standard Ebooks), plus about 200 detective and crime novels from Project Gutenberg. Every source is listed in sources.txt. Public-domain status outside the US may differ.
DeepSeek-AI, DeepSeek-V3 Technical Report, 2024.
Code: MIT. The training text is public domain in the US (see above).
