Chicken Normandy
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
15:00:49 UTC
00:00:49 KST

Chicken Normandy
A cozy French chicken dinner with an irresistibly creamy apple and Dijon mustard sauce.
Serves4 to 6
Prep15 minutes
Cook45 minutes to 55 minutes
Why You’ll Love Chicken Normandy
- It comes together in just one pan. Sear juicy chicken thighs, sauté apple slices, and simmer a creamy sauce in a single skillet. It makes dinner prep and cleanup a breeze.
- The simple pan sauce is irresistible. The secret to a delicious pan sauce is capturing every morsel of flavor along the way. Lift the golden fond after browning the chicken with applejack, simmer with apple cider and Dijon mustard, then finish with tangy crème fraîche.
It’s that time of year that I’m finding excuses to sneak apples into every meal of the day. German apple pancakes stand in for a classic Dutch baby pancake, and strawberry spinach salad takes a backseat to one that’s made with apples and walnuts. There are countless ways to enjoy the crisp, sweet flavor of apples for dinner, but chicken Normandy is a cozy French chicken dinner that has become one of my favorites.
Chicken Normandy is a braised chicken dish made with fresh apples, apple cider, and apple brandy, each adding its own unique fruit flavor to the dish. The combination of apples with fresh thyme, zippy Dijon mustard, and tangy crème fraîche creates a creamy sauce that’s sweet, savory, and irresistibly delicious. Still, that’s not where the wins for this dinner stop. Chicken Normandy is made in a single skillet, so this dinner is a breeze to prepare. Just crisp the bacon and sear the chicken, make the sauce, and simmer until the chicken is juicy and apples are tender. Don’t forget to serve with a hunk of crusty bread to soak up every last drop of the sauce.
Straight from Our Recipe Tester
“This was everything I want in a Sunday dinner. It was unfussy to prepare, but the flavor is both deep and nuanced, savory, hearty, and warming. A perfect poem of a fall meal. I will definitely be making this again. I served this with potatoes, but it would also be great with a loaf of crusty bread to soak up the sauce.” —Andrea, October, 2026
Why You Should Trust Me
I studied culinary science to learn the hows and whys behind ingredients and cooking techniques. I’ve worked in test kitchens for the last 18 years developing and testing recipes for The Kitchn, cookbooks, and television. I love creating smart and delicious recipes that are worth repeating.
Key Ingredients in Chicken Normandy
- Chicken thighs: Bone-in, skin-on chicken thighs are juicy and flavorful. Choose thighs that are about the same size so they cook at the same rate.
- Applejack: This blended apple brandy is made from hard cider. Laird’s is a readily available brand of applejack, but Calvados or Cognac can be used instead.
- Apple cider: Apple cider has a complex, fresh apple flavor. If apple cider isn’t available, unfiltered apple juice makes an excellent substitute.
- Crème fraîche: Adds a creamy texture and tangy flavor to the apple-Dijon pan sauce. Find crème fraîche stocked alongside other specialty cheeses, or substitute with sour cream.
Chicken Normandy Recipe
A cozy French chicken dinner with an irresistibly creamy apple and Dijon mustard sauce.
Prep time 15 minutes
Cook time 45 minutes to 55 minutes
Serves 4 to 6
Nutritional Info
Ingredients
- 6 bone-in, skin-on chicken thighs (2 1/2 to 3 pounds)
- 1 1/2 teaspoons kosher salt, divided
- 1/4 teaspoon freshly ground black pepper
- 3 ounces sliced bacon (not thick cut), cut crosswise into 1/4-inch pieces
- 1 medium shallot, diced (3 tablespoons)
- 2 cloves garlic, minced
- 2 tablespoons all-purpose flour
- 1/2 cup applejack or Cognac
- 2 cups apple cider or unfiltered apple juice
- 5 sprigs fresh thyme, plus more for garnish
- 1 tablespoon Dijon mustard
- 1 medium sweet-tart apple, such as Pink Lady or Honeycrisp (8 ounces), cored and sliced 1/2-inch thick
- 2 tablespoons crème fraîche or sour cream
Instructions
- Pat 6 bone-in, skin-on chicken thighs dry with paper towels. Season all over with 1 teaspoon of the kosher salt and 1/4 teaspoon black pepper.
- Cook 3 ounces chopped bacon in a large 12-inch skillet over medium heat, stirring occasionally, until crisp and browned, about 5 minutes. Using a slotted spoon, transfer the bacon to a paper towel-lined plate.
- Add the chicken skin-side down to the skillet and sear undisturbed until the skin is very crisp and deep golden-brown, about 10 minutes. Flip and cook until the second side is just browned, 2 to 3 minutes more. Transfer to a plate skin-side up (the chicken will not be cooked through). Carefully pour off all but 2 tablespoons of the fat in the skillet.
- Add 1 diced medium shallot and 2 minced garlic cloves to the skillet. Cook over medium heat until translucent, about 1 minute. Add 2 tablespoons all-purpose flour and cook until no dry spots remain and a deeply browned film forms at the bottom of the skillet, about 1 minute.
- Add 1/2 cup applejack and simmer, scraping up any browned bits from the bottom of the skillet, until thickened and reduced, about 30 seconds. Stir in 2 cups apple cider, 5 fresh thyme sprigs, 1 tablespoon Dijon mustard, and the remaining 1/2 teaspoon kosher salt. Bring to a simmer.
- Return the chicken skin-side up to the skillet and add any accumulated juices on the plate. Add 1 sliced medium apple and simmer until the sauce thickens slightly, the apples are crisp-tender, and an instant-read thermometer inserted into the thickest part of the chicken registers at least 165°F, 20 to 22 minutes.
- Turn off the heat. Remove and discard the thyme sprigs. Stir 2 tablespoons crème fraîche into the sauce. Garnish with the bacon and fresh thyme leaves if desired
Recipe Notes
Storage: Refrigerate leftovers in an airtight container for up to 4 days.

I Swear You’ll Use This Game-Changing Saucepan More Than Any Other Pot (It’s on Sale!)
As a professional recipe developer, I have a lot of kitchen equipment at home. But one of my all-time favorite pieces of cookware is the Cuisinart 2-quart pour saucepan. It has a built-in strainer, which makes it a total game-changer in my kitchen. At first glance, it looks like a regular saucepan — but the lid is designed to strain, and the pot has a pour spout so you can boil and drain without hauling out a colander. It’s a life-saver on lazy nights when all I want is comfort food, and it has earned a permanent spot on my stovetop. Here’s why I think it’s one of the most useful (and underrated) pots you can own. Bonus: It’s on sale for Black Friday!
Why Cuisinart’s 2-Quart Pour Saucepan Makes Boiling and Draining So Much Easier
The idea behind Cuisinart’s 2-quart pour saucepan is brilliantly simple. It comes with a multipurpose lid that fits snugly and has straining sections on two sides. Each side has two different-sized draining holes, and they’re useful for different foods. The smaller holes are great for little things like pastina, grains, orzo, and lentils, so nothing slips through. The larger holes are perfect for larger pasta shapes, potatoes, dumplings, and vegetables, as the water drains out quickly. There’s no juggling a big pot of boiling water into a colander, and no steam in your face.
The pour spout does a lot of the work, too. It gives you a clean, controlled stream whether you’re draining water or pouring something you want to keep, like a sauce. At two quarts, the pan is compact enough to store easily but roomy enough for a satisfying portion of pasta or a few potatoes. It’s also the perfect size for boiling eggs. You can cook a few at a time and drain the hot water right off before moving them to an ice bath for deviled eggs or egg salad sandwiches.
My Honest Review of Cuisinart’s 2-Quart Pour Saucepan
I’m not exaggerating when I say that this is one of my favorite pieces of cookware. Not having to pull out a separate colander is the biggest perk for me. It means one fewer thing to wash, which matters a lot when I’m tired and dinner just needs to happen.
I’ve used it for just about every kind of boil-and-drain situation. Any pasta or noodle works, and so do tortellini, ravioli, and frozen dumplings. It’s great for potatoes that are headed for mashed potatoes, and it’s perfect for blanching green beans because you can drain them and get them straight into ice water in seconds.
It’s also my partner in crime on nights when I really don’t feel like cooking. Making instant stir-fry noodles (Indomie Mi Goreng is my favorite!) and boxed mac and cheese is so much easier when the draining is built right in.
The pour spout is a bonus I didn’t expect to appreciate as much as I do. It makes pouring sauces and gravies much neater, with way fewer drips down the side of the pot.
The size is just right for everyday cooking, too. Beyond boiling and draining, I also use this saucepan to make small-batch soups and sauces, simmer oatmeal and grits, and cook rice. Cuisinart’s 2-quart pour saucepan is so versatile that I reach for it more than other pots I’ve owned for years.

Sweet Potato Recipes
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
14:30:00 UTC
23:30:00 KST

You gotta love a vegetable that can sit next to a burger as fries and then turn up in pie for dessert! Sweet potatoes are so much more than a Thanksgiving side, and they earn their place in my kitchen all year long. You can roast them until caramelized, blend them into silky soup, or use them to stretch a skillet dinner a little further. I’ve gathered 13 easy sweet potato recipes that put this affordable, filling ingredient to work, with a few holiday favorites for good measure! If you’ve got regular potatoes to use up, don’t miss our potato recipes roundup for even more ideas.
All recipes are rigorously tested in our Nashville Test Kitchen to ensure they are easy, affordable, and delicious.
Classic Sweet Potato Recipes
Some days I want to make something cozy from scratch; other days, I want dinner to involve little more than a sheet pan and some salt. These classics cover both, with creamy soup, simple baked potatoes, fries, and a few sweeter options in the mix.
1. Baked Sweet Potatoes
Baked Sweet Potatoes
Sometimes, butter, salt, and pepper are all a baked sweet potato needs. But I also love how easy it is to dress one up with black beans, leftover chili, or whatever needs using in the fridge. They’re filling, inexpensive, and take just five minutes of hands-on prep. Short on time? Partially cooking them in the microwave before baking can cut the oven time down considerably.
2. Sweet Potato Soup
Creamy Sweet Potato Soup
I’m all for a quick stovetop soup, but roasting the sweet potatoes first is worth the extra step in this creamy sweet potato soup. It brings out their natural sweetness, which pairs well with the cumin, smoked paprika, cinnamon, and a little cayenne. Everything gets blended until silky smooth and finished with a splash of heavy cream (coconut milk works too!). At around $1.47 per serving, this is some seriously affordable comfort food.
3. Roasted Sweet Potatoes
Roasted Sweet Potatoes
Three ingredients and a hot sheet pan. That’s all you need for these beautifully caramelized roasted sweet potatoes! I preheat the baking sheet to give the cubes a head start on browning, then roast them until the edges are golden and the centers are soft and creamy. No parboiling, no complicated seasonings, and just $0.66 per serving. They’re delicious straight from the oven, but I also like adding leftovers to salads and grain bowls.
4. Air Fryer Sweet Potato Fries
Air Fryer Sweet Potato Fries
I always appreciate a side dish that only needs a few ingredients and doesn’t take over the oven. These air fryer sweet potato fries are tossed with olive oil and Creole seasoning for a sweet, salty, slightly spicy flavor that goes really well with burgers and sandwiches. They’re thicker and more tender than traditional fries rather than super crispy, but that makes them especially good for dipping into a little creamy comeback sauce.
5. Twice-Baked Sweet Potatoes
Twice Baked Sweet Potatoes
A regular baked sweet potato is delicious, but sometimes you want to dress things up a little. For these twice-baked sweet potatoes, scoop out the fluffy insides and mash them with butter, brown sugar, cinnamon, nutmeg, and vanilla before putting them back in their skins and topping with marshmallows and homemade candied pecans. My advice? Make the pecans ahead so there’s one less thing to do when you’re ready to serve.
6. Sweet Potato Hash
Sweet Potato Hash
This sweet potato hash brings together diced sweet potatoes, savory breakfast sausage, peppers, and onions in one hearty skillet. It’s delicious with a fried egg on top, but there’s plenty here to make a satisfying breakfast or brunch on its own. You get six servings from one skillet, making this a handy recipe for feeding a crowd or getting ahead on breakfasts for the week.
Hearty and Budget-Friendly Dinners
Sweet potatoes make a very convincing case for stretching dinner, especially when they’re paired with beans, pasta, or ground meat. These three meals have enough going on to stand alone, with plenty of savory spices to balance out the potatoes’ natural sweetness.
7. Sweet Potato Ground Turkey Skillet
Sweet Potato and Ground Turkey Skillet
Ground turkey gets a serious flavor upgrade in this sweet potato and ground turkey skillet! Thyme, sage, and smoked paprika season the turkey and vegetables, while orzo cooks right in the same pan, soaking up the flavorful broth. A handful of kale and some Parmesan finish things off, giving you four generous servings in about 40 minutes. I particularly appreciate that the pasta cooks alongside everything else (saving another pot from the washing up!)
8. Sweet Potato Black Bean Skillet
Sweet Potato Black Bean Skillet
This sweet potato black bean skillet is a hearty, naturally vegan dinner that comes together in about 25 minutes. The sweet potatoes are browned in the skillet to bring out their natural sweetness, then combined with black beans, bell pepper, kale, tomatoes, and a smoky blend of spices. I like serving this over rice to make it even more filling, and the leftovers are ideal for meal prep!
9. Sweet Potato Chili
Chorizo Sweet Potato Chili
There’s a very good reason this sweet potato chili doesn’t need a long list of seasonings. Mexican chorizo already brings plenty of smoky, spicy flavor to the pot! Tender sweet potatoes balance that heat, while inexpensive canned beans help turn it into six filling servings on a budget. Add your favorite chili toppings, grab some tortilla chips, and dinner is sorted. Leftovers freeze well, too.
Thanksgiving Sweet Potato Favorites
I have a soft spot for the classics at Thanksgiving, and sweet potatoes are responsible for some of the best ones. This little lineup includes a crunchy-topped casserole, a creamy homemade pie, and two delicious breads worth making room for. Better still, they all use familiar ingredients and won’t send your holiday grocery bill through the roof.
10. Sweet Potato Casserole
Sweet Potato Casserole
I’m not saying a crunchy pecan streusel is better than marshmallows, but it makes a pretty strong case! Underneath that buttery topping, this sweet potato casserole has a fluffy, lightly spiced filling made with mashed sweet potatoes, cinnamon, vanilla, and brown sugar. It makes eight servings for less than a dollar each, which is pretty impressive for a classic Thanksgiving side dish. And you only need about 40 minutes from start to finish!
11. Sweet Potato Pie
Sweet Potato Pie
If you’re looking for an affordable homemade dessert to finish your Thanksgiving meal, don’t overlook sweet potato pie. Baking the sweet potatoes first gives the creamy custard filling a deeper, slightly caramelized flavor, while cinnamon, nutmeg, and vanilla add that familiar holiday warmth. A store-bought pie crust keeps things manageable, and you can make the whole pie ahead of time to free up the oven on Thanksgiving Day.
12. Sweet Potato Cornbread
Sweet Potato Cornbread
A hot cast-iron skillet gives this sweet potato cornbread a beautifully browned crust, while mashed sweet potatoes and sour cream keep the inside rich and moist. It’s thicker and a little sweeter than everyday cornbread, with cinnamon and nutmeg adding just enough warmth without turning it into cake. I think it’s especially delicious served warm with butter.
13. Sweet Potato Biscuits
Sweet Potato Biscuits
I can make a pretty convincing argument for serving homemade biscuits at any meal, but especially Thanksgiving! Adding mashed sweet potato to the dough gives these sweet potato biscuits a lovely orange color, a subtle sweetness, and a wonderfully soft & tender texture. You can even microwave the sweet potato to save time!
More Great Recipe Roundups
Our Sweet Potato round up was originally published 9/29/23. It was retested, reworked, and republished to be better than ever 11/15/26.

Tesla is inviting some people to buy a Cybercab ‘for personal use’ — but read the fine print
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
14:19:12 UTC
23:19:12 KST

Tesla is doubling, or perhaps tripling down on its bold claims of an autonomous future with the offer to “select customers” to purchase a Cybercab for “personal use.” But the gilded, steering-wheel-free two-seater will “only be available in supported service areas” — meaning you’ll only be able to buy one if you live in one of the cities where Tesla currently operates its Robotaxi service.
Tesla is inviting some people to buy a Cybercab ‘for personal use’ — but read the fine print
Look past the $29,990 sticker price and the math — and regulatory risk — gets messy fast.
The price of a Cybercab is listed at $29,990, plus a mandatory $299-a-month “Autonomy Subscription.” Tesla’s Robotaxi account on X announced the new offer, including screenshots of the order page and the invite email. But don’t expect a fleet of personally owned Cybercabs to suddenly materialize on public roads: “Initial availability is limited,” the invitation email warns.
Tesla began offering rides in the Cybercab as part of its Model Y-powered Robotaxi service in Austin in September. The company now has 278 Cybercabs in operation, representing approximately 40 percent of its fleet, according to the crowd-sourced Robotaxi Tracker.
The inclusion of a mandatory $299/month subscription is sure to rankle some fans who may have dreamed of cruising around in a $30,000 driverless Tesla, as originally promised by Tesla CEO Elon Musk when he first introduced the Cybercab in 2024. Musk made no mention of the Autonomy Subscription at that event, though it shouldn’t come as too much of a surprise given the $99/month fee Tesla currently charges for its Full Self-Driving (FSD) feature.
Still, that money adds up over time. Over a standard 5-year ownership period, the mandatory $299/month fee adds $17,940 to the sticker price (even with the first month included), bringing the real baseline cost to roughly $47,600 before taxes, charging, and insurance — more than a brand-new Model 3 or Model Y that you can actually drive anywhere in the country.
Unlike a standard Tesla where canceling FSD just reverts the car to manual driving, canceling the $299/month Autonomy Subscription renders the Cybercab completely unusable on public roads because there is no manual fallback. In effect, the car becomes a brick. Additionally, Tesla says that buyers will have to put down a $1,000 non-refundable deposit just to get in line for a December delivery window that isn’t guaranteed.
One of the biggest selling points of Musk’s 2024 “We, Robot” pitch was that regular people could buy a Cybercab and add it to Tesla’s Robotaxi network to earn passive income while they sleep or work. But the order terms explicitly prohibit owners from using the vehicle for commercial purposes — meaning no Uber, no DoorDash, and no putting it on Tesla’s own Robotaxi network to offset the $299/month fee. Tesla AI chief Ashok Elluswamy has floated Q2 2027 as a possible target for letting customer cars join the network, so early buyers are paying full freight with zero way to monetize the car.
Moreover, Tesla only has to give you 30 days’ warning before raising the price. If they update the rules and you keep using the service, you automatically agree to the new terms. Also, if you cancel — or if Tesla suspends your access — you won’t get a partial refund for the rest of the month you already paid for.
Of course, that’s just the fine print. On X, Tesla CEO Elon Musk was promising a much wider rollout, subject — of course — to “regulatory approval.” Musk frequently frames regulatory approval as the primary external bottleneck holding back his robotaxi timelines as a way to shift focus away from Tesla’s technological shortcomings.
(For what it’s worth, he also called his own company’s $299/month Autonomy Subscription “too high,” arguing that it should actually be $115/month or closer to Tesla’s $99/month Full Self-Driving subscription.)
But Musk seemed more focused on taunting YouTuber Marques Brownlee, who promised to shave his head if Tesla delivered a Cybercab for under $30,000 before 2027, than answering any of the litany of safety and liability questions raised by this offer. (For his part, Brownlee says he thinks his hair is safe.)
For starters, how does Tesla plan on selling a vehicle that doesn’t comply with current Federal Motor Vehicle Safety Standards that require traditional controls like steering wheels and pedals? Tesla says the Autonomy Subscription covers autonomous driving, maintenance, and towing, but explicitly excludes auto insurance. The company hasn’t explained how personal auto insurers will underwrite a car with no human driver, or how liability is split between the owner and Tesla if the vehicle causes an accident while operating autonomously. Nor has Tesla disclosed how it selects invited buyers or how many units are earmarked for private sale versus its internal fleet.
Then there is the small matter of the National Highway Traffic Safety Administration already opening a formal audit into how Tesla self-certified a vehicle without a steering wheel, pedals, or side mirrors. Tesla submitted its legal justification to NHTSA in October, requested that the entire response be kept confidential, and is now taking customer deposits while NHTSA’s ruling is still pending.
Which means early buyers aren’t just paying $47,600 for a geofenced car they aren’t allowed to drive — they’re putting down non-refundable cash on a vehicle the federal government hasn’t even decided is street-legal.

My Family Requests These Pecan Pie Bars Every Thanksgiving

Skip the Pie Crust: My Family Says These Pecan Pie Bars Are Even Better Than Pie
Pecan pie is one of my favorite holiday desserts, but I’ll be honest: I don’t always have the patience for pie crust. That’s exactly why I love our Food Editor Patty Catalano’s pecan pie bars. They’ve got all the gooey, nutty sweetness of classic pecan pie, with a buttery shortbread crust that’s way easier to make. No rolling or crimping — just press it into the pan and bake. I made these bars last year for Thanksgiving, and they were a hit!
Get the recipe: Pecan Pie Bars
What Makes These Pecan Pie Bars So Good
The buttery shortbread crust comes together in minutes (no rolling pin required!) and gives the bars the perfect foundation for the rich, gooey filling. You can cut them into 12 generous bars, but I like to slice them into smaller pieces for the perfect bite — especially when there’s a dessert table filled with pumpkin pie and cookies. They’re easy to share, and (dare I say) even more delicious than pie.
Tips for Making Pecan Pie Bars
- Use pecan pieces. No need to buy pecan halves — pecan pieces or chopped pecans work well because they’re distributed evenly throughout the filling.
- Choose your syrup. Light corn syrup or dark corn syrup (or a mix of both) will give the bars their signature glossy, caramel-like texture.

Serving GLM-5.3 at 607.8 tok/s across two Nvidia DGX stations with vLLM
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
14:09:25 UTC
23:09:25 KST

Running a frontier-scale model locally means keeping all its weights in GPU memory; the largest models no longer fit on one machine. Two NVIDIA DGX Stations connected over ConnectX-8 RDMA serve one model through a single endpoint, with tensor parallelism splitting the model weights and computation across both GPUs.
The NVIDIA GLM-5.3 NVFP4 model, a quantized version of Z.ai’s mixture-of-experts (MoE) model, has 753 billion parameters, with 40 billion activated per token. Sparse activation reduces computation, but all model weights still need to remain available because each token can be routed to different experts. At about 464 GB, the checkpoint exceeds the 252 GB of HBM3e on a single GB300 GPU. This tutorial uses two DGX Stations to keep the weights in GPU memory, with CPU weight offload disabled.
Before starting, connect the two Stations with the Connect Two DGX Stations playbook.
This post shows how to serve GLM-5.3 locally across two NVIDIA DGX Stations with vLLM, send a request, and measure behavior under concurrent load. Tensor parallelism (TP=2) splits the model’s tensors across the two GB300 GPUs. Both Stations compute every request, and Station A returns the answer through one OpenAI-compatible API. By the end, you’ll have a working endpoint and a baseline for how it behaves under load.
Prerequisites
- Two GB300 DGX Stations running Linux, connected and validated with the Connect Two DGX Stations playbook, including its GPU-memory RDMA test on both rails.
- SSH and sudo access on both Stations with the same Linux username. Docker and the NVIDIA Container Toolkit come preinstalled with the DGX Station software stack. Allow outbound access to the container registry, Hugging Face, and package repositories.
- At least 464 GB of free disk space on each Station for its copy of the checkpoint, plus room for the vLLM container image and temporary files.
- Acceptance of the NVIDIA Open Model Agreement and the original GLM-5.3 license terms, listed on the model card. SPEED-Bench is governed by the NVIDIA Evaluation Dataset License Agreement.
- A Hugging Face account and read access token, with access to the gated cais/hle dataset: sign in, open its page, and select Agree and access repository. Step 5 downloads SPEED-Bench prompts from this dataset.
The companion video follows the same workflow.
Step 1. Prepare the two Stations
This tutorial uses a separate computer, such as your laptop, to manage both Stations. Start by opening an SSH session to each Station from that computer. Alternatively, work from a local terminal on each Station. Run every command in this step on both Stations unless it starts with “On A.”
The two Stations run the same container and checkpoint in different roles: Station A (rank 0) hosts the API, and Station B (rank 1) runs only a worker, with no API server.
First, check Docker access and collect the network values you’ll use later:
docker version
ibdev2netdev
ip -br addr
The ibdev2netdev output lists the two direct rails, cx8r0 and cx8r1. Pick one for serving traffic, for example cx8r1, and note two things for it on each Station:
- Its RDMA device, on the ibdev2netdev line that ends with the rail’s name. In mlx5_1 port 1 ==> cx8r1 (Up), the device is mlx5_1.
- Its IPv4 address, on the ip -br addr line that starts with the rail’s name. In cx8r1 UP 192.168.101.1/24, the address is 192.168.101.1.
The rail name and device are the same on both Stations; only the IPv4 address differs.
Next, create ~/glm53.env on each Station. This file stores the values that later commands use:
nano ~/glm53.env
Paste the following block and replace each <...> placeholder, keeping the quotes, with the values you noted.
RANK="<NODE_RANK>"                    # RANK="0" on Station A, RANK="1" on Station B 
HEAD_IP="<STATION_A_IPV4_ADDRESS>"    # IPv4 address you noted on Station A; same in both files
WORKER_IP="<STATION_B_IPV4_ADDRESS>"  # IPv4 address you noted on Station B; same in both files
RAIL_IFACE="<RAIL_NAME>"              # Rail you picked, for example cx8r1; same in both files
RAIL_HCA="<RDMA_DEVICE>"              # RDMA device you noted, for example mlx5_1; same in both files
IMG=vllm/vllm-openai:v0.30.0-aarch64
IMG+=@sha256:4864d46625cbc3307623e29ac742030655e27249feba7b97ec925ce4cc4dfb56
MODEL_REPO=nvidia/GLM-5.3-NVFP4
MODEL_REV=e3b8f9f2fad29727dfa95e766c5cac18ea1414bf
MODEL_HOST="$HOME/models/GLM-5.3-NVFP4"
CONTAINER=glm53-full
PORT=8000
if [ "$RANK" = 0 ]; then
  NODE_IP=$HEAD_IP; PEER_IP=$WORKER_IP
else
  NODE_IP=$WORKER_IP; PEER_IP=$HEAD_IP
fi
GPU_UUID=$(nvidia-smi --query-gpu=uuid,name --format=csv,noheader |
  awk -F, '$2 ~ /GB300/ {gsub(/ /,"",$1); print $1; exit}')
For example, with the default addresses on cx8r1, Station A’s file starts like this. Station B’s file is identical except for RANK="1":
RANK="0"
HEAD_IP="192.168.101.1"
WORKER_IP="192.168.101.2"
RAIL_IFACE="cx8r1"
RAIL_HCA="mlx5_1"
MODEL_HOST is built from your home directory, and Step 2 copies the checkpoint over SSH to the same path on Station B. 
On both Stations, load the values and check storage, connectivity, and baseline memory:
source ~/glm53.env
mkdir -p "$MODEL_HOST" && df -h "$MODEL_HOST"
ethtool "$RAIL_IFACE"
ip link show dev "$RAIL_IFACE"
ping -I "$RAIL_IFACE" -M do -s 8972 -c 3 "$PEER_IP"
timedatectl show -p NTPSynchronized --value
nvidia-smi
These checks catch cabling and addressing mistakes before they surface as NCCL timeouts. Expect a 400000Mb/s link, MTU 9000, successful jumbo-frame pings, and synchronized time. nvidia-smi should show no compute processes on the GB300. On a Station with a display GPU, Xorg can appear on both GPUs with a few MiB. 
Step 2 copies the checkpoint from A to B over SSH, so A needs passwordless access to B. On A, create a key without a passphrase (see SSH keys for details), copy its public key to B after checking B’s host fingerprint, and confirm the login:
ssh-keygen -t ed25519
ssh-copy-id "$WORKER_IP"
ssh -o BatchMode=yes "$WORKER_IP" hostname
Step 2. Download and copy the model
Tensor parallelism requires both ranks to run the same software against the same weights. Start by pulling the pinned image on both Stations:
docker pull "$IMG" &&
docker image inspect "$IMG" --format '{{.Architecture}} {{.RepoDigests}}'
The pull can take several minutes. Matching arm64 digests mean both ranks run identical software.
On A, download the pinned checkpoint. At about 464 GB, it takes roughly an hour at 1 Gb/s, or about six minutes at 10 Gb/s:
mkdir -p "$MODEL_HOST"
docker run --rm --network host -v "$MODEL_HOST:/model" \
  --entrypoint bash "$IMG" -lc \
  "exec hf download '$MODEL_REPO' --revision '$MODEL_REV' --local-dir /model"
When hf download prints Downloaded, copy the files to B. On A:
ssh "$WORKER_IP" "mkdir -p '$MODEL_HOST'" &&
rsync -aH --info=progress2 --exclude '.cache/' \
  "$MODEL_HOST/" "$WORKER_IP:$MODEL_HOST/"
Each disk keeps the full checkpoint; vLLM partitions the weights across the GPUs at load time, with no further quantization.
On A, compare both copies (the check reads B’s file list over SSH), then validate the checkpoint:
(
  set -euo pipefail
  L=$(find "$MODEL_HOST" -path '*/.cache' -prune -o \
    -type f -printf '%P %s\n' | LC_ALL=C sort | sha256sum)
  R=$(ssh "$WORKER_IP" "set -o pipefail; find '$MODEL_HOST' \
    -path '*/.cache' -prune -o -type f -printf '%P %s\\n' | \
    LC_ALL=C sort | sha256sum")
  test "$L" = "$R"
  test "$(find "$MODEL_HOST" -maxdepth 1 \
    -name 'model-*-of-00050.safetensors' | wc -l)" = 50
  python3 -c "import json; c=json.load(open('$MODEL_HOST/config.json')); \
assert c['architectures']==['GlmMoeDsaForCausalLM']; \
assert c['quantization_config']['quant_algo']=='MIXED_PRECISION'"
  echo MODEL_READY
)
MODEL_READY means both Stations hold the same file names and sizes in the expected layout. MIXED_PRECISION is expected: NVFP4 quantization targets MoE-block linear weights and activations, not every tensor.
Step 3. Launch the distributed service
This step turns two machines into one service: the same launch script runs on both Stations, reads ~/glm53.env, and starts one vLLM rank on each. Two settings matter most if you adapt it: --max-model-len 98304, the context ceiling in tokens, which covers input plus output, and --gpu-memory-utilization 0.93, which gives vLLM 93% of GPU memory. If you change other flags, change one at a time and test again.
For the latest recommended configurations, see NVIDIA’s Local AI recipe repository.
On both Stations, create a file named ~/launch-glm53.sh:
nano ~/launch-glm53.sh
The script enables multi-token prediction (MTP), a form of speculative decoding: the model’s MTP layer drafts four tokens per step, and vLLM verifies them with the full model before accepting them, so each step can produce several tokens. The gain depends on how often drafts are accepted, which varies with the prompt.
FP8 KV-cache storage halves cache memory compared with 16-bit storage, leaving room for more tokens. Prefix caching is enabled to avoid reprocessing prompt prefixes the server has already seen. Paste the script below; it checks your configuration, finds the RoCE GID index for the selected rail, and starts this Station’s vLLM rank in a container:
set -euo pipefail
source "$HOME/glm53.env"
case "$RANK" in 0|1) ;; *) echo "Invalid RANK" >&2; exit 1;; esac
test -n "$GPU_UUID"
test -f "$MODEL_HOST/config.json"
GID_INDEX=$(/usr/sbin/show_gids 2>/dev/null |
  awk -v d="$RAIL_HCA" -v ip="$NODE_IP" \
  '$1==d && $5==ip && $6=="v2" {g=$3} END {if(g!="") print g}')
test -n "$GID_INDEX"
HEADLESS=
if [ "$RANK" = 1 ]; then HEADLESS=--headless; fi
SPEC_CONFIG='{"method":"mtp","num_speculative_tokens":4}'
KERNEL_CONFIG='{"enable_flashinfer_autotune":false,"moe_backend":"flashinfer_cutlass"}'
COMPILE_CONFIG='{"cudagraph_mode":"FULL_DECODE_ONLY","cudagraph_capture_sizes":[5,10,20,40,80],"pass_config":{"fuse_allreduce_rms":false}}'
docker run -d --name "$CONTAINER" \
  --gpus device="$GPU_UUID" --network host --ipc host --shm-size 32g \
  --device /dev/infiniband --cap-add IPC_LOCK \
  --ulimit memlock=-1 --ulimit stack=67108864 \
  -v "$MODEL_HOST:/model:ro" \
  -e RANK="$RANK" -e HEADLESS="$HEADLESS" \
  -e MASTER_ADDR="$HEAD_IP" -e MODEL_REPO="$MODEL_REPO" -e PORT="$PORT" \
  -e SPEC_CONFIG="$SPEC_CONFIG" -e KERNEL_CONFIG="$KERNEL_CONFIG" \
  -e COMPILE_CONFIG="$COMPILE_CONFIG" \
  -e NCCL_CUMEM_ENABLE=1 -e NCCL_IB_HCA="$RAIL_HCA" \
  -e NCCL_IB_GID_INDEX="$GID_INDEX" \
  -e NCCL_IB_TIMEOUT=22 -e NCCL_IB_RETRY_CNT=7 \
  -e VLLM_ALLREDUCE_USE_FLASHINFER=0 -e VLLM_ALLREDUCE_USE_SYMM_MEM=0 \
  -e NCCL_SOCKET_IFNAME="$RAIL_IFACE" -e GLOO_SOCKET_IFNAME="$RAIL_IFACE" \
  -e NCCL_NET_GDR_LEVEL=5 -e NCCL_DEBUG=INFO -e NCCL_DEBUG_SUBSYS=INIT,NET \
  -e NCCL_DEBUG_FILE='/tmp/nccl_%h_%p.log' -e VLLM_HOST_IP="$NODE_IP" \
  -e VLLM_EXECUTE_MODEL_TIMEOUT_SECONDS=1800 \
  -e PYTORCH_CUDA_ALLOC_CONF=expandable_segments:True \
  -e VLLM_ALLOW_LONG_MAX_MODEL_LEN=1 -e FLASHINFER_DISABLE_VERSION_CHECK=1 \
  --entrypoint bash "$IMG" -lc '
    set -euo pipefail  
    # Use NCCL 2.30.7 from the image: older versions can deadlock
    # cross-node CUDA graph replays over RoCE (vLLM issue #52504).
    export VLLM_NCCL_SO_PATH=$(python3 -c "import nvidia.nccl,os;
print(os.path.join(list(nvidia.nccl.__path__)[0], \"lib\", \"libnccl.so.2\"))")
    test -f "$VLLM_NCCL_SO_PATH"
    EXTRA=()
    if [ -n "$HEADLESS" ]; then EXTRA+=("$HEADLESS"); fi
    exec vllm serve /model --served-model-name "$MODEL_REPO" \
      --host 0.0.0.0 --port "$PORT" \
      --nnodes 2 --node-rank "$RANK" --master-addr "$MASTER_ADDR" \
      --master-port 29501 --tensor-parallel-size 2 \
      --speculative-config "$SPEC_CONFIG" \
      --max-model-len 98304  --max-num-batched-tokens 8192 \
      --block-size 128 --max-num-seqs 16 \
      --trust-remote-code --disable-custom-all-reduce \
      --kv-cache-dtype fp8 --enable-prefix-caching \
      --gpu-memory-utilization 0.93 \
      --enable-prompt-tokens-details \
      --kernel-config "$KERNEL_CONFIG" --compilation-config "$COMPILE_CONFIG" \
      --distributed-timeout-seconds 1800 \
      --enable-auto-tool-choice --tool-call-parser glm47 --reasoning-parser glm47 \
      "${EXTRA[@]}"
  '
Run the script on B first, then on A:
bash ~/launch-glm53.sh
Each command prints a container ID and returns immediately; the service is not ready yet. B’s worker waits for A to join, then each Station loads its share of the weights from local storage, prepares kernels, and captures CUDA graphs. This takes several minutes.
On A, follow the log to see when the service is ready (--since hides output from earlier runs):
docker logs --follow --since "$(docker inspect \
  --format '{{.State.StartedAt}}' "$CONTAINER")" "$CONTAINER"
When the log shows Application startup complete, press Ctrl+C to stop following it; the service keeps running. 
At this point one model spans two machines. Neither GPU holds a complete copy, and neither can answer a request alone: every token needs both ranks to exchange partial results over the RoCE rail.
The remaining GPU memory holds the KV cache, which stores the context of in-flight requests. The startup log reports its capacity (GPU KV cache size). All requests in flight share it, so longer prompts or more users need more of it. If you run out, lower --max-model-len or send fewer requests at once.
Step 4. Verify serving and send a request
On A, check the API (127.0.0.1 is A itself):
docker exec "$CONTAINER" curl -fsS --max-time 10 \
  "http://127.0.0.1:$PORT/health" >/dev/null && echo HEALTH_OK
HEALTH_OK proves that Station A answers. Next, on both Stations, check that the ranks communicate over GPU RDMA rather than falling back to TCP sockets. The check reads the NCCL logs that the launcher writes through NCCL_DEBUG=INFO, and the same block shows the loaded memory:
docker exec "$CONTAINER" bash -lc '
  set -euo pipefail
  files=(/tmp/nccl_*.log)
  test -e "${files[0]}"
  grep -hE "NET/IB.*GDRDMA|GPU Direct RDMA Enabled" "${files[@]}" >/dev/null
  grep -hF "$NCCL_IB_HCA" "${files[@]}" >/dev/null
  if grep -hE "NET/Socket|NCCL WARN|NCCL ERROR" "${files[@]}"; then
    exit 1
  fi
  echo RDMA_CHECK_OK
'
nvidia-smi
RDMA_CHECK_OK means the check passed with no warnings. In nvidia-smi, A shows the VLLM::Worker_TP0 process and B shows VLLM::Worker_TP1, one rank per GPU. The startup log reports about 218 GiB of weights per GPU, roughly half the checkpoint, which confirms that the model is split across the two GPUs.
On A, send a chat request through the OpenAI-compatible API:
docker exec "$CONTAINER" curl -fsS --max-time 120 \
  "http://127.0.0.1:$PORT/v1/chat/completions" \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"model":"nvidia/GLM-5.3-NVFP4",
    "messages":[{"role":"user","content":"Quarterly units sold: Q1 = 120; Q2 = 180; Q3 = 150. Summarize the trend in one sentence and calculate the percentage change from Q1 to Q2."}],
    "temperature":0,"max_tokens":4096,
    "chat_template_kwargs":{"reasoning_effort":"low"}}' |
python3 -c 'import json,sys; c=json.load(sys.stdin)["choices"][0];
print(c["message"]["content"]); print("finish_reason:",c["finish_reason"])'
The response should look like this:
**Trend:** Units sold rose sharply in Q2 before partially declining in Q3, remaining above the Q1 level.
**Q1 → Q2 percentage change:** (180 - 120) / 120 × 100 = **50% increase**
finish_reason: stop
finish_reason: stop means the model finished its answer instead of hitting the token limit. The request sets reasoning_effort to low in chat_template_kwargs. GLM-5.3 also accepts high and max, which let it reason longer before answering, at the cost of latency. Because the launcher enables tool calling (--enable-auto-tool-choice), agent frameworks that use the OpenAI tools API can call the same endpoint.
Step 5. Concurrency and release resources
AIPerf, the NVIDIA benchmarking tool for inference endpoints, measures throughput and latency. Installing it inside the serving container can change vLLM’s dependencies, so on A, install it in its own virtual environment:
sudo apt update &&
sudo apt install -y python3.12-venv python3.12-dev build-essential &&
python3.12 -m venv "$HOME/.venvs/aiperf-0.12.0" &&
"$HOME/.venvs/aiperf-0.12.0/bin/pip" install 'aiperf==0.12.0' &&
"$HOME/.venvs/aiperf-0.12.0/bin/pip" check
The install should end with No broken requirements found.
AIPerf can send synthetic prompts or replay an existing dataset. This tutorial uses the 80 Coding conversations from SPEED-Bench, a set of real prompts built to evaluate speculative decoding, so MTP is measured on realistic text. SPEED-Bench’s preparation script fetches these prompts from several Hugging Face datasets, including the gated cais/hle: if you haven’t already, open its page and select Agree and access repository before you continue. Then, on A, log in to Hugging Face:
"$HOME/.venvs/aiperf-0.12.0/bin/hf" auth login
Next, download and prepare the dataset. See the official AIPerf guide for details:
SPEED_BENCH_DIR="$HOME/datasets/speed-bench"
mkdir -p "$SPEED_BENCH_DIR"
curl -fLsS https://raw.githubusercontent.com/NVIDIA-NeMo/Skills/refs/heads/main/nemo_skills/dataset/speed-bench/prepare.py | \
  "$HOME/.venvs/aiperf-0.12.0/bin/python" - --config qualitative --output_dir "$SPEED_BENCH_DIR"
On A, check the dataset and service. Continue when the check prints BENCHMARK_READY:
(
  set -euo pipefail
  source ~/glm53.env
  curl -fsS "http://$HEAD_IP:$PORT/health" >/dev/null
  "$HOME/.venvs/aiperf-0.12.0/bin/python" - <<'PY_CHECK'
import json
from pathlib import Path
root = Path.home()
path = root / "datasets/speed-bench/qualitative.jsonl"
assert path.exists(), "Dataset missing: rerun the SPEED-Bench preparation"
rows = [json.loads(line) for line in path.read_text().splitlines() if line.strip()]
coding = [row for row in rows if row["category"] == "coding"]
assert len(coding) == 80, "Expected 80 Coding conversations"
assert sum(len(row["messages"]) for row in coding) == 89
assert all("FULL BENCHMARK DATA SHOULD BE FETCHED" not in message["content"]
           for row in coding for message in row["messages"])
print("BENCHMARK_READY: dataset has 80 Coding conversations, 89 turns in total.")
PY_CHECK
)
The full sweep covers concurrency 1, 8, 16, and 32. At each level, AIPerf sends 3×C warmup requests, then measures 10×C requests, where C is the concurrency. Some conversations are sent more than once at higher concurrency.
Benchmark requests use the model’s default reasoning effort (max) and an 8,192-token output cap; responses can finish before reaching it. Full sweeps can take several hours.
 A two-hour request timeout leaves room for long responses, --server-metrics collects server counters used to measure MTP acceptance, and --export-level raw keeps each response for inspection. 
For a quick test, run the block below on A with both containers running and no other API traffic. It tests C1 and C8 with prefix caching enabled, as in the reference measurements in Table 2, below.
(
  set -euo pipefail
  source ~/glm53.env
  CODING_DATASET="$HOME/datasets/speed-bench/qualitative.jsonl"
  test -f "$CODING_DATASET"
 export CONCURRENCIES=${CONCURRENCIES:-"1 8"} # Quick run: C1 and C8.
  # Optional full benchmark: follow the reproduction instructions below.
  # Step 3: --max-num-seqs 16 -> export CONCURRENCIES="1 8 16"
  # Step 3: --max-num-seqs 32 -> export CONCURRENCIES=32
  RUN_DIR="$HOME/aiperf-results/blog-coding-$(date +%Y%m%d-%H%M%S)"
  for C in $CONCURRENCIES;  do
    ART="$RUN_DIR/c$C"
    env -u HF_HUB_OFFLINE -u TRANSFORMERS_OFFLINE \
      "$HOME/.venvs/aiperf-0.12.0/bin/aiperf" profile \
      -m "$MODEL_REPO" --url "http://$HEAD_IP:$PORT" \
      --endpoint-type chat --streaming --tokenizer "$MODEL_HOST" \
      --custom-dataset-type speed_bench_coding --input-file "$CODING_DATASET" \
      --request-count "$((10 * C))" --warmup-request-count "$((3 * C))" \
      --osl 8192 \
      --use-server-token-count --concurrency "$C" --random-seed 42 \
      --request-timeout-seconds 7200 \
      --server-metrics "http://$HEAD_IP:$PORT/metrics" \
      --export-level raw --artifact-dir "$ART"
    printf "Results: %s\n" "$ART"
  done
  printf "Results directory: %s\n" "$RUN_DIR"
)
AIPerf saves metrics, raw responses, and logs in the results directory. Warmup requests are excluded from the reported metrics.
Optional: Reproduce Table 2. In ~/launch-glm53.sh from Step 3 on both Stations, keep --enable-prefix-caching and set --max-num-seqs to 16 or 32 for the corresponding group shown in the comments above. Before each group, stop and remove the current containers on A, then B (docker stop --timeout 60 "$CONTAINER", followed by docker rm "$CONTAINER"). Relaunch the updated script on B, then A with bash ~/launch-glm53.sh and wait for Application startup complete. On A, run the matching export CONCURRENCIES command from the comments, then rerun the AIPerf block.
To test without prefix caching, replace --enable-prefix-caching with --no-enable-prefix-caching in the Step 3 script on both Stations, then recreate the containers as above. Performance may differ from Table 2.
Figure 2 compares overall output throughput across measured requests with mean per-user throughput. Token counts include reasoning.
Table 2, below, shows how throughput and latency change as more requests share the service.
Overall throughput peaks at 607.8 tok/s at C16. It falls to 385.0 tok/s at C32, where mean request latency is 453.5 s. Higher concurrency also reduces mean per-user throughput, from 115.3 tok/s at C1 to 13.0 tok/s at C32.
After the requests finish, stop the vLLM container on A, then on B:
docker stop --timeout 60 "$CONTAINER"
docker inspect --format \
  'Status={{.State.Status}} Running={{.State.Running}}' "$CONTAINER"
nvidia-smi
The status should read Status=exited Running=false, with no vLLM processes in nvidia-smi; the checkpoint, containers, and results stay on disk. To resume, run docker start "$CONTAINER" on B, then A, and repeat the Step 4 checks.
Troubleshoot common blockers
- Missing variables or SSH failure: source ~/glm53.env after reconnecting; verify A-to-B key authentication and B’s fingerprint.
- SPEED-Bench preparation fails with a gated-dataset error: sign in to Hugging Face with the same account, open the cais/hle page, select Agree and access repository, then rerun the preparation.
- Startup or RDMA check failure: inspect docker logs "$CONTAINER" on both nodes; verify the checkpoint and the GID, and rerun the playbook’s validation (script 07).
- CUDA out of memory: lower --max-model-len or concurrency, then retest.
Put the endpoint to work
You now have a 753-billion-parameter model running behind one local API on two NVIDIA DGX Stations, with every weight in GPU memory. That opens up work that’s hard to do on a hosted endpoint: running inference on sensitive or proprietary data, prototyping agents locally, and iterating on prompts without per-token costs.
Connect your own application using the vLLM serving documentation, then rerun AIPerf with your prompts to find the concurrency that fits your workload. A frontier-scale model is now ready to build on, right from your desk.

====================================================================================================

Serving GLM-5.3 at 607.8 tok/s across two Nvidia DGX stations with vLLM
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
14:09:25 UTC
23:09:25 KST

Running a frontier-scale model locally means keeping all its weights in GPU memory; the largest models no longer fit on one machine. Two NVIDIA DGX Stations connected over ConnectX-8 RDMA serve one model through a single endpoint, with tensor parallelism splitting the model weights and computation across both GPUs.
The NVIDIA GLM-5.3 NVFP4 model, a quantized version of Z.ai’s mixture-of-experts (MoE) model, has 753 billion parameters, with 40 billion activated per token. Sparse activation reduces computation, but all model weights still need to remain available because each token can be routed to different experts. At about 464 GB, the checkpoint exceeds the 252 GB of HBM3e on a single GB300 GPU. This tutorial uses two DGX Stations to keep the weights in GPU memory, with CPU weight offload disabled.
Before starting, connect the two Stations with the Connect Two DGX Stations playbook.
This post shows how to serve GLM-5.3 locally across two NVIDIA DGX Stations with vLLM, send a request, and measure behavior under concurrent load. Tensor parallelism (TP=2) splits the model’s tensors across the two GB300 GPUs. Both Stations compute every request, and Station A returns the answer through one OpenAI-compatible API. By the end, you’ll have a working endpoint and a baseline for how it behaves under load.
Prerequisites
- Two GB300 DGX Stations running Linux, connected and validated with the Connect Two DGX Stations playbook, including its GPU-memory RDMA test on both rails.
- SSH and sudo access on both Stations with the same Linux username. Docker and the NVIDIA Container Toolkit come preinstalled with the DGX Station software stack. Allow outbound access to the container registry, Hugging Face, and package repositories.
- At least 464 GB of free disk space on each Station for its copy of the checkpoint, plus room for the vLLM container image and temporary files.
- Acceptance of the NVIDIA Open Model Agreement and the original GLM-5.3 license terms, listed on the model card. SPEED-Bench is governed by the NVIDIA Evaluation Dataset License Agreement.
- A Hugging Face account and read access token, with access to the gated cais/hle dataset: sign in, open its page, and select Agree and access repository. Step 5 downloads SPEED-Bench prompts from this dataset.
The companion video follows the same workflow.
Step 1. Prepare the two Stations
This tutorial uses a separate computer, such as your laptop, to manage both Stations. Start by opening an SSH session to each Station from that computer. Alternatively, work from a local terminal on each Station. Run every command in this step on both Stations unless it starts with “On A.”
The two Stations run the same container and checkpoint in different roles: Station A (rank 0) hosts the API, and Station B (rank 1) runs only a worker, with no API server.
First, check Docker access and collect the network values you’ll use later:
docker version
ibdev2netdev
ip -br addr
The ibdev2netdev output lists the two direct rails, cx8r0 and cx8r1. Pick one for serving traffic, for example cx8r1, and note two things for it on each Station:
- Its RDMA device, on the ibdev2netdev line that ends with the rail’s name. In mlx5_1 port 1 ==> cx8r1 (Up), the device is mlx5_1.
- Its IPv4 address, on the ip -br addr line that starts with the rail’s name. In cx8r1 UP 192.168.101.1/24, the address is 192.168.101.1.
The rail name and device are the same on both Stations; only the IPv4 address differs.
Next, create ~/glm53.env on each Station. This file stores the values that later commands use:
nano ~/glm53.env
Paste the following block and replace each <...> placeholder, keeping the quotes, with the values you noted.
RANK="<NODE_RANK>"                    # RANK="0" on Station A, RANK="1" on Station B 
HEAD_IP="<STATION_A_IPV4_ADDRESS>"    # IPv4 address you noted on Station A; same in both files
WORKER_IP="<STATION_B_IPV4_ADDRESS>"  # IPv4 address you noted on Station B; same in both files
RAIL_IFACE="<RAIL_NAME>"              # Rail you picked, for example cx8r1; same in both files
RAIL_HCA="<RDMA_DEVICE>"              # RDMA device you noted, for example mlx5_1; same in both files
IMG=vllm/vllm-openai:v0.30.0-aarch64
IMG+=@sha256:4864d46625cbc3307623e29ac742030655e27249feba7b97ec925ce4cc4dfb56
MODEL_REPO=nvidia/GLM-5.3-NVFP4
MODEL_REV=e3b8f9f2fad29727dfa95e766c5cac18ea1414bf
MODEL_HOST="$HOME/models/GLM-5.3-NVFP4"
CONTAINER=glm53-full
PORT=8000
if [ "$RANK" = 0 ]; then
  NODE_IP=$HEAD_IP; PEER_IP=$WORKER_IP
else
  NODE_IP=$WORKER_IP; PEER_IP=$HEAD_IP
fi
GPU_UUID=$(nvidia-smi --query-gpu=uuid,name --format=csv,noheader |
  awk -F, '$2 ~ /GB300/ {gsub(/ /,"",$1); print $1; exit}')
For example, with the default addresses on cx8r1, Station A’s file starts like this. Station B’s file is identical except for RANK="1":
RANK="0"
HEAD_IP="192.168.101.1"
WORKER_IP="192.168.101.2"
RAIL_IFACE="cx8r1"
RAIL_HCA="mlx5_1"
MODEL_HOST is built from your home directory, and Step 2 copies the checkpoint over SSH to the same path on Station B. 
On both Stations, load the values and check storage, connectivity, and baseline memory:
source ~/glm53.env
mkdir -p "$MODEL_HOST" && df -h "$MODEL_HOST"
ethtool "$RAIL_IFACE"
ip link show dev "$RAIL_IFACE"
ping -I "$RAIL_IFACE" -M do -s 8972 -c 3 "$PEER_IP"
timedatectl show -p NTPSynchronized --value
nvidia-smi
These checks catch cabling and addressing mistakes before they surface as NCCL timeouts. Expect a 400000Mb/s link, MTU 9000, successful jumbo-frame pings, and synchronized time. nvidia-smi should show no compute processes on the GB300. On a Station with a display GPU, Xorg can appear on both GPUs with a few MiB. 
Step 2 copies the checkpoint from A to B over SSH, so A needs passwordless access to B. On A, create a key without a passphrase (see SSH keys for details), copy its public key to B after checking B’s host fingerprint, and confirm the login:
ssh-keygen -t ed25519
ssh-copy-id "$WORKER_IP"
ssh -o BatchMode=yes "$WORKER_IP" hostname
Step 2. Download and copy the model
Tensor parallelism requires both ranks to run the same software against the same weights. Start by pulling the pinned image on both Stations:
docker pull "$IMG" &&
docker image inspect "$IMG" --format '{{.Architecture}} {{.RepoDigests}}'
The pull can take several minutes. Matching arm64 digests mean both ranks run identical software.
On A, download the pinned checkpoint. At about 464 GB, it takes roughly an hour at 1 Gb/s, or about six minutes at 10 Gb/s:
mkdir -p "$MODEL_HOST"
docker run --rm --network host -v "$MODEL_HOST:/model" \
  --entrypoint bash "$IMG" -lc \
  "exec hf download '$MODEL_REPO' --revision '$MODEL_REV' --local-dir /model"
When hf download prints Downloaded, copy the files to B. On A:
ssh "$WORKER_IP" "mkdir -p '$MODEL_HOST'" &&
rsync -aH --info=progress2 --exclude '.cache/' \
  "$MODEL_HOST/" "$WORKER_IP:$MODEL_HOST/"
Each disk keeps the full checkpoint; vLLM partitions the weights across the GPUs at load time, with no further quantization.
On A, compare both copies (the check reads B’s file list over SSH), then validate the checkpoint:
(
  set -euo pipefail
  L=$(find "$MODEL_HOST" -path '*/.cache' -prune -o \
    -type f -printf '%P %s\n' | LC_ALL=C sort | sha256sum)
  R=$(ssh "$WORKER_IP" "set -o pipefail; find '$MODEL_HOST' \
    -path '*/.cache' -prune -o -type f -printf '%P %s\\n' | \
    LC_ALL=C sort | sha256sum")
  test "$L" = "$R"
  test "$(find "$MODEL_HOST" -maxdepth 1 \
    -name 'model-*-of-00050.safetensors' | wc -l)" = 50
  python3 -c "import json; c=json.load(open('$MODEL_HOST/config.json')); \
assert c['architectures']==['GlmMoeDsaForCausalLM']; \
assert c['quantization_config']['quant_algo']=='MIXED_PRECISION'"
  echo MODEL_READY
)
MODEL_READY means both Stations hold the same file names and sizes in the expected layout. MIXED_PRECISION is expected: NVFP4 quantization targets MoE-block linear weights and activations, not every tensor.
Step 3. Launch the distributed service
This step turns two machines into one service: the same launch script runs on both Stations, reads ~/glm53.env, and starts one vLLM rank on each. Two settings matter most if you adapt it: --max-model-len 98304, the context ceiling in tokens, which covers input plus output, and --gpu-memory-utilization 0.93, which gives vLLM 93% of GPU memory. If you change other flags, change one at a time and test again.
For the latest recommended configurations, see NVIDIA’s Local AI recipe repository.
On both Stations, create a file named ~/launch-glm53.sh:
nano ~/launch-glm53.sh
The script enables multi-token prediction (MTP), a form of speculative decoding: the model’s MTP layer drafts four tokens per step, and vLLM verifies them with the full model before accepting them, so each step can produce several tokens. The gain depends on how often drafts are accepted, which varies with the prompt.
FP8 KV-cache storage halves cache memory compared with 16-bit storage, leaving room for more tokens. Prefix caching is enabled to avoid reprocessing prompt prefixes the server has already seen. Paste the script below; it checks your configuration, finds the RoCE GID index for the selected rail, and starts this Station’s vLLM rank in a container:
set -euo pipefail
source "$HOME/glm53.env"
case "$RANK" in 0|1) ;; *) echo "Invalid RANK" >&2; exit 1;; esac
test -n "$GPU_UUID"
test -f "$MODEL_HOST/config.json"
GID_INDEX=$(/usr/sbin/show_gids 2>/dev/null |
  awk -v d="$RAIL_HCA" -v ip="$NODE_IP" \
  '$1==d && $5==ip && $6=="v2" {g=$3} END {if(g!="") print g}')
test -n "$GID_INDEX"
HEADLESS=
if [ "$RANK" = 1 ]; then HEADLESS=--headless; fi
SPEC_CONFIG='{"method":"mtp","num_speculative_tokens":4}'
KERNEL_CONFIG='{"enable_flashinfer_autotune":false,"moe_backend":"flashinfer_cutlass"}'
COMPILE_CONFIG='{"cudagraph_mode":"FULL_DECODE_ONLY","cudagraph_capture_sizes":[5,10,20,40,80],"pass_config":{"fuse_allreduce_rms":false}}'
docker run -d --name "$CONTAINER" \
  --gpus device="$GPU_UUID" --network host --ipc host --shm-size 32g \
  --device /dev/infiniband --cap-add IPC_LOCK \
  --ulimit memlock=-1 --ulimit stack=67108864 \
  -v "$MODEL_HOST:/model:ro" \
  -e RANK="$RANK" -e HEADLESS="$HEADLESS" \
  -e MASTER_ADDR="$HEAD_IP" -e MODEL_REPO="$MODEL_REPO" -e PORT="$PORT" \
  -e SPEC_CONFIG="$SPEC_CONFIG" -e KERNEL_CONFIG="$KERNEL_CONFIG" \
  -e COMPILE_CONFIG="$COMPILE_CONFIG" \
  -e NCCL_CUMEM_ENABLE=1 -e NCCL_IB_HCA="$RAIL_HCA" \
  -e NCCL_IB_GID_INDEX="$GID_INDEX" \
  -e NCCL_IB_TIMEOUT=22 -e NCCL_IB_RETRY_CNT=7 \
  -e VLLM_ALLREDUCE_USE_FLASHINFER=0 -e VLLM_ALLREDUCE_USE_SYMM_MEM=0 \
  -e NCCL_SOCKET_IFNAME="$RAIL_IFACE" -e GLOO_SOCKET_IFNAME="$RAIL_IFACE" \
  -e NCCL_NET_GDR_LEVEL=5 -e NCCL_DEBUG=INFO -e NCCL_DEBUG_SUBSYS=INIT,NET \
  -e NCCL_DEBUG_FILE='/tmp/nccl_%h_%p.log' -e VLLM_HOST_IP="$NODE_IP" \
  -e VLLM_EXECUTE_MODEL_TIMEOUT_SECONDS=1800 \
  -e PYTORCH_CUDA_ALLOC_CONF=expandable_segments:True \
  -e VLLM_ALLOW_LONG_MAX_MODEL_LEN=1 -e FLASHINFER_DISABLE_VERSION_CHECK=1 \
  --entrypoint bash "$IMG" -lc '
    set -euo pipefail  
    # Use NCCL 2.30.7 from the image: older versions can deadlock
    # cross-node CUDA graph replays over RoCE (vLLM issue #52504).
    export VLLM_NCCL_SO_PATH=$(python3 -c "import nvidia.nccl,os;
print(os.path.join(list(nvidia.nccl.__path__)[0], \"lib\", \"libnccl.so.2\"))")
    test -f "$VLLM_NCCL_SO_PATH"
    EXTRA=()
    if [ -n "$HEADLESS" ]; then EXTRA+=("$HEADLESS"); fi
    exec vllm serve /model --served-model-name "$MODEL_REPO" \
      --host 0.0.0.0 --port "$PORT" \
      --nnodes 2 --node-rank "$RANK" --master-addr "$MASTER_ADDR" \
      --master-port 29501 --tensor-parallel-size 2 \
      --speculative-config "$SPEC_CONFIG" \
      --max-model-len 98304  --max-num-batched-tokens 8192 \
      --block-size 128 --max-num-seqs 16 \
      --trust-remote-code --disable-custom-all-reduce \
      --kv-cache-dtype fp8 --enable-prefix-caching \
      --gpu-memory-utilization 0.93 \
      --enable-prompt-tokens-details \
      --kernel-config "$KERNEL_CONFIG" --compilation-config "$COMPILE_CONFIG" \
      --distributed-timeout-seconds 1800 \
      --enable-auto-tool-choice --tool-call-parser glm47 --reasoning-parser glm47 \
      "${EXTRA[@]}"
  '
Run the script on B first, then on A:
bash ~/launch-glm53.sh
Each command prints a container ID and returns immediately; the service is not ready yet. B’s worker waits for A to join, then each Station loads its share of the weights from local storage, prepares kernels, and captures CUDA graphs. This takes several minutes.
On A, follow the log to see when the service is ready (--since hides output from earlier runs):
docker logs --follow --since "$(docker inspect \
  --format '{{.State.StartedAt}}' "$CONTAINER")" "$CONTAINER"
When the log shows Application startup complete, press Ctrl+C to stop following it; the service keeps running. 
At this point one model spans two machines. Neither GPU holds a complete copy, and neither can answer a request alone: every token needs both ranks to exchange partial results over the RoCE rail.
The remaining GPU memory holds the KV cache, which stores the context of in-flight requests. The startup log reports its capacity (GPU KV cache size). All requests in flight share it, so longer prompts or more users need more of it. If you run out, lower --max-model-len or send fewer requests at once.
Step 4. Verify serving and send a request
On A, check the API (127.0.0.1 is A itself):
docker exec "$CONTAINER" curl -fsS --max-time 10 \
  "http://127.0.0.1:$PORT/health" >/dev/null && echo HEALTH_OK
HEALTH_OK proves that Station A answers. Next, on both Stations, check that the ranks communicate over GPU RDMA rather than falling back to TCP sockets. The check reads the NCCL logs that the launcher writes through NCCL_DEBUG=INFO, and the same block shows the loaded memory:
docker exec "$CONTAINER" bash -lc '
  set -euo pipefail
  files=(/tmp/nccl_*.log)
  test -e "${files[0]}"
  grep -hE "NET/IB.*GDRDMA|GPU Direct RDMA Enabled" "${files[@]}" >/dev/null
  grep -hF "$NCCL_IB_HCA" "${files[@]}" >/dev/null
  if grep -hE "NET/Socket|NCCL WARN|NCCL ERROR" "${files[@]}"; then
    exit 1
  fi
  echo RDMA_CHECK_OK
'
nvidia-smi
RDMA_CHECK_OK means the check passed with no warnings. In nvidia-smi, A shows the VLLM::Worker_TP0 process and B shows VLLM::Worker_TP1, one rank per GPU. The startup log reports about 218 GiB of weights per GPU, roughly half the checkpoint, which confirms that the model is split across the two GPUs.
On A, send a chat request through the OpenAI-compatible API:
docker exec "$CONTAINER" curl -fsS --max-time 120 \
  "http://127.0.0.1:$PORT/v1/chat/completions" \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"model":"nvidia/GLM-5.3-NVFP4",
    "messages":[{"role":"user","content":"Quarterly units sold: Q1 = 120; Q2 = 180; Q3 = 150. Summarize the trend in one sentence and calculate the percentage change from Q1 to Q2."}],
    "temperature":0,"max_tokens":4096,
    "chat_template_kwargs":{"reasoning_effort":"low"}}' |
python3 -c 'import json,sys; c=json.load(sys.stdin)["choices"][0];
print(c["message"]["content"]); print("finish_reason:",c["finish_reason"])'
The response should look like this:
**Trend:** Units sold rose sharply in Q2 before partially declining in Q3, remaining above the Q1 level.
**Q1 → Q2 percentage change:** (180 - 120) / 120 × 100 = **50% increase**
finish_reason: stop
finish_reason: stop means the model finished its answer instead of hitting the token limit. The request sets reasoning_effort to low in chat_template_kwargs. GLM-5.3 also accepts high and max, which let it reason longer before answering, at the cost of latency. Because the launcher enables tool calling (--enable-auto-tool-choice), agent frameworks that use the OpenAI tools API can call the same endpoint.
Step 5. Concurrency and release resources
AIPerf, the NVIDIA benchmarking tool for inference endpoints, measures throughput and latency. Installing it inside the serving container can change vLLM’s dependencies, so on A, install it in its own virtual environment:
sudo apt update &&
sudo apt install -y python3.12-venv python3.12-dev build-essential &&
python3.12 -m venv "$HOME/.venvs/aiperf-0.12.0" &&
"$HOME/.venvs/aiperf-0.12.0/bin/pip" install 'aiperf==0.12.0' &&
"$HOME/.venvs/aiperf-0.12.0/bin/pip" check
The install should end with No broken requirements found.
AIPerf can send synthetic prompts or replay an existing dataset. This tutorial uses the 80 Coding conversations from SPEED-Bench, a set of real prompts built to evaluate speculative decoding, so MTP is measured on realistic text. SPEED-Bench’s preparation script fetches these prompts from several Hugging Face datasets, including the gated cais/hle: if you haven’t already, open its page and select Agree and access repository before you continue. Then, on A, log in to Hugging Face:
"$HOME/.venvs/aiperf-0.12.0/bin/hf" auth login
Next, download and prepare the dataset. See the official AIPerf guide for details:
SPEED_BENCH_DIR="$HOME/datasets/speed-bench"
mkdir -p "$SPEED_BENCH_DIR"
curl -fLsS https://raw.githubusercontent.com/NVIDIA-NeMo/Skills/refs/heads/main/nemo_skills/dataset/speed-bench/prepare.py | \
  "$HOME/.venvs/aiperf-0.12.0/bin/python" - --config qualitative --output_dir "$SPEED_BENCH_DIR"
On A, check the dataset and service. Continue when the check prints BENCHMARK_READY:
(
  set -euo pipefail
  source ~/glm53.env
  curl -fsS "http://$HEAD_IP:$PORT/health" >/dev/null
  "$HOME/.venvs/aiperf-0.12.0/bin/python" - <<'PY_CHECK'
import json
from pathlib import Path
root = Path.home()
path = root / "datasets/speed-bench/qualitative.jsonl"
assert path.exists(), "Dataset missing: rerun the SPEED-Bench preparation"
rows = [json.loads(line) for line in path.read_text().splitlines() if line.strip()]
coding = [row for row in rows if row["category"] == "coding"]
assert len(coding) == 80, "Expected 80 Coding conversations"
assert sum(len(row["messages"]) for row in coding) == 89
assert all("FULL BENCHMARK DATA SHOULD BE FETCHED" not in message["content"]
           for row in coding for message in row["messages"])
print("BENCHMARK_READY: dataset has 80 Coding conversations, 89 turns in total.")
PY_CHECK
)
The full sweep covers concurrency 1, 8, 16, and 32. At each level, AIPerf sends 3×C warmup requests, then measures 10×C requests, where C is the concurrency. Some conversations are sent more than once at higher concurrency.
Benchmark requests use the model’s default reasoning effort (max) and an 8,192-token output cap; responses can finish before reaching it. Full sweeps can take several hours.
 A two-hour request timeout leaves room for long responses, --server-metrics collects server counters used to measure MTP acceptance, and --export-level raw keeps each response for inspection. 
For a quick test, run the block below on A with both containers running and no other API traffic. It tests C1 and C8 with prefix caching enabled, as in the reference measurements in Table 2, below.
(
  set -euo pipefail
  source ~/glm53.env
  CODING_DATASET="$HOME/datasets/speed-bench/qualitative.jsonl"
  test -f "$CODING_DATASET"
 export CONCURRENCIES=${CONCURRENCIES:-"1 8"} # Quick run: C1 and C8.
  # Optional full benchmark: follow the reproduction instructions below.
  # Step 3: --max-num-seqs 16 -> export CONCURRENCIES="1 8 16"
  # Step 3: --max-num-seqs 32 -> export CONCURRENCIES=32
  RUN_DIR="$HOME/aiperf-results/blog-coding-$(date +%Y%m%d-%H%M%S)"
  for C in $CONCURRENCIES;  do
    ART="$RUN_DIR/c$C"
    env -u HF_HUB_OFFLINE -u TRANSFORMERS_OFFLINE \
      "$HOME/.venvs/aiperf-0.12.0/bin/aiperf" profile \
      -m "$MODEL_REPO" --url "http://$HEAD_IP:$PORT" \
      --endpoint-type chat --streaming --tokenizer "$MODEL_HOST" \
      --custom-dataset-type speed_bench_coding --input-file "$CODING_DATASET" \
      --request-count "$((10 * C))" --warmup-request-count "$((3 * C))" \
      --osl 8192 \
      --use-server-token-count --concurrency "$C" --random-seed 42 \
      --request-timeout-seconds 7200 \
      --server-metrics "http://$HEAD_IP:$PORT/metrics" \
      --export-level raw --artifact-dir "$ART"
    printf "Results: %s\n" "$ART"
  done
  printf "Results directory: %s\n" "$RUN_DIR"
)
AIPerf saves metrics, raw responses, and logs in the results directory. Warmup requests are excluded from the reported metrics.
Optional: Reproduce Table 2. In ~/launch-glm53.sh from Step 3 on both Stations, keep --enable-prefix-caching and set --max-num-seqs to 16 or 32 for the corresponding group shown in the comments above. Before each group, stop and remove the current containers on A, then B (docker stop --timeout 60 "$CONTAINER", followed by docker rm "$CONTAINER"). Relaunch the updated script on B, then A with bash ~/launch-glm53.sh and wait for Application startup complete. On A, run the matching export CONCURRENCIES command from the comments, then rerun the AIPerf block.
To test without prefix caching, replace --enable-prefix-caching with --no-enable-prefix-caching in the Step 3 script on both Stations, then recreate the containers as above. Performance may differ from Table 2.
Figure 2 compares overall output throughput across measured requests with mean per-user throughput. Token counts include reasoning.
Table 2, below, shows how throughput and latency change as more requests share the service.
Overall throughput peaks at 607.8 tok/s at C16. It falls to 385.0 tok/s at C32, where mean request latency is 453.5 s. Higher concurrency also reduces mean per-user throughput, from 115.3 tok/s at C1 to 13.0 tok/s at C32.
After the requests finish, stop the vLLM container on A, then on B:
docker stop --timeout 60 "$CONTAINER"
docker inspect --format \
  'Status={{.State.Status}} Running={{.State.Running}}' "$CONTAINER"
nvidia-smi
The status should read Status=exited Running=false, with no vLLM processes in nvidia-smi; the checkpoint, containers, and results stay on disk. To resume, run docker start "$CONTAINER" on B, then A, and repeat the Step 4 checks.
Troubleshoot common blockers
- Missing variables or SSH failure: source ~/glm53.env after reconnecting; verify A-to-B key authentication and B’s fingerprint.
- SPEED-Bench preparation fails with a gated-dataset error: sign in to Hugging Face with the same account, open the cais/hle page, select Agree and access repository, then rerun the preparation.
- Startup or RDMA check failure: inspect docker logs "$CONTAINER" on both nodes; verify the checkpoint and the GID, and rerun the playbook’s validation (script 07).
- CUDA out of memory: lower --max-model-len or concurrency, then retest.
Put the endpoint to work
You now have a 753-billion-parameter model running behind one local API on two NVIDIA DGX Stations, with every weight in GPU memory. That opens up work that’s hard to do on a hosted endpoint: running inference on sensitive or proprietary data, prototyping agents locally, and iterating on prompts without per-token costs.
Connect your own application using the vLLM serving documentation, then rerun AIPerf with your prompts to find the concurrency that fits your workload. A frontier-scale model is now ready to build on, right from your desk.

====================================================================================================

Show HN: Agweb – a browser for agents that stays out of your way
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
14:08:12 UTC
23:08:12 KST

A browser for coding agents on macOS, built on WebKit (Safari's engine). I run several coding agents at once, and Playwright's Chrome windows kept stealing my keyboard, while headless browsers got blocked by some sites and left me guessing what went wrong. agweb is one window that never takes focus: agents drive it from the shell with agweb, a playwright-cli-style command, and you watch them all live.
Idle tabs are suspended: on my Mac five open pages sit at 0% CPU, where Chrome's GPU process kept five to seven cores busy.
- Never steals focus. No window comes to the front, nothing pops up. Dialogs, changing tabs, file pickers and downloads are answered in code.
- You can always see it. The dashboard (⌘D) shows every tab of every agent at once, live, with each agent's last command. And it is always a real window: some sites block headless browsers, and headless leaves you guessing what went wrong.
- Many agents at once. Each agent works in its own workspace with its own tabs, grouped by agent in the sidebar.
- Log in once. A named profile (-p work) keeps its logins and is shared by every agent using it, at the same time.
- Light. Idle tabs are throttled and then suspended, so open pages cost almost nothing once nobody uses them.
- Acts like a person. Clicks and keys are native macOS events, and the pointer moves like a hand, so hover menus and drag-and-drop work.
- Made for agents. Snapshots give each element a ref that lasts while it is on the page. snapshot --diff and find show only what matters, and every action reports what it changed.
- Passwords stay out of the transcript. fill --secret-cmd 'pass show site' types a secret the agent never sees in replies. 1Password and Bitwarden CLIs work too.
- No ads. Ads and trackers are blocked with uBlock Origin's lists, which agweb downloads every 4 days. An agent testing analytics can turn it off for its own workspace (agweb -w task adblock off).
- Demo videos. video-start records a tab to MP4, with the pointer and clicks drawn in.
Needs macOS 26 or later. Download Agweb.dmg from Releases, drag Agweb to Applications and open it. Then Help ▸ Install Command Line Tool and Help ▸ Install Agent Skill (for Claude Code and Codex). Both are links into the app, so they update with it.
To build from source instead (Xcode 26 or later):
./scripts/build.sh
build/Agweb.app/Contents/Helpers/agweb install-cli
agweb install-skillagweb -w task open en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WebKit   # a workspace for this task; starts the app if needed
agweb -w task snapshot                            # the page as a tree with [ref=eN] handles
agweb -w task click 'role=button[name="Main menu"]'
The click's reply already says what it did, so the agent needs no second snapshot:
### Changes
  - navigation "Site" [ref=e2]
-   - button "Main menu" [ref=e3]
+   - button "Main menu" [checked] [expanded] [ref=e3]
+   - list [ref=e1167]
+     - listitem [ref=e1168]
+       - link "Main page" [ref=e1169]
agweb help lists every command. Settings (⌘,) has text size, dashboard columns, a CPU and memory readout and the ad blocker switch.
Same commands where possible, so agents already know them; a Playwright name it lacks suggests its own (scroll-to → scroll-into-view). On top:
- Replies say what changed: fetches started, lines added or removed, dialogs, popups, downloads, console errors. No snapshot after every step, so far fewer tokens.
- Refs last while the element is on the page.
- ask hands the tab to you for a login, MFA code or CAPTCHA, without taking focus.
- Actions wait for the fetches they caused, so agents rarely sleep.
- text returns the page's article as markdown.
- Shared logins: agents on one profile run at once; a Chrome profile opens in one browser only.
- WebKit only: no Chrome DevTools Protocol, so Playwright and Chrome extensions can't attach, and Chrome-only sites may misbehave.
- route mocks fetch and XHR only; requests lists images, scripts and styles by URL only (--static).
- The ad blocker can't run uBlock's scripts, so YouTube ads and some "turn off your ad blocker" walls get through.
- For what it can't do, use Playwright.
./scripts/selftest.sh runs every command against a local page in a separate instance; swift test runs the unit tests. AGWEB_ROOT=/tmp/dir starts a second instance beside your own that keeps everything in that folder. Defuddle (MIT) is bundled for text.

====================================================================================================
