Show HN: Lampka – a Windows PDF reader whose dark mode keeps figures in colour
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
14:06:56 UTC
23:06:56 KST

Built for theses, papers, reports and bundles
Long documents keep sending you to a reference, a footnote or an exhibit. Lampka takes you there and brings you back to where you were.
Citations without losing your place Pro
Hover over a citation, figure or link to see where it leads. Click through, and Back brings you straight home.
Two places at once Pro
Split view shows the same document twice: the text beside its footnotes, references or exhibits. Links can open in the other pane.
Listen when your eyes are tired Pro
Read aloud with your computer's own voices, offline. It follows along sentence by sentence, skips headers and citation numbers, and remembers where you stopped.
Page numbers that match the print Free
Lampka uses the document's own page labels, so "see page xii" or "A-14" takes you to the right page.
Keep your line Free
A reading ruler marks the lines you're on, or dims everything else, so dense pages stay easy to follow. Let it follow your mouse, or keep it still while the page scrolls under it.
Turn sideways tables Free
Turn a single page to read a wide table, and print in your reading colours. Your PDF itself is never changed.
Easy on your eyes after dark
Choose a night theme, or tell Lampka when your evening starts and it switches by itself. Text and paper change colour while photos, diagrams and coloured drawings keep theirs, so charts and illustrated manuals still make sense. How it works
Brightness and contrast are remembered separately for each theme, so night can be dimmer than day without fiddling every evening.
Make a theme your own, and share it
Pick any text and background colours, give them a name, and they join the list next to Day and Night, ready for evening mode too. Brightness and contrast are saved with them.
Found a combination that's easy on the eyes? Share it as a small file and anyone you send it to can add it with one click. A theme file holds only a name and colours.
Opens exactly where you stopped
Every PDF you read sits in your library with the page you're on and how far through you are. Close Lampka mid-sentence and next time you're back on that line.
It recognises documents by their contents, so your place and notes survive renaming or moving the file.
Highlight it like it's paper Pro
Drag across a sentence to highlight it in one of five colours, add a note, or bookmark the page. Everything collects in one list you can jump through, and exports as tidy Markdown for your study notes.
Highlights are kept by Lampka, so the PDF itself is never modified.
Read it like a book
Two-page view pairs left and right pages the way they were printed, and Page Down turns a whole spread. The table of contents follows along, so you always know which chapter you're in.
And the small things
Find anything
Press Ctrl+F and every match lights up, with a count as you step through them.
Fit the way you read
Fit the page width, or the whole page so Page Up and Down flip exactly one page.
Take it with you
Export your settings, library and notes as one file and bring them to another computer.
Comments from others
See the notes and replies other people left in a PDF, with the text they refer to.
Your reading stays yours
The privacy policy has the details.
Works offline, without an account
Lampka never sends anything about you or your reading to anyone.
Your PDFs stay where you keep them
Lampka never changes, copies or uploads them.
Your notes stay with you
They're kept on your computer, or in your own cloud storage if you turn on sync.
Never sold
If you join the email list or buy Pro, your details are used for that and nothing else.
Respects the author's wishes
If a PDF doesn't allow printing or copying, Lampka follows that.
Reading is free, and Pro is paid for once
Everything is free during the beta. After that, reading stays free, and the study and listening tools are a one-time purchase. Every new install gets 14 days of Pro to try.
Lampka
Free
Always free for reading
- All reading themes, true dark pages and evening mode
- A library that remembers your place in every document
- Links with Back, contents, bookmarks and find
- Printed page numbers, reading ruler, two-page view
- Turning pages, printing in your colours
- The PDF's own comments and replies
- Send feedback and bug reports any time
Download the beta
Lampka Pro
one-time
Pay once and get every 1.x update.
- Everything in Lampka, plus:
- Citation, figure and link previews
- Split view
- Read aloud, offline
- Highlighter, notes and Markdown export
- Sync progress, notes and settings between computers via your own OneDrive or Dropbox
- Desk patterns around the page
- Personal support by email

one-time
Lampka Pro at the early-supporter price, until 1.0 comes out. Pro will then be . One payment covers every 1.x update.
- Everything in Lampka, plus:
- Citation, figure and link previews
- Split view
- Read aloud, offline

Everything is free during the beta. Your key keeps Pro unlocked after it ends. The details.
On sale soon
Prices include VAT where it applies. You may see your own currency at checkout.
Full refund within 30 days, no questions asked.
40% off for students and people who use disability support: how to ask.
Try the free beta
Lampka for Windows 10 and 11 is free while it's in beta. Try it on something long and let me know how it goes.
On a Mac? A Mac version comes later. Leave your email and you'll hear when it's ready.
Lampka is code-signed. As it's new, Windows may show "Windows protected your PC" the first time: click More info, then Run anyway. Other messages? See below.
The browser says the file "isn't commonly downloaded"
Browsers say this about any new program until many people have downloaded it. Open the browser's downloads list, click the … menu next to Lampka-Setup and choose Keep. Edge may ask once more: choose Show more, then Keep anyway.
To check that the file is the genuine one, compare its SHA-256 fingerprint with the one on the release page.
Windows says "Windows protected your PC"
That's Microsoft Defender SmartScreen. It warns about programs that few people have downloaded yet, even signed ones, and Lampka is new. Click More info; the window then shows the app name, Lampka-Setup, the publisher and a Run anyway button. Click it. You'll only see this the first time, and Windows stops showing it as more people install Lampka.
If the publisher says "Unknown publisher", don't run it: download Lampka again from this page.
Windows says "Smart App Control blocked an app"
Lampka is signed, so Smart App Control should let it run. If it blocks Lampka, delete the file, download it again from this page, and try once more. Still blocked? Email support@lampka.app.
Installing on Windows
- Run the downloaded Lampka-Setup file and follow the steps. It installs just for you; no administrator rights needed.
- Choose whether you want a desktop shortcut and whether Lampka should appear in the "Open with" list for PDF files.
- Start Lampka, then right-click its taskbar icon and choose Pin to taskbar if you like.
To remove Lampka, use Settings, Apps. Your notes and library are kept unless you delete them yourself.
Lampka news by email
Occasional emails with Lampka news, new features and offers, including when the Mac version is ready. Unsubscribe in one click. See how your email is used.
Questions
Is it really free?
Yes. Reading is always free. During the beta, the Pro tools are free too. Each beta build works for 90 days, and a newer one gives you another 90. After the beta, Pro is a one-time purchase, not a subscription.
Does it run on a Mac?
Not yet. The beta is for Windows 10 and 11. A Mac version is planned; leave your email above to hear when it's ready.
Will Lampka change my PDFs?
No. Themes, highlights, notes and bookmarks are stored by Lampka on your computer. The original files are never touched, so they look the same in any other app.
Where are my highlights and notes kept?
In Lampka's own folder on your computer. Use File, then Export settings, to back them up or move them to another machine.
Does it collect any data?
No. The app works offline and doesn't need an account, so nothing about you or your reading leaves your computer. If you let it, Lampka also checks lampka.app once a day for a new version, and that check sends nothing about you. It keeps a small log on your computer to help explain a crash, without file names or document text. It only reaches me if you send a report. The privacy policy explains the few things this website and email sign-up involve.
How do I get my key?
By email from support@lampka.app, within 24 hours of buying. Paste it into Help > Enter licence key. It works offline.
Can I use Pro on more than one computer?
Yes. One licence covers all the computers you use yourself.
What happens when the beta ends?
Reading stays free. Pro features need a licence, and your highlights and notes stay either way.
Can I get a refund?
Yes. If Lampka Pro isn't right for you, ask within 30 days of buying and you'll get your money back, no questions asked. See refunds.
Is there a discount?
Yes: 40% off Lampka Pro for students and for people who use a disability support service. Email a short proof of eligibility and you'll get a personal code. See how to ask.
Can it edit or sign PDFs?
Not yet. Lampka is built for reading and studying. If you need to edit PDFs, keep your current editor for that and use Lampka for the reading.
Something doesn't work. How do I tell you?
In Lampka, open Help and choose Send feedback…. It opens an email with some technical details, which you can read and change before sending. It never includes file names. Or email support@lampka.app with what happened and, if you can, the PDF that caused it. I read every report.
What support do I get?
Everyone can send feedback and bug reports, and they are all read. Lampka Pro owners also get a personal answer by email, usually within a day or two. With the free version I can't promise a one-to-one reply to every message.

Show HN: Fursoy – Out-of-order RV64IMAFDV core in SystemVerilog (Linux 6.6)
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
14:06:34 UTC
23:06:34 KST

A 64-bit Out-of-Order Superscalar RISC-V Processor (RV64IMAFDV + Zba + Zbb + Zicsr + Zifencei) booting mainline Linux 6.6.50 and BusyBox userspace under Sv39 virtual memory.
Fursoy Slice-1 (fursoy_s1_core / fursoy_s1_soc) is a clean-slate 64-bit out-of-order superscalar RISC-V processor implemented in SystemVerilog, featuring Sv39 demand paging, machine/supervisor/user privilege separation, cycle-exact hardware performance telemetry, and 500 MHz Nangate 45nm standard-cell synthesis closure across 8 core functional blocks.
- 2-Wide Superscalar OoO Core (fursoy_s1_core):
  - 2-wide fetch, decode, register rename, and in-order retirement (M/S/U privilege modes, medeleg/mideleg, WFI, MRET/SRET).
  - 128-entry Reorder Buffer (ROB_DEPTH = 128) with 4-bit wrap-safe epoch tags and precise exception/interrupt flush recovery.
  - 160-entry Physical Integer Register File (PRF_SIZE = 160, 128 speculative free-list entries) plus 8 branch checkpoints (NUM_CHECKPOINTS = 8) for single-cycle speculative RAT rollback.
  - Dedicated issue queues: 32-entry Integer IQ (INT_IQ_DEPTH = 32), 32-entry Memory IQ (MEM_IQ_DEPTH = 32), FP IQ (FPRF_SIZE = 64), and decoupled 8-entry Vector IQ (VLEN = 256, ELEN = 64, 48 vector physical registers).
  - Parallel-prefix 64-bit Kogge-Stone fursoy_s1_alu and fursoy_s1_branch_unit (single-cycle execution + carry-free target equality), 3-stage pipelined multiplier, and hardware divider.
- Sv39 Virtual Memory & Non-Blocking Caches:
  - Dual-port Sv39 DTLB: 64-entry 4-way set-associative base TLB (16 sets, Tree-PLRU) + 8-entry 2 MiB / 1 GiB superpage CAM with 32-entry Translation Request Table (TRT_DEPTH = 32).
  - Sv39 ITLB: 64-entry 4-way set-associative base TLB + 8-entry superpage CAM.
  - Shared Hardware PTW (fursoy_s1_ptw): Walks Sv39 page tables with non-destructive L1D dirty snoop coherency so page-table writes sitting dirty in L1D are observed directly without eviction, paired with SFENCE.VMA store-queue drain and D-cache clean synchronization.
  - L1I Cache (fursoy_s1_l1i): 16 KiB, 4-way set-associative (64 sets × 64 B), VIPT-safe with conservative next-line prefetcher and 2 I-MSHRs.
  - L1D Cache (fursoy_s1_l1d): 16 KiB, 4-way set-associative (64 sets × 64 B), 8-bank (PA[5:3]) non-blocking data cache with 16-entry Load Queue, 16-entry Store Queue, 4 MSHRs, and 2-entry Writeback Buffer.
- SoC & Peripherals (fursoy_s1_soc):
  - 5-to-1 Priority Crossbar: Core MMIO > PTW walks > L1I refills > L1D refills > L1D dirty writebacks, with anti-starvation fairness and unmapped-aperture bus error trapping.
  - Platform Devices: 256 MiB DRAM interface (0x8000_0000), CLINT (0x0200_0000), PLIC (0x0C00_0000, 31 external sources), 16550 UART (0x1000_0000), and custom minimal SBI v2.0 M-mode firmware (Base, TIME, SRST, DBCN).
Evaluated standalone across 8 core functional blocks using Yosys 0.33 + sv2v v0.0.13 + OpenSTA 3.1.0 on NangateOpenCellLibrary (45nm Typical, 1.10V, 25C) targeting 500 MHz (2.000 ns clock period) (tools/run_s1_synth.py --run-synth, full report in build/synth/s1_synth_report.json):
Note: Total standard-cell area across these 8 standalone blocks is 175,106.74 um^2 (NAND2_X1 = 0.798 um^2). Full-SoC place-and-route and SRAM macro compilation are not yet executed.
The Milestone 9 closeout (tools/run_s1_m9_closeout.py, saved in results/s1-m9-closeout.json) boots mainline Linux 6.6.50 in Verilator with zero kernel panics, zero oopses, zero access faults, and 0 CPI conservation errors across 89,861,075 simulated cycles (76,236,390 retired instructions, 2,227,761 U-mode commits, 2,255 demand page faults / 1,027 U-mode, IPC = 0.84838):
[    0.000000] Linux version 6.6.50 ... (riscv64-linux-gnu-gcc 13.3.0)
[    0.000000] SBI specification v2.0 detected
[    0.000000] SBI implementation ID=0xf00501 Version=0x1
[    0.000000] earlycon: uart8250 at MMIO32 0x0000000010000000 (options '')
...
[    6.809584] Run /init as init process
[INIT] Fursoy Slice-0 Linux BusyBox Userspace active!
FURSOY_USERSPACE_BEGIN
[TEST 01] uname ... Linux (none) 6.6.50 riscv64 GNU/Linux
...
[TEST 20] repeated process creation/exit
[TEST 21] userspace memory stress
[MEM_STRESS_START] Running stack and heap multi-width memory validation...
[MEM_STRESS_PASS] All stack, heap, multi-width, and strided tests passed.
FURSOY_USERSPACE_PASS
Every cycle is mutually exclusively owned by one architectural bucket asserted continuously in hardware (sum(C_k) == mcycle = 89,861,075, cpi_conservation_errors = 0):
sudo apt-get update
sudo apt-get install -y \
  verilator g++ python3 device-tree-compiler \
  gcc-riscv64-unknown-elf gcc-riscv64-linux-gnu
(Optional, for physical synthesis & STA: yosys, sv2v, OpenSTA sta, and Nangate45 .lib via FURSOY_LIBERTY or tools/synth/lib/NangateOpenCellLibrary_typical.lib.)
From the repository root:
# 1. Production RTL lint (Verilator --lint-only, expects 0 warnings/errors)
python3 tools/run_s1_lint.py
# 2. Fast Slice-1 verification regressions (runtime ~3-6 min total)
python3 tools/run_s1_m9_phase_a.py   # Interrupts, mideleg, WFI, precise trap boundaries
python3 tools/run_s1_m9_phase_b.py   # CLINT, PLIC, UART 16550 platform peripherals
python3 tools/run_s1_m9_phase_c.py   # 5-master crossbar, DTB, unmapped faults
python3 tools/run_s1_m9_phase_d.py   # SBI v2.0 firmware + mixed-privilege stress
python3 tools/run_s1_m8_closeout.py  # RV64FD + RVV 1.0 FP/Vector backend
python3 tools/run_s1_m7_closeout.py  # Multi-bank non-blocking L1D + coherency
python3 tools/run_s1_m6_closeout.py  # Frontend BPU / ITLB / L1I NLP
# 3. Nangate 45nm @ 500 MHz synthesis & OpenSTA check
python3 tools/run_s1_synth.py --run-synth
# 4. Full Linux 6.6.50 + BusyBox boot closeout (~1.5-2 hours wall time)
# Requires a built RISC-V Image at build/linux-6.6.50/arch/riscv/boot/Image
# (kernel config/patches in software/linux/, initramfs helper in tools/build_initramfs.py).
python3 tools/run_s1_m9_closeout.py

====================================================================================================

The Claude Delusion
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
13:36:17 UTC
22:36:17 KST

November 13th, 2026
Something weird happens to people who are successful. They start to think they're gods.
Actually accomplishing anything is obviously no strict pre-requisite of success. And maybe it's not so much "being successful", but rather "being rich". How do you become rich? Some people are just lucky. Lucky to have a physical talent, lucky to have parents able and willing to nurture your talent, and of course quite often lucky to have been at the right place at the right time1.
Being lucky doesn't mean they didn't also work hard. But even—especially!—the Paris Hiltons of the world, the children of Hollywood superstars, the crooks starting their corrupt empires with "a small loan" (or a share of an emerald mine) from their fathers—they all strongly believe that they deserve their success and then conclude that anybody not as successful only has themselves to blame.
This is especially the case for people in tech, where for years and years being good at programming has been equated with overall (and often: superior) intelligence2, and those illusory superiority effects then amplify rampant Dunning-Kruger to quickly turn "I am good at writing code, and with some luck, I managed to be successful" into a train of thought that goes more like "I am successful. Success is not random, so I must deserve my success. I appear to be a genius, I've mastered coding, anything else is trivial. Ergo, I am good at everything."
Let's take Bill Gates as an example. Now, mind you, Bill Gates certainly has accomplished a lot, and his skillset and professional background includes without a doubt a lot of computer programming and various ruthless monopolistic business tactics. In a recent New York Times interview, he describes how he evaluated OpenAI's level of progress (emphasis mine):
And I was going down to see OpenAI on a regular basis to see the work they were doing, and I challenged them, saying: Hey, if you can read a biology textbook and pass the advanced placement exam — getting a perfect grade, which is a 5 — then you will have proven that you are reading. That is, encoding knowledge in an accessible form. And so it’s six months before the public release that Sam Altman, Greg Brockman and Ilya Sutskever come to my house and demonstrate to me getting a 5 on the A.P. exam, even on questions that I had made up that it couldn't possibly have seen — very complex biology problems. It was nearly perfect. So that was shock No.1.
Now Bill Gates surely has shown an interest in biology and, through his work with the Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation, has probably read a lot of articles and research in this field. But the hubris to think himself a subject matter expert able to make up advanced, novel questions in a scientific field outside his own and suitable to assess AI's true capabilities and then being "shocked" that it could answer these questions makes my point.
Although, what really cooks his noodle is the idea that AI can do what he used to do: write code.
And then late last year, when Anthropic's Claude coding models got super good, I could see that they are as good as I am — it’s significantly my most developed talent, because I was obsessed from age 13 to 24 as to whether I could write code as good or better than anyone. That’s another moment where I went: This is incredible. That the capability of doing long-running, complex tasks has now gotten to the point that they are superhuman at writing code and in finding flaws in code.
Take note of the emphasis I added: the very notion that something else could be "as good as I am" is, first of all, hard to believe and secondly necessarily means that it's "superhuman". That man is not lacking self-esteem, no sirree, Bob.
Likewise, this is also where many of the tech bros appear to be losing their minds, and this thinking ("Anything approaching my skill level is superhuman.") explains a lot about how we ended up with both stories of ultimate doom and human extinction as well as the utopian nirvana where AI solves all of mankind's problems (well, all of the billionaires' problems, which appear to largely converge on pesky mortality).
For most tech nerds, being good at programming computers was a differentiating skill. It was weird, normies didn't understand it at all, you spoke your own language (languages!), you could make a computer do things. You understood how the internet works, and anything that's not working was clearly written by complete and utter morons, and you could easily write this yourself in an afternoon if you wanted to.
So now you've created a computer program that can do the same thing. Your mind boggles! And it speaks! It confirms you, it praises you, it tells you that you're brilliant—clearly evidence that it is infallible itself. But now imagine if it was better than you. What at first seems inconceivable then must strike fear and awe in your heart, for you have created a powerful monster with direction and purpose all its own. (And you sure have consumed enough pulpy science fiction to know that your creature is coming for you eventually.)
"Can you imagine a Beowulf cluster of these?" was an old Slashdot meme before memes were a thing. But the notion of having an army of minions to do your bidding has always appealed to the nerds looking to assert their superiority, and thus we arrived at "agents". "Swarms" of "autonomous" "agents".
This narrative of agency conveniently also relieves you of responsibility. You didn't fail to properly restrict a potentially malicious program you trained to hack other systems with persistence. No, the "agents" went "rogue". You have summoned powerful daemons indeed. Daemons that can produce code faster than you (gasp!), code you don't fully understand (although of course you could, you just don't want to, you don't have time, you have too much going on, what with your homegrown heavily customized harness). It becomes obvious quite quickly that this, then, must be SUPERINTELLIGENCE. Superintelligence that COULD KILL US ALL!
I picked on Bill Gates above to illustrate the overconfidence effect, but of course there is no shortage of much more exaggerated egos making bold statements outside of (or even within) their purported area of expertise, reflecting a complete and utter inability to relate to other people. To them, we're all just NPCs.
The more powerful people are, the less empathy they exhibit. This is neither news nor limited to tech; dog knows there are countless rich assholes outside of tech doing asshole things. But few outside the tech sector suffer the same level of delusion, of thinking themselves solely and uniquely qualified to decide mankind's future, to claim to have birthed a digital Prometheus, to boldly declare (and use as a perpetual shield) a literal Deus ex Machina to cure all diseases—if only they could find the time to prioritize that sort of work over, say, their military contracts.
Pretending to even remotely care about climate change3 while at the same time undoing "15 years' worth of greenhouse gas reductions" and then listing environmental concerns as a risk factor in your S1 is just the cherry on top.
If these are the new American Gods... well, je n'ai jamais eu besoin de cette hypothèse-là.
November 13th, 2026
Footnotes:
[1] The actual citation for "Bill Gates went to one of the only high schools in the world that had a computer" is "The Psychology of Money by Morgan Housel; Malcolm Gladwell discusses this in Outliers as well. ↩
[2] The entire interview circus in the tech world revolved around this notion of exclusivity and brilliant jerks: "Google hires geniuses" was an oft-repeated mantra in Silicon Valley, so logically, if you were hired by Google, you were a genius. (Yes, all >180,000 of Google's employees.) This translates to the other big or exclusive companies, and explains why everybody leaving will always note that they were always surrounded by the most brilliant minds. It's Inverse Groucho Marx Bias. ↩
[3] And hey, we really don't need AI to "figure out how to combat climate change". We already know how to do it. We just have to actually show any willingness to stop it. Unfortunately, that's also exactly where these megalomaniacs get in the way. ↩
Links:

For most tech nerds, being good at programming computers was a differentiating skill. It was weird, normies didn't understand it at all, you spoke your own language (languages!), you could make a computer do things. You understood how the internet works, and anything that's not working was clearly written by complete and utter morons, and you could easily write this yourself in an afternoon if you wanted to.
So now you've created a computer program that can do the same thing. Your mind boggles! And it speaks! It confirms you, it praises you, it tells you that you're brilliant—clearly evidence that it is infallible itself. But now imagine if it was better than you. What at first seems inconceivable then must strike fear and awe in your heart, for you have created a powerful monster with direction and purpose all its own. (And you sure have consumed enough pulpy science fiction to know that your creature is coming for you eventually.)
"Can you imagine a Beowulf cluster of these?" was an old Slashdot meme before memes were a thing. But the notion of having an army of minions to do your bidding has always appealed to the nerds looking to assert their superiority, and thus we arrived at "agents". "Swarms" of "autonomous" "agents".
This narrative of agency conveniently also relieves you of responsibility. You didn't fail to properly restrict a potentially malicious program you trained to hack other systems with persistence. No, the "agents" went "rogue". You have summoned powerful daemons indeed. Daemons that can produce code faster than you (gasp!), code you don't fully understand (although of course you could, you just don't want to, you don't have time, you have too much going on, what with your homegrown heavily customized harness). It becomes obvious quite quickly that this, then, must be SUPERINTELLIGENCE. Superintelligence that COULD KILL US ALL!
I picked on Bill Gates above to illustrate the overconfidence effect, but of course there is no shortage of much more exaggerated egos making bold statements outside of (or even within) their purported area of expertise, reflecting a complete and utter inability to relate to other people. To them, we're all just NPCs.
The more powerful people are, the less empathy they exhibit. This is neither news nor limited to tech; dog knows there are countless rich assholes outside of tech doing asshole things. But few outside the tech sector suffer the same level of delusion, of thinking themselves solely and uniquely qualified to decide mankind's future, to claim to have birthed a digital Prometheus, to boldly declare (and use as a perpetual shield) a literal Deus ex Machina to cure all diseases—if only they could find the time to prioritize that sort of work over, say, their military contracts.
Pretending to even remotely care about climate change3 while at the same time undoing "15 years' worth of greenhouse gas reductions" and then listing environmental concerns as a risk factor in your S1 is just the cherry on top.
If these are the new American Gods... well, je n'ai jamais eu besoin de cette hypothèse-là.
November 13th, 2026
Footnotes:
[1] The actual citation for "Bill Gates went to one of the only high schools in the world that had a computer" is "The Psychology of Money by Morgan Housel; Malcolm Gladwell discusses this in Outliers as well. ↩
[2] The entire interview circus in the tech world revolved around this notion of exclusivity and brilliant jerks: "Google hires geniuses" was an oft-repeated mantra in Silicon Valley, so logically, if you were hired by Google, you were a genius. (Yes, all >180,000 of Google's employees.) This translates to the other big or exclusive companies, and explains why everybody leaving will always note that they were always surrounded by the most brilliant minds. It's Inverse Groucho Marx Bias. ↩
[3] And hey, we really don't need AI to "figure out how to combat climate change". We already know how to do it. We just have to actually show any willingness to stop it. Unfortunately, that's also exactly where these megalomaniacs get in the way. ↩

A visual explanation of reinforcement learning
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
13:31:06 UTC
22:31:06 KST

To try to answer this question, we’ll build a digital being with a simple brain, and watch it learn a new behavior.
In our previous article, we created a creature called a spark that could steer towards light. And we saw how small changes to its neural circuitry gave rise to very different behaviors.
Back then, we’d wired these circuits into the spark’s brain by hand. But now, we want it to learn the same behaviors by itself.
After all, you didn’t come into this world knowing how to walk, talk or read. While some of your abilities, such as breathing or swallowing, are wired by evolution, you gained others through an arduous process of trial and error.
We want our spark to do the same. To give you an intuition for this learning process, we’re going to hand over the controls to you. We’d like you to wire the spark’s brain.
But first, we need to tell you what you’ll be wiring. The spark’s brain has just four neurons — two neurons serving as its eyes, and two neurons that wiggle its flippers.
There are straight connections between neurons on the same side of its body, crossed connections between neurons on opposite sides, and drive, which controls the spark’s speed when there’s no light.
So its brain wiring boils down to just three numbers — the strength of each kind of connection, and the drive.
Now let’s set this spark loose. Use the sliders below to wire its brain as it moves. We’ll keep score.
Your goal: Make the spark love the light by getting a score above 0.5.
Finding the right configuration takes a lot of trial and error. (If you get stuck, click the hint button above for a nudge in the right direction.)
Let’s visualize what you just did. To guide the spark to the light, you tweaked three numbers. We can represent these numbers as the three axes of a cube.
The payoff of this picture is that we can visualize your spark’s learning trajectory as a path within this cube.
To guide the spark, you probably nudged a slider one way, saw whether this made things better or worse, and then used this feedback to decide your next move.
Let’s distill this into a learning algorithm — a step-by-step procedure by which the spark can rewire its brain through practice.
Here’s how it’ll work: The spark gets many tries to steer towards the light. Before every trial, its brain wiring will change a little at random, just like you wiggled the sliders to and fro.
If the trial goes better than previous ones, then the spark gets a reward and its wiring shifts in the direction of this random change. And if the trial goes worse, the spark gets a punishment, and its wiring shifts the other way.
This isn’t that different from how you learned how to walk or ride a bike. Falling down was your punishment, and the thrill of balancing was your reward.
Can this simple algorithm help the spark find the light?
In the beginning, the spark is a novice, with a brain that’s wired together at random. Unsurprisingly, it doesn’t get very far.
The baby spark can spin in place, but it can’t yet move around. But it’s about to get better…
After 50 tries of this learning algorithm, our infant spark has taken its first steps. And while it won’t break any speed records, it’s now on the move…
After 250 tries, our juvenile spark has picked up speed. But it veers away from the light at the last moment.
After 500 tries, our adolescent spark boldly charges towards the light. But it hasn’t yet figured out how to slow down or stop.
After 2,000 tries, our spark is now an adult — an expert at seeking and following the light. It clings to the light and can turn on a dime. (Try moving the light to see the spark follow it.)
Like before, we can visualize this learning process as a path.
This isn’t a path through physical space. Instead, it’s a path through the space of possible brains. It shows how the spark’s brain changed as it learned.
Other novice sparks would start from different points, and they would each trace out their own learning trajectory.
This tangled web illustrates the learning paths of hundreds of novice sparks, each starting with a differently wired brain. As if by magic, all of these threads converge to the point marked ♥︎.
The paths aren’t straight lines: Learning isn’t linear. There are setbacks, wrong turns and mistakes along the way. And it isn’t necessarily fast. Some of the learners took thousands of tries to reach their goal.
You’ll notice that the paths are all jittery. That’s because every step involves randomness. We can smooth out this jitter by averaging over many trajectories at every point, revealing a map of the flow of learning.
It’s worth taking a moment to appreciate how surprising this is — given enough tries, the same learning process turned every novice into an expert light seeker.
Of the myriad possible arrangements of this spark’s brain, only a tiny fraction generate the behavior that we want. There are far more ways to fail than to succeed.
And yet, you’ve just witnessed a small miracle. The spark navigated this labyrinth of dead ends and rewired its brain to find the light, guided by nothing but reward and punishment.
Learning doesn’t stop once you become an adult. We learn throughout our lives and adapt to changing circumstances.
Our sparks can do this too. For example, what if the light represents a harmful chemical or a dangerous predator instead of a food source?
The spark is now rewarded for avoiding the light rather than for seeking it, and this leads to new behaviors:
If the light is harmful, then LOVE becomes the worst strategy, and the sparks learn to FLEE (escape as fast as possible) or to FREEZE (play dead).
If instead the spark is rewarded for approaching the light with high speed, then it learns to charge towards the light, i.e. to FIGHT.
Much like a child learns from its environment, the spark can learn very different behaviors — fleeing, freezing, fighting or following the light — depending on how it’s rewarded.
You’ve now seen how a novice spark can rewire its brain and learn new behaviors through a process known as reinforcement learning.
Biological brains also learn through reinforcement. For example, baby songbirds learn to sing by babbling. They try out random variations of their song. If a variation gets closer to their memory of an adult’s song, they get a burst of dopamine (and if it gets further away, the dopamine dips). Guided by these rewards and punishments, they can recreate the song from memory, even with no teacher present!
Now, we do have to come clean about something. This spark’s brain had just four neurons and three numbers that determined how these neurons were connected. This simple structure allowed us to watch its brain learn in three dimensions.
Your brain has around 86 billion neurons and over a hundred trillion connections. The space of possible ways in which it could be wired is therefore unimaginably vast.
But while the details are much more complicated, the underlying idea is the same: Learning is how you navigate this vast internal space. And feedback is the compass that points the way.
Thanks for reading chapter two of Myriad, a series exploring how minds emerge from matter. Myriad is a collaboration between Arkarup Banerjee, a neuroscientist who studies behavior, and Aatish Bhatia, a physicist turned science journalist.
The sparks in this article are inspired by the work of the neuroscientist Valentino Braitenberg. We implemented their neural circuitry as follows:
\(\begin{aligned}v_L &= \sigma(\textcolor{#7ee081}{w_s}\, b_L + \textcolor{#c49bff}{w_c}\, b_R + \textcolor{#ff8fb8}{d})\\ v_R &= \sigma(\textcolor{#c49bff}{w_c}\, b_L + \textcolor{#7ee081}{w_s}\, b_R + \textcolor{#ff8fb8}{d})\end{aligned}\)
where \(v_L\) and \(v_R\) are the left and right flipper speeds, \(b_L\) and \(b_R\) are the brightness levels at the left and right eyes, \(\textcolor{#7ee081}{w_s}\) and \(\textcolor{#c49bff}{w_c}\) are the straight and crossed connection strengths between the eyes and the flippers, and \(\textcolor{#ff8fb8}{d}\) is a drive that sets the spark’s speed when there is no light. \(\sigma(x)\) is the ReLU function. The spark’s forward speed is the average of the two flipper speeds, and its turning speed is proportional to their difference.
There are three parameters in our simplified setup: \(\textcolor{#7ee081}{w_s}\textrm{,}\) \(\textcolor{#c49bff}{w_c}\) and \(\textcolor{#ff8fb8}{d}\textrm{.}\) The spark learns values for these parameters using a version of the REINFORCE algorithm published by Ronald Williams in 1992.
Each trial, the parameters were wiggled by a small random amount and the spark moved for 500 time steps, starting from a random point on the edge of the arena. Its reward was the average brightness along its path.
This reward was then compared to a running average of recent rewards. If the spark did better than usual, its parameters were nudged in the direction of this random change. If it did worse, its parameters were nudged in the opposite direction. The size of the nudge was proportional to how much better (or worse) it did:
\(\small \underset{\mathclap{\htmlClass{sans}{\text{change}}}}{\Delta W\vphantom{(\overline{R})}}\hspace{0.3em}=\hspace{0.5em}\underset{\mathclap{\htmlClass{sans}{\color{#66b3ff}\text{step size}}}}{\textcolor{#66b3ff}{\alpha}\vphantom{(\overline{R})}}\hspace{2.1em}\underset{\mathclap{\htmlClass{sans}{\color{#ff9b32}\text{wiggle}}}}{\textcolor{#ff9b32}{\varepsilon}\vphantom{(\overline{R})}}\hspace{1.2em}\underset{\mathclap{\htmlClass{sans}{\color{#ffc832}\text{improvement}}}}{\textcolor{#ffc832}{(R - \overline{R})}\vphantom{(\overline{R})}}\)
Here \(\Delta W\) is the change in the three parameters, \(\textcolor{#66b3ff}{\alpha}\) is the learning rate, \(\textcolor{#ff9b32}{\varepsilon}\) is the random wiggle, and \(\textcolor{#ffc832}{R - \overline{R}}\) is how much better or worse than usual the spark did. We repeated this thousands of times.
Williams’ original approach adds randomness to each neuron’s output. We add it to the weights and biases instead, an approach known as weight perturbation. A similar idea, called evolution strategies, was used by OpenAI in 2017 to teach AI models to play Atari video games.
Sign up below for more rigorously researched visual explanations of neuroscience, and share this article with others who might enjoy it.

The $4 Trader Joe’s Italian Dinner Find That Tastes Like It Came from My Local Trattoria

====================================================================================================

Advancing science and engineering with Antigravity's /teamwork
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
13:20:43 UTC
22:20:43 KST

November 13, 2026
Vahab Mirrokni, Google Fellow and VP, Google Research
We share recent results from Antigravity’s /teamwork multiagent systems, which enhance human-AI collaborations to solve open problems across mathematics, chemistry, systems software, and ML engineering. All of this work is done in collaboration with human experts, and we are making these tools available externally.
Over the past several weeks, teams across Google and academic partner institutions have tackled open problems in math, science, and complex systems engineering that had remained open for years. Using Gemini 4 Argon, Gemini 4 Carbon, Gemini 4 Hydrogen and multi-agent workflows such as Antigravity’s /teamwork (building on various Gemini models), these collaborations have produced new innovations across information theory, formal verifications, molecular structure discoveries, and kernel optimization, along with several production infrastructure gains at Google. We are making these agentic tools available in Antigravity’s /teamwork (alongside /boost and other agents) so that researchers and developers can apply them to their own work.
Frontier research and systems engineering require navigating vast literature, building custom computational pipelines, testing against physical measurements, and verifying every step. Progress comes from an interactive discovery loop where researchers frame questions and define the theoretical or physical constraints, while long-horizon agents write custom solvers, explore large search spaces, test candidate designs, and check long chains of logic.
Two limits of current language models motivate /teamwork's design. First, model generation can be unpredictable, leaving a gap between a single attempt and finding the correct answer. The system bridges this gap by pairing parallel exploration with dedicated falsifiers (e.g., the Lean 4 proof checker, unmodified software test suites, execution simulators, web search, or hardware counters) to automatically verify which candidate solutions work.
Second, AI models often lose track of details over multi-day tasks because attention disperses across growing context windows. To prevent this, /teamwork partitions context by construction: each specialized agent receives only the exact files and tools it needs for a specific step. Coordinators hand off tasks to fresh successors, while critics and auditors challenge and verify results from clean contexts without the original producer's reasoning.
Teamwork expresses these workflows as declarative self-improving natural-language patterns and dynamically routes each user prompt to the pattern best suited to the task. These patterns range from distributed coding and iterative problem-solving (packaged standalone as /boost, a sequential solve-and-verify loop refined from prior trajectories) to specialized research workflows like long proof and document review, all of which are available in Antigravity today. This allows teams to design custom specifications from past run transcripts routing across different harness patterns and dynamically improving agentic harness. While many of these features are available in Antigravity, we will provide more updates in the coming weeks providing all the features to our external users on Antigravity. Following our previous posts on Antigravity /teamwork, we provide new results to open challenges empowered by new variants of /teamwork and Gemini 4 models.
Across mathematics and theoretical computer science, researchers paired with Gemini 4 and /teamwork harnesses to collaborate on large-scale proofs and generate complete algorithms and proofs directly from open problem statements. We present several examples.
Researchers from Google, CUHK, USC, and CMU used /teamwork's Long Proof pattern (implementing Stellar Colosseum in Antigravity) to prove information theory's 2013 Courtade–Kumar conjecture in full generality. The result confirms that when a random bit string passes through a noisy channel, no compression of the input to a single bit preserves more information than a "dictator function", which just copies a single input bit. Gemini first found counterexamples to a generalized conjecture from the CUHK team's earlier paper. The CUHK team then proposed a new candidate function that reduced the conjecture to a 14-variable optimization problem, along with a key analytical lower bound. Gemini 4 Argon and earlier Gemini models then partitioned the parameter space and developed most of the new analytical bounds and interval-arithmetic certificates. They also extended the proof from balanced to unbalanced functions without human input. The team formalized the entire proof in Lean 4, producing 50 million lines of code.
Researchers also posed open problems directly to Gemini 4 and to multi-agent harnesses such as Stellar Colosseum and /teamwork, receiving end-to-end algorithms and proofs (often with Lean 4 formalizations) that the authors verified, simplified, and edited for publication. In adaptive data analysis, a multi-agent harness running on Gemini 4 Argon independently proved and Lean-verified near-optimal sample bounds for statistical queries chosen adaptively from earlier answers, closing a gap open since 2014. These bounds yield the first nearly space-optimal algorithm for data streams with both insertions and deletions that stays accurate against adaptive adversaries. In quantum computing, a new construction settled a 2014 open question by finding the first n-bit Boolean functions needing strictly more than n/2 quantum queries at constant error, while pinning down the exact query overhead across symmetric functions.
Several results break long standing barriers in processing and compressing large matrices and graphs. For fifty years until STOC 2024, combinatorial algorithms for Boolean matrix multiplication and triangle detection (avoiding the algebraic cancellations of fast matrix multiplication) saved only logarithmic factors over cubic time. A new deterministic algorithm resolves STOC 2024's main open question, achieving its target speedup over cubic time. In high-dimensional geometry, a new lower bound resolves an open problem on sparse networks, proving that approximating all pairwise distances among n points within any constant factor can require polynomially more than n edges, even with extra Steiner points and arbitrarily many hops. In randomized linear algebra, stacked sparse sketches resolve a 2025 open question by breaking the classical sparsity barrier for compressing a matrix to linear dimension without collapsing any direction of an r-dimensional subspace, needing only √log r nonzeros per row (quadratically fewer than the log r required to preserve all lengths) and nearly matching a new lower bound. For weighted graphs under dynamic edge-weight insertions and deletions, Stellar Colosseum resolved an open question left by FOCS 2022, developing the first single-pass streaming algorithm for cut sparsification that achieves near-optimal memory, update time, and reconstruction time simultaneously.
Our results also span optimization, arithmetic, data structures, and statistics. In optimization, resolving an open question on online inverse linear optimization (inferring an expert's hidden objective from decisions over time), the approach gave a deterministic algorithm achieving optimal regret while cutting runtime from exponential in the dimension to polynomial time. For n-bit integer multiplication, multi-agent campaigns simplified the theoretical Turing-machine bound below n log n, while a separate effort gave a new O(n log n) algorithm on a single two-dimensional grid whose C implementation matches the GNU Multiple Precision Arithmetic Library (GMP) bit-for-bit up to a billion bits. In data structures, another result made significant progress on Tarjan's 1985 Deque Conjecture (whether self-adjusting splay trees handle insertions and deletions at both ends in constant amortized time), proving a new upper bound on the per-operation overhead that simultaneously beats every finitely iterated inverse-Ackermann function, verified in Lean 4. Finally, in high-dimensional statistics, the approach proved that approximate leave-one-out cross-validation, which avoids refitting the model from scratch, remains accurate for non-smooth sparse penalties like elastic net.
Checking a computer-assisted manuscript by hand places a heavy burden on peer reviewers, and subtle flaws can hide inside long calculations. Formalizing mathematics in Lean 4 turns a proof into code that a computer kernel checks line by line. Once human mathematicians confirm that the formal theorem statement matches the original problem, the proof only compiles if every logical step holds.
Many of the deepest open problems in mathematics govern how physical waves propagate, how dynamical systems evolve, and whether polynomial equations have integer solutions. Working directly from informal proofs, our multi-agent framework, LEAP, developed with colleagues in Google Cloud AI and paired with Argon, has made progress in these core areas.
Notably, /teamwork-LEAP fully autonomously produced an end-to-end, machine-checked formalization of the Weil conjectures, the landmark results on counting solutions of polynomial equations over finite fields that culminated in Pierre Deligne's 1974 proof. This formalization spans about 1.8 million lines of Lean, produced across 28,800 subagent invocations. The system also synthesized over 919,000 lines of Lean to produce the first formalization of a recent proof of Hilbert's tenth problem over general rings of integers. It also independently reproduces an end-to-end formalization of a recent proof of finite-time blowup for the 3D Navier–Stokes equations with smooth forcing, part of one of the seven Millennium Prize Problems and open for about 90 years.
Beyond these, it also generated the first formalization of the Birkhoff conjecture, a dynamical-systems problem open since 1927. Moreover, it formalized for the first time the 3D Bochner–Riesz conjecture, a central problem in harmonic analysis open for more than 50 years, finding and fixing a gap in a recently released informal proof along the way. Additional machine-checked breakthroughs include resolving the C¹ regularity of infinity-harmonic functions in three and higher dimensions (an open question for about 20 years), formalizing Killip and Vişan's theorem that the KdV water-wave equation is well-posed in H⁻¹ and verifying a UCLA group's informal proofs of the 40-year-old Jamison Caterpillar Conjecture, the Ağırseven-Ollis conjecture, and an improved upper bound on Erdős Problem #1063.
Finally, we provided Deep Review for authors of another CS conference: ahead of STOC 2027, authors of 578 submissions (over a third of all submissions) opted in to receive Deep Review feedback on their drafts. In an ongoing author survey (N = 70), 90% of respondents rated the feedback mostly or almost all factual and 90% said they would use the tool again. Over 30% said Deep Review caught substantive proof bugs or mathematically significant typos, and 14% said it found technical gaps that took more than an hour to fix. Surveyed authors shared that “Tools of this kind can help produce cleaner and more precise research, faster” and that DeepReview provides peace of mind on scientific discovery”. We provide users two weekly calls to Deep Review for free. Deep Review is also implemented as /teamwork's document review pattern and available in Antigravity. Read more about DeepReview in our recent blog post.
Beyond formal proofs, pairing specialized generator agents with independent verifier agents enables discovery workflows in experimental laboratory science.
Determining a molecule’s structure from experimental nuclear magnetic resonance data is one of chemistry’s hardest puzzles. For example, in HIV protease inhibitors, one misplaced bond separates an inactive look-alike from a drug. By organizing 30 specialized agents into a self-correcting loop with /teamwork and a physics-informed spectral simulator, we achieved 95% top-1 exact match and 100% top-3 accuracy on complex benchmark molecules, nearly doubling single-agent performance. Beyond teaching Gemini to reason over structure, this lays the groundwork for ongoing works on 3D protein and complex structures dynamics.
Beyond this example, science problems including applications in nuclear fusion and quantum algorithms reported in the Gemini 4 blog post benefited from /teamwork and the Argon model.
We also applied a similar multi-round auditing workflow to build empirical benchmarks. Starting from five human-specified acceptance criteria like data anonymization, 29 agents running on Argon over 11.5 hours turned 13 public tables into a 50-task causal inference benchmark. In these tasks, models must determine whether a causal effect can be identified and estimate it against a known answer. After initial checks, separate reviewer and auditor agents caught shortcuts, flagged wording hints, and vetoed hallucinated scripts, turning each into a permanent test. Automating this heavily manual task generation supplies the hard problems needed to help Gemini answer counterfactual "what-if" questions.
When paired with objective evaluators such as network benchmarks, game engines, unmodified test suites, and hardware timers, /teamwork campaigns can execute multi-day software and hardware engineering projects.
Using /teamwork and Argon, we built FarArray, a low-latency kernel-bypass Partitioned Global Address Space based on the SNAP microkernel. Applications built on FarArray include lock-free Connected Components, which scale to 1-trillion-edge graphs with 60x to 90x throughput gains and 40x to 180x speedups at 6x lower RAM over TCP key-value stores. Similarly, InstantEmbedding (Personalized PageRank) reduced peak DRAM by up to 14x and runtime by 11x to 27x over accelerated MapReduce implementations. The team also used /teamwork and Argon to create custom TPU kernels for Graph Neural Networks, delivering up to a 185x High Bandwidth Memory reduction and 8x to 15x speedups in forward and backward passes. Further details are available in our recent Antigravity blog post.
To test autonomous optimization over long horizons, hill-climbing teams on Argon trained a 9x9 Go engine from scratch starting from a basic AlphaZero implementation. Human researchers set the chip allocation and evaluation setup, while the harness ran autonomous campaigns for faster self-play (achieving a 2,300x speedup) and faster Elo readings, promoting champions strictly via statistical tests on locked records. In under eight days, the engine gained roughly 2,600 Elo, reaching 1,885 Elo, placing it between KataGo at 16 and 32 visits per move.
Large code migrations fail when agents stub out dependencies or alter tests to make a broken port pass. A new /teamwork rewrite pattern, soon to be available in Antigravity, solved this by scheduling each module only after its dependencies compile, linking every leaf module against the original, unmodified test suite. Building on the initial 25% port of the Zircon microkernel, a two-week Argon campaign rewrote the remaining C++ code, adding about 800,000 lines of safe Rust that pass all kernel unit tests and boot on arm64 hardware. /teamwork also rewrote libgav1, Google's AV1 video decoder, from C++ into around 170k lines Rust, achieving 100% bit-exact parity across a broad conformance test suite. Initially, the port was around 4x slower than the original C++ code. However, after giving /teamwork access to profiling tools and a mandate to maximize speed on sample video clips, the Rust port became 20-30% faster than the original C++ and hand-optimized assembly code on both x86_64 and ARM.
For day-to-day coding tasks, the Antigravity /boost command packages /teamwork's iterative coding pattern. A long-running /teamwork campaign analyzed past run trajectories and found that tightly coupled code is best handled through sequential implementation and revision stages rather than parallel splits, improving /boost on coding tasks. This is an example of a harness optimizing another harness for token efficiency.
For machine learning systems, full hardware evaluations of custom JAX and Pallas TPU kernels take 7 to 32 minutes, limiting how many candidates prior optimization agents can explore. Using /teamwork's DuoDiscover pattern on Argon, four parallel agents identified performance bottlenecks and constructed fast, 23-second proxy evaluators called yardsticks. These yardsticks produce candidate orderings that are continuously validated against periodic full hardware runs using Kendall's rank correlation. DuoDiscover achieves a 4.09x geometric-mean speedup over reference implementations across 17 JAXBench kernels and a 2.99x geometric-mean speedup across 7 hand-optimized vLLM TPU-Inference Pallas kernels for frontier open-weight models.
We have also applied /teamwork and /boost to many production infrastructure and hardware modeling problems across Google. Examples include:
External scientists and developers can use Gemini 4 models with /teamwork and /boost in Antigravity and Deep Review today. We have also opened applications giving 10,000 academic researchers six months of free access to our highest usage tier. Upcoming updates will bring our formal proof pattern and additional specialized workflows into Antigravity. These features will be accompanied by deep-dive tutorials on the Antigravity blog in the coming weeks, ensuring that developers and researchers have the resources needed to generate, inspect, and iteratively refine custom multi-agent teams. We are excited to make our tools (in addition to our models) available for everyone.
Antigravity’s /teamwork feature is a result of a Google-wide effort with many contributors and ongoing explorations. Contributions include: Yuan Deng[279eb3], Honghao Lin[279eb3], Jieming Mao[279eb3], Vahab Mirrokni[279eb3], Vishnu Veeramachaneni[279eb3], David Woodruff[279eb3], Song Zuo[279eb3], Shubham Agarwal, Santiago Balseiro, MohammadHossein Bateni, Mohsen Bayati, Garrett Bingham, Guy Blelloch, Douglas Chen, Vincent Cohen-Addad, Piaoyang Cui, Tulsee Doshi, Eric First, Michael Galkin, Elena Gribelyuk, Anupam Gupta, Guru Guruganesh, Mike Heddes, Patrick Heisel, Iman Hosseini, Kevin Hou, Lalit Jain, Adel Javanmard, Rajesh Jayaram, Xiaoen Ju, Po-Nien Kung, Silvio Lattanzi, Samuel Lavoie, Chun-Liang Li, Matthew Li, Yinan Li, Yuchen Li, Yiming Liu, Thang Luong, Brandon Mayer, Simon Meierhans, Swaroop Mishra, Ashish Naik, Sebastian Nowozin, Renato Paes Leme, Nikos Parotsidis, Ali Parviz, Neel Patel, Violet Peng, Bryan Perozzi, Anshul Ramachandran, Shashir Reddy, Lijie Ren, Madhavi Sewak, D Shin, Reza Shokri, Sukhdeep Singh, Samuel Smith, Linfeng Song, Dilara Soylu, Zheng Tan, Sasan Tavakkol, Hadi Tavassol, Yifeng Teng, Tom Wollschläger, Haifeng Xu, Zicheng Xu, Yihao Xue, Amir Yazdanbakhsh, and Goran Žužić. Carla Bromberg and Lauren Fraser for program management, and Francis Duan, Pan-Pan Jiang, Xiyan Shao, Jane Shapiro, Weilun Shi, and Xinyu Ye for their contributions to the project. We also thank support from several leaders Sergey Brin, Demis Hassabis, Koray Kavukcuoglu, Pushmeet Kohli, James Manyika, Yossi Matias, Varun Mohan, and Amin Vahdat.
Several academic collaborators contributing to the research papers: Zijie Chen, Amin Gohari, Chandra Nair (CUHK), Songbin Liu, Arian Maleki, Chen Shang, Hanbo Yang, Wenjin Zhang (Columbia University), Zihan Hao, Qipeng Liu, Jianan Shao (UCSD), Lorenzo Beretta (IBM Cambridge), Simone Bombari (BYU & Flatiron Institute), Mahbod Majid, Margalit Glasgow (MIT), Raphael Arkady Meyer (UC Berkeley, ICSI, & LBNL), Kijun Shin (CMU), Huacheng Yu, Elena Gribelyuk (Princeton University), Xin Lyu (UCB/ ByteDance), Samson Zhou (Texas A&M), Erik Waingarten (UPenn), and Sorrachai Yingchareonthawornchai (ETH-ITS).

Advancing science and engineering with Antigravity's /teamwork
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
13:20:43 UTC
22:20:43 KST

November 13, 2026
Vahab Mirrokni, Google Fellow and VP, Google Research
We share recent results from Antigravity’s /teamwork multiagent systems, which enhance human-AI collaborations to solve open problems across mathematics, chemistry, systems software, and ML engineering. All of this work is done in collaboration with human experts, and we are making these tools available externally.
Over the past several weeks, teams across Google and academic partner institutions have tackled open problems in math, science, and complex systems engineering that had remained open for years. Using Gemini 4 Argon, Gemini 4 Carbon, Gemini 4 Hydrogen and multi-agent workflows such as Antigravity’s /teamwork (building on various Gemini models), these collaborations have produced new innovations across information theory, formal verifications, molecular structure discoveries, and kernel optimization, along with several production infrastructure gains at Google. We are making these agentic tools available in Antigravity’s /teamwork (alongside /boost and other agents) so that researchers and developers can apply them to their own work.
Frontier research and systems engineering require navigating vast literature, building custom computational pipelines, testing against physical measurements, and verifying every step. Progress comes from an interactive discovery loop where researchers frame questions and define the theoretical or physical constraints, while long-horizon agents write custom solvers, explore large search spaces, test candidate designs, and check long chains of logic.
Two limits of current language models motivate /teamwork's design. First, model generation can be unpredictable, leaving a gap between a single attempt and finding the correct answer. The system bridges this gap by pairing parallel exploration with dedicated falsifiers (e.g., the Lean 4 proof checker, unmodified software test suites, execution simulators, web search, or hardware counters) to automatically verify which candidate solutions work.
Second, AI models often lose track of details over multi-day tasks because attention disperses across growing context windows. To prevent this, /teamwork partitions context by construction: each specialized agent receives only the exact files and tools it needs for a specific step. Coordinators hand off tasks to fresh successors, while critics and auditors challenge and verify results from clean contexts without the original producer's reasoning.
Teamwork expresses these workflows as declarative self-improving natural-language patterns and dynamically routes each user prompt to the pattern best suited to the task. These patterns range from distributed coding and iterative problem-solving (packaged standalone as /boost, a sequential solve-and-verify loop refined from prior trajectories) to specialized research workflows like long proof and document review, all of which are available in Antigravity today. This allows teams to design custom specifications from past run transcripts routing across different harness patterns and dynamically improving agentic harness. While many of these features are available in Antigravity, we will provide more updates in the coming weeks providing all the features to our external users on Antigravity. Following our previous posts on Antigravity /teamwork, we provide new results to open challenges empowered by new variants of /teamwork and Gemini 4 models.
Across mathematics and theoretical computer science, researchers paired with Gemini 4 and /teamwork harnesses to collaborate on large-scale proofs and generate complete algorithms and proofs directly from open problem statements. We present several examples.
Researchers from Google, CUHK, USC, and CMU used /teamwork's Long Proof pattern (implementing Stellar Colosseum in Antigravity) to prove information theory's 2013 Courtade–Kumar conjecture in full generality. The result confirms that when a random bit string passes through a noisy channel, no compression of the input to a single bit preserves more information than a "dictator function", which just copies a single input bit. Gemini first found counterexamples to a generalized conjecture from the CUHK team's earlier paper. The CUHK team then proposed a new candidate function that reduced the conjecture to a 14-variable optimization problem, along with a key analytical lower bound. Gemini 4 Argon and earlier Gemini models then partitioned the parameter space and developed most of the new analytical bounds and interval-arithmetic certificates. They also extended the proof from balanced to unbalanced functions without human input. The team formalized the entire proof in Lean 4, producing 50 million lines of code.
Researchers also posed open problems directly to Gemini 4 and to multi-agent harnesses such as Stellar Colosseum and /teamwork, receiving end-to-end algorithms and proofs (often with Lean 4 formalizations) that the authors verified, simplified, and edited for publication. In adaptive data analysis, a multi-agent harness running on Gemini 4 Argon independently proved and Lean-verified near-optimal sample bounds for statistical queries chosen adaptively from earlier answers, closing a gap open since 2014. These bounds yield the first nearly space-optimal algorithm for data streams with both insertions and deletions that stays accurate against adaptive adversaries. In quantum computing, a new construction settled a 2014 open question by finding the first n-bit Boolean functions needing strictly more than n/2 quantum queries at constant error, while pinning down the exact query overhead across symmetric functions.
Several results break long standing barriers in processing and compressing large matrices and graphs. For fifty years until STOC 2024, combinatorial algorithms for Boolean matrix multiplication and triangle detection (avoiding the algebraic cancellations of fast matrix multiplication) saved only logarithmic factors over cubic time. A new deterministic algorithm resolves STOC 2024's main open question, achieving its target speedup over cubic time. In high-dimensional geometry, a new lower bound resolves an open problem on sparse networks, proving that approximating all pairwise distances among n points within any constant factor can require polynomially more than n edges, even with extra Steiner points and arbitrarily many hops. In randomized linear algebra, stacked sparse sketches resolve a 2025 open question by breaking the classical sparsity barrier for compressing a matrix to linear dimension without collapsing any direction of an r-dimensional subspace, needing only √log r nonzeros per row (quadratically fewer than the log r required to preserve all lengths) and nearly matching a new lower bound. For weighted graphs under dynamic edge-weight insertions and deletions, Stellar Colosseum resolved an open question left by FOCS 2022, developing the first single-pass streaming algorithm for cut sparsification that achieves near-optimal memory, update time, and reconstruction time simultaneously.
Our results also span optimization, arithmetic, data structures, and statistics. In optimization, resolving an open question on online inverse linear optimization (inferring an expert's hidden objective from decisions over time), the approach gave a deterministic algorithm achieving optimal regret while cutting runtime from exponential in the dimension to polynomial time. For n-bit integer multiplication, multi-agent campaigns simplified the theoretical Turing-machine bound below n log n, while a separate effort gave a new O(n log n) algorithm on a single two-dimensional grid whose C implementation matches the GNU Multiple Precision Arithmetic Library (GMP) bit-for-bit up to a billion bits. In data structures, another result made significant progress on Tarjan's 1985 Deque Conjecture (whether self-adjusting splay trees handle insertions and deletions at both ends in constant amortized time), proving a new upper bound on the per-operation overhead that simultaneously beats every finitely iterated inverse-Ackermann function, verified in Lean 4. Finally, in high-dimensional statistics, the approach proved that approximate leave-one-out cross-validation, which avoids refitting the model from scratch, remains accurate for non-smooth sparse penalties like elastic net.

Tech Companies Are Trying to Shift the Concept of Privacy (Again)
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
12:45:45 UTC
21:45:45 KST

Privacy, which companies were once happy to claim was all about personal choice, is now primed to affect everyone around you even more than it already did.
Meta and Google’s smart glasses can record anything the owner is looking at or use artificial intelligence to describe it. Apple’s* Audio Intelligence feature turns every Apple Watch into a transcription machine that records or summarizes your day. Amazon’s Bee devices take that a step further and can record and transcribe everything. Dozens of other companies are introducing pendants, rings, and other products designed to record, transcribe, or summarize everything we say and do all day long.
Privacy has always been a team sport. The actions you take with communication apps, collaborative software, or your cloud backup solutions, have long impacted everyone else you’re interacting with. But moving those choices deeper into the physical world where strangers, colleagues, family, and friends are all now a part of your privacy choices is drastically changing the game.
Tech companies want us to accept a reality where one person’s privacy choices (or lack thereof) means you suddenly need to be an expert in consumer tech who understands the capabilities of many devices on the market, how they work, how and if they signal when they’re recording, and what platforms they do or do not connect to. They believe these ambient surveillance devices that work in the background and are designed to look as normal as possible are the future, while they also expect the owners of these devices to use them responsibly and to notify those around them of their capabilities to act within whatever laws their states may have.
This is not reasonable, nor how consent works in any practical sense.
Let’s break this down by looking at some of the biggest players currently in the game: Apple and Amazon, who have two different takes on transcribing audio throughout the day, as well as Meta and Google, who both have slapped AI and cameras onto eyeglasses.
How to Think About the Technical Promises Companies Make
Ask Apple about privacy concerns and they’ll point to a white paper that explains all the technical barriers between them as a company and the data generated by the watch. Ask Meta and they’ll talk about all the ways you can customize the privacy controls or how certain details, like faces, may be blurred out when you interact with Meta AI through the glasses (but not during standard recording). Google does this too. Amazon will also talk about how you’re always in control, and no audio is stored (though it’ll also let you enable Amazon store integration, so your conversations can directly result in ads for products). And so on across all the companies making these products.
Some of these solutions do a better job than others at cutting out potential law enforcement or company access to this data. There will be a time to dive into these details more deeply, but the technical privacy promises these companies focus on only cover the consumer and user of the device. They ignore every bystander you interact with and their inability to actually opt out. There is no technical protection for you as a non-user of these devices, the person who finds yourself captured by them.
Still, we hope to look deeper into the technical differences between this category of devices soon.
There Must Be Some Laws to Consider, Right?
With something like Apple’s Audio Intelligence and Amazon’s Bee devices, it may be best to treat them similar to an always-recording microphone, even if companies try to claim there’s “technically” no audio recording. These tools still often use a microphone to capture raw audio from conversations and convert it into summaries or transcripts before discarding the audio. Google's blog post notes that it has whole responsible use part of their Terms of Service where users have to agree not to record others without permission or in sensitive places.
But it’s not just the question of the legality from the point of view of the user. Law enforcement will eventually try to compel access to the data that these products create (either real-time audio or the summaries and transcripts they create). And while some products, like Apple’s Audio Intelligence, are engineered to make that difficult, not every product will be. We know from history that when data exists, governments will do everything they can to access it. There’s no reason to think something would change today.
No Tech and Legal Solutions Will Reduce Social Issues
There is a problem humming along in the background that is impossible to address with technical or legal solutions. If secretly documenting conversations becomes the norm, it makes every private conversation less spontaneous and open. It breaks a longstanding social norm that you will not record and document every conversation with friends, family, colleagues, or strangers.
The companies that make these sorts of products like to claim they’re no different from tools we already have. You can easily record everything right now with a phone or a camera. Heck, you can do so with a camcorder or cassette recorder if you want. And you can transcribe conversations with pen and paper, too. But those are actionable steps we take that both require effort from the user, and are clear to others around us. This new wave of passive surveillance tech is designed to encourage continuous monitoring and hide its use.
Even taken at face value, none of these products can understand the complex social context we all navigate every day. We intrinsically know there are different social agreements we all make when we’re standing on a street corner, at a protest, in a bathroom, at a doctor’s office, at the gym, or in a restaurant. Taking notes in a meeting at work is completely normal. Getting a summary of a conversation with your best friend about their divorce is not. Always-on monitoring of our conversations is an unacceptable burden on our conversational privacy.
Google seems to all but admit this problem in a conversation with CNN when it described the company’s experiments with a notification system that would alert people’s phones when someone was recording nearby:
“And just alerting bystanders doesn’t do much to help, [Juston Payne, senior director of product management at Google] said. “It can actually be weirdly disempowering to you in that you’re like, ‘What am I meant to do now?’” he said.”
What We Can Do About It
It’s easy to buy into the tech company line that these products are inevitable, but we’ve seen so many vaporware products over the years that we should take those claims of success with a grain of salt. We’ve seen entire product categories come and go within just a few years, and if the public doesn’t want this stuff, the tech companies will have to throw in the towel.
Of course, that doesn’t mean we all just have to sit around and hope that’s what happens. For its part, Apple launched its Audio Intelligence features in beta specifically to get feedback, and we think it’s worth taking the time to fill out their feedback forms (or in the Feedback Assistant app if you’re in the beta) and let them know what you think.
Products from the likes of Meta, Google, Amazon, and others might be harder to push back on, but politely and clearly engaging with those who use them in ways that make you uncomfortable is a good place to start. And if you see abusive videos on social networks where these sorts of products are used, report them. If you find these products useful for yourself, make it clear to those around you when you’re using them, and only do so with consent and in social moments where it makes sense to do so.
*Apple is an EFF donor. EFF donors do not influence our agenda or our positions.

====================================================================================================

Tech Companies Are Trying to Shift the Concept of Privacy (Again)
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
12:45:45 UTC
21:45:45 KST

Privacy, which companies were once happy to claim was all about personal choice, is now primed to affect everyone around you even more than it already did.
Meta and Google’s smart glasses can record anything the owner is looking at or use artificial intelligence to describe it. Apple’s* Audio Intelligence feature turns every Apple Watch into a transcription machine that records or summarizes your day. Amazon’s Bee devices take that a step further and can record and transcribe everything. Dozens of other companies are introducing pendants, rings, and other products designed to record, transcribe, or summarize everything we say and do all day long.
Privacy has always been a team sport. The actions you take with communication apps, collaborative software, or your cloud backup solutions, have long impacted everyone else you’re interacting with. But moving those choices deeper into the physical world where strangers, colleagues, family, and friends are all now a part of your privacy choices is drastically changing the game.
Tech companies want us to accept a reality where one person’s privacy choices (or lack thereof) means you suddenly need to be an expert in consumer tech who understands the capabilities of many devices on the market, how they work, how and if they signal when they’re recording, and what platforms they do or do not connect to. They believe these ambient surveillance devices that work in the background and are designed to look as normal as possible are the future, while they also expect the owners of these devices to use them responsibly and to notify those around them of their capabilities to act within whatever laws their states may have.
This is not reasonable, nor how consent works in any practical sense.
Let’s break this down by looking at some of the biggest players currently in the game: Apple and Amazon, who have two different takes on transcribing audio throughout the day, as well as Meta and Google, who both have slapped AI and cameras onto eyeglasses.
How to Think About the Technical Promises Companies Make
Ask Apple about privacy concerns and they’ll point to a white paper that explains all the technical barriers between them as a company and the data generated by the watch. Ask Meta and they’ll talk about all the ways you can customize the privacy controls or how certain details, like faces, may be blurred out when you interact with Meta AI through the glasses (but not during standard recording). Google does this too. Amazon will also talk about how you’re always in control, and no audio is stored (though it’ll also let you enable Amazon store integration, so your conversations can directly result in ads for products). And so on across all the companies making these products.
Some of these solutions do a better job than others at cutting out potential law enforcement or company access to this data. There will be a time to dive into these details more deeply, but the technical privacy promises these companies focus on only cover the consumer and user of the device. They ignore every bystander you interact with and their inability to actually opt out. There is no technical protection for you as a non-user of these devices, the person who finds yourself captured by them.
Still, we hope to look deeper into the technical differences between this category of devices soon.

But it’s not just the question of the legality from the point of view of the user.
