It breaks a longstanding social norm that you will not record and document every conversation with friends, family, colleagues, or strangers.
The companies that make these sorts of products like to claim they’re no different from tools we already have. You can easily record everything right now with a phone or a camera. Heck, you can do so with a camcorder or cassette recorder if you want. And you can transcribe conversations with pen and paper, too. But those are actionable steps we take that both require effort from the user, and are clear to others around us. This new wave of passive surveillance tech is designed to encourage continuous monitoring and hide its use.
Even taken at face value, none of these products can understand the complex social context we all navigate every day. We intrinsically know there are different social agreements we all make when we’re standing on a street corner, at a protest, in a bathroom, at a doctor’s office, at the gym, or in a restaurant. Taking notes in a meeting at work is completely normal. Getting a summary of a conversation with your best friend about their divorce is not. Always-on monitoring of our conversations is an unacceptable burden on our conversational privacy.
Google seems to all but admit this problem in a conversation with CNN when it described the company’s experiments with a notification system that would alert people’s phones when someone was recording nearby:
“And just alerting bystanders doesn’t do much to help, [Juston Payne, senior director of product management at Google] said. “It can actually be weirdly disempowering to you in that you’re like, ‘What am I meant to do now?’” he said.”
What We Can Do About It
It’s easy to buy into the tech company line that these products are inevitable, but we’ve seen so many vaporware products over the years that we should take those claims of success with a grain of salt. We’ve seen entire product categories come and go within just a few years, and if the public doesn’t want this stuff, the tech companies will have to throw in the towel.
Of course, that doesn’t mean we all just have to sit around and hope that’s what happens. For its part, Apple launched its Audio Intelligence features in beta specifically to get feedback, and we think it’s worth taking the time to fill out their feedback forms (or in the Feedback Assistant app if you’re in the beta) and let them know what you think.
Products from the likes of Meta, Google, Amazon, and others might be harder to push back on, but politely and clearly engaging with those who use them in ways that make you uncomfortable is a good place to start. And if you see abusive videos on social networks where these sorts of products are used, report them. If you find these products useful for yourself, make it clear to those around you when you’re using them, and only do so with consent and in social moments where it makes sense to do so.

Ex-Nvidia CEO Misled Company Leaders On Affair With Chinese Businesswoman

Ex-Nvidia CEO Misled Company Leaders On Affair With Chinese Businesswoman (reuters.com) 48
			
		 	
				An anonymous reader quotes a report from Reuters: Former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang misled several of the company's leaders, including general counsel Tim Teter and chief marketing officer Alison Wagonfeld, about the nature of his relationship with Chinese businesswoman Alice Huang, with whom he had an affair, according to two people familiar with the matter and internal correspondence reviewed by Reuters. [...] During the autumn of 2025 and in October 2026, in response to press queries that drew some Nvidia leaders' attention to his relationship with Megaspeed and Alice Huang, Huang at both times described their interactions as appropriate, the people said, speaking on the condition of anonymity. Reuters only reviewed messages from the exchanges in October, not the ones from 2025. In two separate messages in October, Huang told Nvidia leaders, including Teter and Wagonfeld, that the interactions reflected standard business dealings with customers. In other messages, Huang affirmed the routine nature of his interactions with Megaspeed.
Nvidia's Huang also discussed taking legal action against the New York Times after it published an article in October of last year that drew attention to his meetings in public with Alice Huang, the people said. That article had prompted Jensen Huang to communicate with some Nvidia leaders in 2025 about their interactions, which he did not describe as an affair, the people said. Ultimately, neither Nvidia nor Huang filed a lawsuit against the paper. A spokesperson for the New York Times said the paper received no legal demand. It is unclear when his relationship with Alice Huang became intimate. Photos posted online and verified by Reuters show them together in the US, China and elsewhere, dating as far back as December 2023.

Amazon's Plan to Index the World's Online Shopping Data
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
07:00:00 UTC
16:00:00 KST

Amazon's Plan to Index the World's Online Shopping Data (yahoo.com) 25
			
		 	
				Amazon is considering a "World Product Graph" that would index information about products sold across the internet in an effort to keep shoppers and AI agents from turning to Google, Reddit or other sources for product research. The proposed database could also improve Amazon's AI shopping assistant by addressing inaccurate or incomplete answers and allowing it to use cheaper models.
"Amazon cannot be the go-destination for every shopping mission if customers must leave to research product relationships on Google, brand websites, and review sites," according to an internal Amazon document. "These answers exist, but the only way to find them has been to leave Amazon for painstaking research on brand websites, third party review sites and tech forums." Reuters could not determine if or when this plan may be implemented, though the document, written like a press release, has an aspirational future release date of October 1, 2027.
Amazon said in a statement: "Draft, outdated documents often paint an incomplete picture and the existence of a brainstorming document such as this one doesn't necessarily reflect our current plans. We're constantly working on new ideas and technologies to make Amazon's product catalog comprehensive, accurate, and helpful for customers."

Why a NAT VPS can cost $1: the economics of an IPv4 address
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
06:59:46 UTC
15:59:46 KST

Blog / IPv4 economics
Why a NAT VPS can cost $1: the economics of an IPv4 address
12 November 2026 · Guide · By Mohammad AlSaraf
Our cheapest plan costs $1.00 a month. That's less than the rent on a single IPv4 address at many providers. So how does it work? The short answer: you don't get an IPv4 address of your own. You share one, and here are the numbers behind that.
IPv4 addresses are scarce, and they cost money
There are only about 4.3 billion IPv4 addresses, and the free pools ran out years ago. Today they are bought, sold and rented like property:
- Buying: broker reports put the average 2026 price at around $20 per address, down from a peak of roughly $45 to $60 in 2021 and 2022 (IPv4Center, IPXO).
- Renting: leases run at roughly $0.35 to $0.60 per address per month on the wholesale market.
- At a hosting provider: the retail price is far higher. Our own provider, Contabo, charges €3.50 a month for one additional IPv4 address on a VPS (Contabo help).
These figures come mostly from brokers and vary by source and block size, so treat them as a range, not an exact price. The point stands either way: if every $1 server came with its own IPv4 address at retail prices, the address alone would cost several times the whole plan.
How sharing works on natbox
Each of our hosts has one public IPv4 address. Every server on that host gets its own block of ports on it: 5 on the free trial, 10 on Nano, 20 on Box and Box CPU, 30 on Box Plus. The first port goes to SSH, the rest go straight to the same port numbers inside your server. On 9 November 2026 our three addresses carried 56, 31 and 14 customer servers.
So the cost of one address is split dozens of ways. Even at Contabo's retail €3.50, that's well under 10 cents a month per server on our busiest host. That is most of the trick behind a $1 plan.
Ports 80 and 443 can't be split into blocks, because every website needs them. So we share those by domain name: you link your domain in the client area, and our proxy sends each visitor to the right server without decrypting anything (we load-tested it).
What you give up
- Standard port numbers over IPv4. Your SSH is on a port like 10050, not 22, and a game server or VPN runs on one of your own port numbers. Most software is happy with that. A few things, like some game clients with a fixed port, are not.
- Only your ports. Over IPv4, only the ports in your block reach your server, plus 80 and 443 for linked domains.
- No outgoing mail ports. Ports 25, 465 and 587 are blocked for everyone, because one spammer would get the shared address blocklisted for all.
- A shared reputation. If someone abuses the address, everyone on it could suffer. That's why we take abuse seriously (how we handle abuse reports).
IPv6 doesn't have the problem
IPv6 has so many addresses that nobody needs to share. Every natbox server, including the free trial, gets its own routed /80: 248 addresses, every port open, port 22 and 443 included, no proxy in between (how it works). As more of the internet speaks IPv6, the shared IPv4 address matters less and less.
Who a NAT VPS is for
If you need a full IPv4 address with every port, a NAT VPS isn't the right tool, and that's fine. If you want a real Linux server for a bot, a small website, a VPN, a monitoring tool or for learning, sharing an IPv4 address is what lets it cost less than a coffee. See the plans.
Get new tutorials in Telegram. Follow @natboxio: every new post, plus short server tips.
More from the blog: Why some servers booted without SSH, and how we fixed it, We made disk writes on natbox over 100 times faster. Here's the trade-off, Restarting for a kernel update: what happened, minute by minute. RSS feed.

Israeli airstrike on Nasser hospital in Gaza killed surgeon and others
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
05:31:44 UTC
14:31:44 KST

An Israeli airstrike on Nasser hospital in southern Gaza killed six people, including a surgeon and a disabled man, and wounded 28 others, according to the hospital director.
The Israeli military carried out the attack on the hospital, in Khan Younis, early on Thursday saying in a statement it had struck a Hamas commander and other fighters there, without providing evidence.
The strike destroyed part of the surgery department, and caused extensive damage to several floors. Services were suspended in four departments: radiology, laboratories, surgical operations and inpatient surgical wards, according to Dr Ahmed al-Fara, hospital director.
The ceiling of one of the wards had caved in, while debris-covered beds lay next to medical instruments twisted from the force of the blast. In other rooms, piles of rubble and shattered concrete cluttered the floor and a hole was left in the floor, where the bomb penetrated the building.
Dr Ayman Abu Ubaid, a surgical specialist at Nasser medical complex, who was wounded in the attack, said: “The bombing caused thick smoke to fill the rooms and fires to break out, amid the groans of wounded and people’s screams. There were many casualties and blood everywhere … Most of us sustained injuries to our limbs, as well as shrapnel wounds in different parts of our bodies.”
Ubaid said the strike came at about 12.30am, without warning. “The patients in the department were all surgical patients waiting to undergo operations. The bombing happened at a time when they should have been asleep,” he said.
Among the dead was Rizk Abu Shaira, 52, a general surgeon who had featured in American Doctor, a documentary about doctors operating in Gaza.
“Dr Rizk was in the American Doctor documentary operating and serving his people. He lost his life doing that, he lost his life because he was doing that,” Dr Thaer Ahmad, who also featured in the documentary, posted on X.
Abu Shaira was killed alongside one of his patients, on whom he had operated.
Nasser hospital is one of the last functioning healthcare facilities in Gaza, after Israeli bombing made most of the strip’s facilities unusable. Last year Israel also struck the hospital, killing five journalists in a double-tap strike on a location often used by reporters for filming. In all, the hospital has been attacked seven times since the war on Gaza began.
The Israeli military has accused Hamas of using hospitals as command centres and to store weapons, an accusation the militant movement rejects. More than 1,700 healthcare workers have been killed in Gaza, while Doctors Without Borders (MSF) says Israel has detained hundreds of medics since 7 October 2023, with at least 95 still in custody.
About 90% of Gaza’s health infrastructure has been destroyed or damage by Israeli bombing, systematic destruction UN experts called “medicide”.
Attacking healthcare facilities and staff is illegal under international humanitarian law, though these facilities can lose their protected status if they are used for military purposes.
There is an increased need for healthcare during the winter months in Gaza, where rains have displaced people from their tents and increased communicable diseases in crowded encampments.
Already, Nasser hospital is lacking about 80% of its essential medicines and medical supplies, said al-Fara, its director.
One of those killed in Thursday’s strike was Seif Ghazi Abu Hatab, 29, who was admitted to Nasser hospital on Monday after a piece of debris fell on his back while he was helping clear rubble from a friend’s home. He was scheduled for surgery at 10am on Thursday, and was meant to receive further treatment abroad.
“Seif was a young man in the prime of his life. He wanted to live, rebuild his home and start a normal life, free from problems and suffering … Seif was killed after already losing two of his brothers and his father. His mother has now lost three of her sons,” said his cousin Tareq Abu Hatab, 37.
Israel launched the war in Gaza after the 7 October 2023 Hamas-led attack that killed about 1,200 people and took 251 hostage. At least 74,645 people have since been killed in Gaza and Israel has carried out a genocide in the strip, according to the UN independent international commission of inquiry on the occupied Palestinian territory.
Israel continues to carry out airstrikes in Gaza, killing more than 1,600 people since the October 2025 “ceasefire”, according to the Gaza ministry of health.

Road Trip at the Borderland
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
04:59:21 UTC
13:59:21 KST

Thoughtful stories for thoughtless times.
Longreads has published hundreds of original stories—personal essays, reported features, reading lists, and more—and more than 14,000 editor’s picks. And they’re all funded by readers like you. Become a member today.
Aube Rey Lescure | Longreads | October 29, 2026 | 5,562 words (20 minutes)
When my father proposes a road trip to admire fall foliage along the Yalu River, the natural border separating parts of northeastern China from North Korea, I am skeptical for several reasons. First, it appears his inspiration comes largely from Douyin, the Chinese TikTok, which has been feeding him videos of regional autumnal splendor, with hypersaturated reds that carry more than a whiff of artificial enhancement.
Secondly, I don’t know how to drive, and neither, really, does my father. He would dispute this, as he did earn a driver’s license recently from a shoddy driving school in Dandong, a border town on the Yalu and our ancestral bastion. He’s already bought a brand new car, a Chinese domestic bestseller, for the bargain price of eight thousand US dollars. In the days since owning it, he’d only gotten into one accident (ramming into a neighbor’s parked car, as captured on the compound CCTV).
Thirdly, our visit to northeastern China (my father lives in Shanghai) is largely for the purpose of seeing my grandmother, who is minded by Sanshu, my youngest uncle, and I’m fairly sure neither has heard of a fall foliage road trip in their lifetimes. Furthermore, my grandmother is allergic to most forms of financial expenditure. At home, she plugs the bathtub and gathers water from a faucet dripping at a volume feeble enough to go undetected by the water meter. With that reservoir and a plastic bucket, she flushes the toilet and fools the utility company. Imagine how she will feel about gas money.
Fourthly, at the time he raises this idea, my father is recovering from cancer, one month post-op from the removal of a malignant tumor in his colon. He did not tell me until after the operation.
“We’ll just drive very slowly,” he says.
Weeks before my visit to China, my father had called me from a hospital bed to let me know about the cancer. This was our first phone call in years—usually, our exchanges are constrained to happy birthdays on WeChat or confirming the travel logistics of my infrequent visits. After I arrive in Shanghai, despite his efforts at dissuasion, I accompany him to the Fudan University Cancer Center for a follow-up appointment. The cancer is diagnosed, retroactively, as stage II. The doctor recommends preventive chemotherapy to deter recurrence. My father says no. The doctor recommends chemo pills. My father says no again.
When the doctor notices my increasingly discomfited expression and asks, “Who’s this?” my father says, “This is my niece.”
“It’s too complicated otherwise,” he tells me afterward. I am half-white; he is Chinese. I want to retort that it’s not complicated; I am his daughter. But I bite my tongue.
Since my teenage years, after my parents divorced, a distance had wedged itself between my father and me, further exacerbated by my move to the US at 16 years old. You might say we are estranged. Once or twice in the past decade, he’s given me a stiff hug, but mostly he greets me with a quick wave at airport arrivals, then hurries off to find a taxi. At his home in Shanghai, I dine and go on outings with my stepmother and sister while he sits out most communal activities. We barely make eye contact, him perpetually positioned in front of his computer screen, watching political commentary videos, me mumbling a greeting as I skulk by. The road trip proposition is, for all these reasons and more, preposterous. The two of us, in a car for days, physically proximate despite the emotional Mariana Trench between us? Maybe that’s why the idea also feels strangely enticing.
When he feels recovered enough, though his stomach is still bandaged, my father and I fly north to Dalian. Nainai, my 81-year-old grandmother, is unaware of my father’s illness. She knows he’d been hospitalized, but he paid a copy shop man to photoshop a report saying he had a stomach infection, not cancer. (The copy shop is located right outside the hospital, and business is booming.) The first evening we arrive at the apartment my grandmother shares with Sanshu, Nainai cooks a feast of homemade fennel dumplings and pork trotters. This is a special occasion: Usually, she spends her days holed up in her apartment, watching reels of web soaps and fake news on her phone at high volume while Sanshu shuts himself in his room. Her ideal granddaughterly visit would be me sitting next to her at home, force-fed like a foie gras goose during intermissions from the reel-viewing.
This can be arranged on the road as well, and my father, as eldest son, has his sway: To take his family on a car trip, or “self-driving tourism” as it’s known on the Chinese internet, is the new pinnacle of the Chinese dream. For a family that once subsisted on meal tickets, whose children collected metal to sell at a scrapyard, car ownership still feels like a fantasy. “Don’t you want to see me practice my driving, Ma?” he asks Nainai, and this seems to erode her defenses. Sanshu listens with quiet eagerness. His days as Nainai’s caretaker are monotone in this seaside city, and like my father, he spends most days on the internet, his only portal to a larger world. Now, here was a chance to drive through it ourselves. Finally, Nainai sighs and agrees. “But we have all this food at home,” she adds with petulance.
On the train trip to Dandong, where the car is to be retrieved (from the compound, scene of the accident), we carry plastic bags of grapes, cabbage, steamed taro, sweet potatoes, and corn. The pork trotters are in there somewhere. The train travels north, making stops at rural towns in the waning light of dusk. The land is mostly flat, low hills hazy in the distance. The moon rises in the pale blue sky and the wind turbines are still; herons graze the glassy surface of a low-lying river.
At the train station in Dandong, my father announces he has “booked a leg of lamb.” It turns out he spent the entire three-hour train ride on Douyin, where he learned that a Mongolian restaurant’s leg of lamb had gone viral. He tried ordering it for takeout, but it has to be spit-roasted at the table. He announces our dinner plans to Sanshu and Nainai. “But what about all the food we have with us?” Nainai says, pointing to all the plastic bags. “Why spend money?”
“We’ll eat those later!” My dad commandeers a cab by the roadside as we exit. I can see he wants to start the trip on a high note, with a proper feast. I remember the childhood vacations when he sat pale as a restaurant bill arrived. Once, on Hainan Island for Chinese New Year, we ate instant ramen in the hotel room to save money. This is different. He is trying.
The Mongolian restaurant is in the city center, dingy and depressing. The tables around us fill up: each with a crate of beers next to it and an ashtray for the chain-smokers. I order some er’guotou for $2. The liquor tastes like synthetic ethanol, but goes down well with the roast lamb, which is succulent. I also order a cold salad of jellyfish and cucumber strands in vinegar, as well as Korean cold noodles in a kimchi-gochujang broth. This is a true borderland meal. Nainai sits with arms crossed across from me, refusing to eat a single morsel of lamb. “We’ve got food to eat at home,” she repeats.
Ignoring her hunger strike, my dad announces the plans for tomorrow: We will drive to Donggang, a nearby city, where Nainai will obtain some medicine she’s entitled to from her health insurance. This was, it is revealed, her primary motivation for agreeing to this trip.
After dinner, I hail a taxi, shamefully, to go to a hotel. I tried to convince Nainai to spend the night at the hotel with me, as I’ve booked myself a “superior riverside room.” I feel guilty for acting like I’m too good to stay at the housing project with the rest of my family. The apartment is small, with a single bathroom. There are not enough beds, I say, a wan excuse. “I’m not going to a hotel,” Nainai says. “And you could sleep in a bed with me.”
“Let her go,” my dad says. “She wants to see North Korea.” I am grateful for this diplomatic intervention. On the riverside avenue in Dandong, many hotels, including mine, are high-rises, which offer views across the border to North Korea. (On a review page for my hotel, someone asked: Do the rooms come equipped with binoculars? Sadly, they don’t, was the answer.)
After failing to convince me to take pears and boiled peanuts, my grandma shoves a mooncake my way before I close the cab door. The car drives along the riverside boulevard and its nighttime plaza dance. This is also an area of nightclubs, rent-by-the-hour karaoke, and entertainment centers. Hotels that double as banquet halls. The agricultural bank runs an LED message against border-zone money laundering.
Furuide, my hotel, is past this seedy stretch. When I walk in with my disintegrating REI backpack and tote bag (containing a plastic bag of Korean noodles doused in lamb soup, and the er’guotou), the reception staff eye me with suspicion. I show them my booking number. Since I’d reserved the superior riverview room, it turns out I’m on the top “executive” floor. They dutifully note my visa number, take a picture of the border entry stamp on my American passport, none of it casual. In my room, I find a free unrefrigerated peach yogurt, a little tub of cookies, and four bottles of water.
Someone sets off fireworks outside the window. And there it is, North Korea, across the bank. I can see deep into its dim night from the height of my 28th-floor room. As I stand by the window, I feel bewildered. It is perverse, this voyeurism into the pariah state, and yet I cannot help but look.
Instant coffee, powdered creamer, dense fog outside. I take a deep breath, put on my sneakers, and go down for a morning run along the river. The wind is fierce, and my Spotify running EDM playlist feels wrong—the soundtrack of American college parties. The North Korean shore has been built up tremendously in the six years since I was last here. A curious massive round tower, shaped like a rising moon, dominates the skyline. There are many new white towers nearby: modern condominiums. A lot of them are purportedly just for show, built for the Chinese to see, ghost towers with no lights at night. They call Kim Jong-un “Kim Three Fat” here, and word is that this year he’s come into some fortune shipping North Korean weapons to Putin, funding these real estate flexes. Along the Yalu River, here on the clean, wide promenade, there are panda-shaped binoculars you can use to look at North Korea by scanning a QR code. A little girl runs up to one, but she’s too short to reach the lenses.
The fog begins to recede, and the river sparkles in the sunshine. I shower quickly at the hotel and get a call from Sanshu that they are in the car downstairs, waiting. My father is cleaning the windshield of his grey SUV while blasting a groovy dance-pop Calvin Harris track on the stereo. I’ll come to learn he won’t drive without EDM, though I’ve never known him to be aware of the genre’s existence, let alone listen to it. Nainai is in the backseat propped against three pillows, and hands me a plastic bag of soy milk and fried dough sticks. She doesn’t have her seat belt on. I force her to buckle up. “But why?” she whines loudly. “Why are you tying me up?”
“She won’t want to take the car again if you do that,” my dad says. But I remain adamant. As retribution, she begins sending and reciting a series of voice memos on WeChat to her old medical school classmate: “Your daughter has found a partner? Wonderful! She’s 30 already? They’ll be married next year! Soon you’ll have a grandson in your arms!” She shouts into her phone over the music. I am 31, unwed, and childless, facts she likes to bring up often.
We drive south along the Yalu River. There is a new bridge into North Korea, but no cars driving across it. As we leave town, the compounds morph into fields and factories. On the riverside is a continuous barbed-wire fence. Every hundred meters I’m blinded by a sharp white flash: security cameras. “Foliage!” I point to a red shrub on the roadside. Besides occasional skinny trees, there are only mounds of dredged sand and stones along the road, stray corn stalks lining canals, shipyards clotted with destitute, rotting fishing boats. Men in faded camo pants search for crawfish in the swamp.
Dad drives at about 30 miles per hour, which I learn is his highest speed. Air that smells like fish and shrimp rot gushes through the open windows. We turn onto a small country road and drive through rural development zones before reaching the city of Donggang, where my father’s cousin Yin lives. She is helping us with Nainai’s medicine haul today. My dad parks, half his SUV jutting out into the sidewalk, in front of a hotel advertising “upscale, middle-scale, and low-scale rooms by the hour,” and an LED sign flashing no to drugs Yin and her husband arrive. They haven’t seen me since I was 3. “Lunch is on me,” my dad announces. We buy pounds of crab, clams, shrimp, jellyfish, giant sea slugs. Now, where to cook it?
We drive back to cousin Yin’s compound. It turns out my dad has never driven in a city, doesn’t know how to turn at a large intersection, or park in moderately narrow spaces. He drives with his foot on the brake the whole time, stopping and starting. Eventually we make it to the compound, and he and Nainai immediately park themselves on the couch. Sanshu goes into the guest bedroom to look at his phone. Who is going to help cousin Yin steam all this seafood we’ve invited ourselves to her house to eat? Me, of course.
The shrimp are ready first, then bowls of crabs, and clams and sea mollusks. We eat without plates, mounds of shells amassing in front of us as we hurl crabs at each other: “You eat, you eat.” Yin’s husband is a taxi driver who sometimes works for North Korean organizations that bring laborers to Chinese factories where they process seafood. They live in military-style dorms and are not allowed to mingle with the Chinese. He shuttles them from the factories to the dorms. “Do they prefer China, or do they want to go home?” my dad asks. “Home,” the cousin’s husband says. “They cannot stay here; there’s no future of marrying and settling. They’re not allowed.”
It is unsettling to think of the North Korean women living here as forced laborers, not quite emigrants, not quite immigrants. I know that for a long time, while growing up in this border town, the only North Koreans my father knew were ones rumored to work at restaurants or night halls. They had successfully gone west of the Yalu River, to the comparatively prosperous land of their Communist big brother. He himself had harbored dreams of going further west, leaving China, crossing borders, forging a life for himself in America, which he saw as a beacon of limitless opportunity.
The feast winds down, and my dad clamors that it is time to go. We are not going, I tell him, before we help clean up the mounds of trash we’ve just created on these relatives’ tables. Nainai is happily recording videos of the food and the leftovers, arranging the crab shells and cakes to fit her directorial aesthetic. I know she will mass-send the videos to every contact on WeChat. After a few tussles over us not taking the leftovers (“we already have many bags of food,” I promise cousin Yin), it is finally time for us to hit up the pharmacy. Nainai has 150 renminbi (RMB) to spend on her medical insurance allotment, except not on prescription medicine, only over-the-counter supplements. Still, she is like a child in a candy store. She peddles it all: TUMS equivalent? Caffeine pills? We end up with two enormous tubs of protein powder (which, alarmingly, she keeps calling “milk powder”), a dozen boxes of cough drops, and a jar of honey.
“Enough! Enough!” my dad is shouting. “I don’t want to drive when it’s dark!” It’s 4 p.m., and we left Dandong at 10:30 a.m. At his driving speed, we have a long journey back.
My dad hooks up a video of hardcore techno from Douyin for our drive back. He meets my amused gaze and explains: “Driving is more epic this way.” I don’t tell him I feel the same about thunderous bass, a soundtrack to so many of my runs. Nainai reveals to cousin Yin, on WeChat, that she’s hidden 200 RMB under a photo frame on their dresser, for the trouble of our lunch. She hates spending money, but is constantly hiding it in others’ houses. In the oncoming dusk, we drive past the same decrepit shipyards, swampy riverside, canals behind barbed wire. “Is your grandma asleep?” my dad asks over the techno.
I look over at her. Her eyes are nearly closed, but her thumb is swiping up the screen in a familiar motion. “No,” I say. “She’s watching reels.”
It is getting deeper into autumn, and the night comes fast. They drop me off the highway by the hotel, stopping illegally as I dart off. Alone in my room, I watch a show on salmon farming in the western borderlands, a movie on old Shanghai mobsters, and a dubbed documentary on “the most lethal animals in Africa.” I watch a giant iguana eat a snake, and a scorpion dissolve a grasshopper with acid. Something, perhaps the magpie I saw this morning, periodically whacks against my window. I drink last night’s er’guotou. Where am I, I think, where am I? I look across the river, but there isn’t much to see in the darkness. A Chinese island called Moon Island—a slim landmass of condominiums, a luxury hotel with a name that translates to Venice Country-Building Hotel, and one viral coffee shop—stands between my line of sight and North Korea. Its bridge is illuminated in bright blue. place of wellness, it says in red neon characters.
On our second day in Dandong, my dad has mapped out an itinerary that will take us into Manchuria. “Can we do that in a day?” I ask shortly after we’ve set off in the car.
“Probably not. We’ll find a hotel.”
“Does Nainai have her overnight medicine?”
Silence. I repeat the question. Finally, my dad says: “Ask your Sanshu.”
“Sanshu?”
He shrugs. “How would I know?”
“How are we going to sleep away if she can’t take her pills?”
“We’re sleeping away?” Nainai looks up from her phone. “Why?”
“A road trip, Ma. The fall foliage, remember?”
We turn the car around, back to the housing project in the Eight-way Ditch neighborhood, so that Nainai can assemble an overnight bag. The apartment is on the 20th floor, with an exposed communal balcony that gives me vertigo looking down. The staircase is blocked with discarded household items, a disastrous fire hazard. Nainai packs a towel in a plastic bag and asks Sanshu, “Three Baby, did you bring clean underwear?”
I dart out of the apartment before the ensuing scene. We drop back down in the elevator and hop into Dad’s car. It’s a gray and rainy day. We set out from Dandong, north along the Yalu River and the sinister towers under construction on the North Korean side. The river is a muddy greenish gray, but the skinny gingko trees along the road are turning gold. We pass by impoverished farm compounds, restaurants boasting North Korean specialties. The river banks are strewn with huge amounts of trash. The roadside eateries are decrepit, mostly empty or closed. There is a sign saying, we collect toads, make toad oil. My dad pulls a rotisserie chicken out of his backpack, and I tear into a thigh as we drive along. He must have bought it this morning, before picking me up from my hotel. My love for rotisserie chicken is a longtime family joke, but not one he nor I have mentioned in over a decade.
We enter an area known for its peach orchards, and the river widens. There are mountains on the other side, terraced fields, not lovely exactly, as the slopes look brown and vegetation poor, but it’s a relief to see what resembles normal nature after the hellish construction. The barbed-wire fence continues all along the river. The only actual North Koreans I see are along a river jetty some miles earlier, many of them laboring in dark clothes. We stop at a rural compound with gates open and three cars but no one around. My dad wanders through an empty banquet hall with tables pushed to the side. We stand outside in the lot. Everyone eats a peach. Finally, we stop by a decent hotel restaurant. There is no one in the dining hall except for a table of cleaning staff, sitting and gossiping. We eat a simple vegetable-heavy meal: shredded cucumber, sour cabbage and pork, salted duck eggs, and a chicken-and-mushroom stew. “I’m craving noodles,” my dad announces. “Simple noodles in clear broth.”
His tone has a childish insistence. The drive has made him hungry. He’s never hungry in Shanghai, where he eats leftovers alone late at night in front of his computer. So we order him noodles, and he slurps them with contentment. When was the last time I’ve seen him content? Maybe when I was 16, when we went to a beach and a wave slapped into him so suddenly he’d let out a peal of laughter and turned to me, bewildered. For a flash, I’d seen the little boy in him. That memory always makes me sad, the rarity of such a glimpse of defenselessness.
After leaving the restaurant, we wind along a narrow road, climbing in altitude, and some orange foliage begins to appear. It’s three in the afternoon when we turn inland, west toward the mountains, toward the Manchurian nature preserve area where we will spend the night. The cloud cover is gray and low, the rain intermittent. There are corn fields everywhere, and little hut-like mounds made from harvested stalks. Fog shrouds the bases of dark mountains. The atmosphere is gloomy and melancholy, but in the car, an EDM remix of “My Heart Will Go On” is blaring, and spirits are decent. My grandmother is off her phone because she’s received a message that she’s used most of her data. I’m directing and co-piloting my dad, and it occurs to me that this—right now—is probably the most we’ve talked in decades: Turn left, turn right, merge onto this state road.
He’s driven a long time now, probably more hours than he’s ever driven since learning, but he is in a good mood. He’s on a road trip in his home province where he grew up in poverty. He’s following open roads, taking his family on an adventure, a foliage tour.
In his 20s, my father met an American missionary who helped sponsor his visa and brought him to Bridgewater, Virginia, where he cleaned the hallways of a community college. He never learned how to drive there. America, it turned out, was not the place he thought it to be. Something had happened during his few years there, a fractured fantasy. After I was born, he returned to China. He never left again. He has, so far, survived his cancer; the operation had removed a 12-centimeter tumor. He paid for lunch, he’ll pay for the hotel tonight, they’ll be the nicest places we can find, which in this area won’t amount to much cost. Within these borders, he can summon a version of infinitude. I feel it, too, this freedom of a loose itinerary, of just driving north along the Yalu River, meandering among these tiny hamlets, chasing the sights of leaves in this bizarre borderland. So much bleakness, and yet when a mountainside is covered in layers of reddening leaves, a spark of yellow, a cluster of orange, there is beauty nonetheless.
We must get on bigger highways to cut west, into the Kuandian Manchu Autonomous County, and the fog gets heavier and the rain denser. I scroll on my phone to find a hotel near the park and show it to my dad, who nods. I plug it into his GPS. As night falls, it seems like the reserve and the hotel will never appear, but suddenly, a sharp left turn, and we enter through a gated kiosk. It is raining hard. At the visitor center, we are able to book a whole villa for less than $200, four rooms, each with our own bed, part of a gargantuan lakeside development. Cheap construction, no gas, water running rusty red out of the taps, waterbugs everywhere. But a whole house just for us! Three floors! We run up and down and up and down, Sanshu is taking a video, even Nainai is curious, wandering, climbing down stairs, opening every drawer. “How much?” she asks, and my dad lies: “400 RMB,” or $65 dollars, past acceptability for Nainai already. But we each get a breakfast voucher, and we have a massive dining table to eat at—no need to consume outside. We’ve brought our plastic bags of leftovers from lunch, the rotisserie chicken, and Nainai produces a plastic shower cap full of steamed taro. There are no plates or cutlery here, so we eat with our hands and out of mugs. A detached house is a foreign structure in these parts, where people are used to narrow apartments in towers. Nainai and Sanshu cannot stop commenting on the scale of the kitchen. There is clear wonder on their faces, and I can tell my father is pleased.
No one else wants to go into the national park tomorrow because tickets cost too much, so I’ll go alone. He brought us all the way here, and I will see some fall foliage. Since the water runs brown, I wash my face upstairs with bottled water, like a princess. The lampshades are a coarse imitation of a Coach handbag’s two-tone, brown-checkered fabric. Downstairs, an imitation fireplace. Outside, a man in a neighboring villa’s yard smoking, staring into the night.
It’s a cloudy, frigid morning, but there it is: fall foliage on the mountains around the artificial lake, right outside my bay window. It’s 7 a.m. and people are already outside, bundled up, posing for photos. We drive bright and early to the visitor center with our breakfast vouchers, just in time as tour buses unload hundreds of other diners. Breakfast is a feast for Nainai: braised tofu, seaweed salad, fermented cabbage, salted duck eggs, sweet potatoes, and hot soy milk, into which I mix a pack of instant coffee. Under duress, I eat the mooncake Nainai gave me two nights ago, while she watches triumphantly. I’m planning for a run in the national park, and since I’m the only one going in, the rest of the family returns to the villa (which Nainai keeps calling “the dorm”). I’m under strict orders to return in two hours—we have to drive back to Dandong and then make the train to Dalian.
Being one of the first visitors in the park, I have the fall foliage all to myself. It’s freezing cold, but the river flows over mossy rocks, and fiery red maples line the banks. The walkway ascends deeper and deeper up the valley, and I’m slow-jogging up the wet leaves. The employees opening up the morning stalls look at me with curiosity. This is it! Fall foliage! Nature! It’s marvelous. Suddenly I feel very proud of my dad and his TikTok vision, his months-old driver’s license. This may not be the most famous national park in China—it’s ranked only 4A, though every display outside the park is a genius advertising sleight-of-hand of striving 5A national park—but it’s beautiful. I run uphill until I have no signal. It’s foggy and drizzly up here. Downhill, downhill then. The scent of pines in the cold air. I’m flying. I make it back to the park gate by 10:30 a.m. The SUV pulls up. I’m ravenous and exhilarated. I hurl myself at the rose cake and red bean bread in the trunk. Then I buckle up Nainai, who cries: “I’m being tied up!” “That’s the whole point!” I shout. This is our bit by now.
We drive back toward Dandong, this time under a resplendent blue sky, the cornfields and multi-colored mountains a cinematic bucolic backdrop. Sanshu tells Nainai: “Ma, stop looking at your phone, look at the leaves outside!”
“My eyesight has gotten better these past two days,” Nainai says, “because I’ve been looking at my phone less, and the scenery more.”
“Then look!”
“Look at what? I’ve seen enough leaves.”
Basking in the sun, I could doze off. My dad’s driving is smoother and faster now, more confident. There is an ease to the SUV’s rumble. Soon we’re back along the Yalu River, and the hilly shore on the other side is also in bloom with a fall palette. The way back goes by fast. We return to Dandong by 1.30 p.m., up on the 20th floor of the housing project, and Sanshu is dispatched to bring back freshly boiled dumplings, chive and egg filling, from a hole-in-the-wall my dad likes downstairs. A sense of accomplishment hangs around the room, though no one says anything out loud. I eat a glutinous rice zongzi dipped in sugar. I could eat the whole world. Nainai looks on approvingly.
I did have one last wish for this trip, before our borderland jaunt concludes. We drive to the cemetery where my grandfather is buried. He died when I was a freshman in college, and I have never been to his tomb. “Row 14, number 11,” Nainai calls after us. She doesn’t go because it’s considered dangerous for older people to visit cemeteries—they may get pulled in by the ghosts beckoning from the underworld.
We stop by a flower shop at the bottom of the compound, where a young man carefully arranges 11 chrysanthemums. We drive in the brisk, cloudless afternoon to the cemetery up on a hill, semi-feral dogs scampering off the wide paths. It is golden hour when we descend a staircase bisecting rows of densely packed tombstones. My dad and Sanshu, not having listened to Nainai, meander into row 12, searching and scanning each headstone for their father.
I’m carrying the flowers, and I walk straight to row 14, number 11. There he is. Wang Mingjun. I call my dad and Sanshu over. They are in a terrible rush because we have to catch the train, but Sanshu brushes some pine needles off the grave. I set down the flowers, and my dad tells me to kneel and touch my head to the tombstone. I do, feeling the cold black marble. I am his child then. I remember the old, smiling man who waited for me outside school gates when I was small, his face the face of my father and my own. I breathe in deep and start back up the stone stairs after my uncle and my father, who are waiting under the pines.
In the following weeks, back in Shanghai, my father returns to his computer, to long hours staring numbly at internet videos, his SUV parked tightly in a compound up north, thousands of miles away. But something has shifted between us. In the final days of my visit, he brings me plates of peeled pomelo. Sliced apples await on the dining room table. We return to another hospital by bike, and though he mostly doesn’t wait for me at intersections, occasionally he glances back as I pedal to catch up. In the examination room, when the doctor gazes at me curiously and my father opens his mouth, I brace myself.
“My daughter,” he says.
Again I stay silent. And I know what has shifted: the winding road along the Yalu River, a fast-coming bend, a flash of crimson in the hills. When he was a young man, this might have been the essence of what my father dreamed of: open roads, miles and miles of unknown potential ahead. No true destination. Handing his mother a peach, watching his daughter race down a slope, amid splendid leaves, back to her family. I don’t know if I had ever seen him more alive than on that drive.
And I was alongside him the entire time. Carefully reading out instructions for the road as he listened. Navigation, the only mode of speech we shared, unfamiliar but safe.
Turn right, turn left, slow down. It’s coming up ahead. We are here.
Aube Rey Lescure is a French-Chinese-American author and editor-in-chief of the literary magazine Off Assignment. Her debut novel, River East, River West, was shortlisted for the 2024 Women’s Prize for Fiction, the Carol Shields Prize, the Massachusetts Book Award, and the Maya Angelou Book Award, among others. Her writing has appeared in The New York Times, Granta, Guernica, AFAR, LitHub, Electric Literature, and more. Her essay “At the Bend of the Road” was selected for The Best American Essays 2022.
Editor and fact-checker: Cheri Lucas Rowlands
Copyeditor: Carolyn Wells

About these screens
Every screen was captured in ScummVM, except the Atari ST monochrome screens, which come from the Hatari emulator. Each capture was scaled to 1600x1200 with nearest-neighbour pixels and 4:3 aspect correction, so every original pixel keeps the shape it had on a period monitor.
The twelve moments are the same in every version, with the same framing, so switching versions changes the machine, not the moment. The PC 16-color screens come from a Spanish copy and the Atari ST mono ones from a French copy; all the others are in English.
Table of contents
Drag a title bar to move a window. Press Esc to go back to the viewer.
Char: Decimal: 0 Hex: 00
PxPlus IBM VGA 9x16 by VileR (int10h.org), CC BY-SA 4.0. Stretched x1.35 here for 4:3, shared under the same licence.
- Emulators
- ScummVM, and Hatari for the Atari ST mono screens
- Output
- 1600 x 1200 pixels per screen
[X] Nearest-neighbour scaling
[X] 4:3 aspect correction
[X] The same 12 moments in every version
The Mac draws a 640x400 picture inside a 640x480 screen, so it shows black bars. The Hercules picture uses the middle 640 of its 720 columns.
The CRT monitors of DOSBox Staging, ported to WebGL2: its presets for Hyllian's CRT shader, and its own VGA shaders where a screen has few pixels. Each version is drawn from its native pixels on the monitor closest to its own, with a whole number of screen pixels for every scanline where the size allows.
- PC, FM Towns
- 31 kHz VGA monitor. Each of the 200 lines is drawn twice: 400 fine scanlines.
- Mac
- The same kind of monitor, at 480 lines.
- EGA, CGA
- The 31 / 15 kHz button swaps the VGA monitor for the card's own 15.7 kHz one: 200 lines.
- Amiga, ST, Sega CD
- 15 kHz monitor or TV, with DOSBox's arcade presets: dark gaps between the 200 lines.
- Hercules, ST mono
- No mask. Hercules in white, green or amber phosphor.
[X] On in the viewer: the CRT button under the picture, or R
[X] Scanlines need pixels: zoom in where the window is too small
[X] Every setting in the CRT lab, on the menu bar

VGA 1080p shaders: Copyright (C) 2020-2024 The DOSBox Staging Team, GNU GPL v2 or later. Built for serious scale.

Next to your database, in the cloud and region you choose.
Same work. A fraction of the bill.
Cloudflare's meters, priced on raw AWS.
- 4.8×

No runtime limits.
128 MB is all the memory a Cloudflare Worker gets. OpenOrange runs your Workers and Durable Objects on machines you choose, so the limits are your hardware's.
Don't sacrifice your DX.
Your wrangler.jsoncBindings, environments, build commands and .dev.vars work as they do with Wrangler. stays the source of truth. Same commands, new name.
Get started.
Run this project's Cloudflare Worker on Rivet with OpenOrange. First, ask me where it should run: on my machine, on a Rivet cluster, or on my own Rivet Engine. Then read https://github.com/rivet-dev/open-orange/blob/main/README.md and follow its quickstart. Do not change my app code.
- Step 1: Install Install the orange CLI globally, then run it in your Worker project. Your code and your Wrangler configuration do not change.
$ pnpm add -g open-orange
- Step 2: Run your Worker Reads your Wrangler configuration. Each Durable Object runs as a Rivet Actor with its own SQLite database. $ orange dev ● Ready in 0.9s Local http://127.0.0.1:8787
- Step 3: Deploy Builds an image of your Worker and rolls it out to your own cloud (AWS, GCP, Azure) or on-prem servers. Or use Rivet Cloud: a fully managed, dedicated cluster on AWS or Google Cloud, in the region you choose. $ orange login $ orange link --cluster <name> $ orange deploy ✓ Deployed my-worker URL https://api.example.com
Migrate your data without downtime.
orange migrate copies every Durable Object and D1*Cloudflare doesn't provide a way to migrate D1 live, so requests to a D1 database are interrupted while its data copies. This takes a few minutes. database from Cloudflare while your app keeps serving. No maintenance window, no lost writes.
Supported bindings.
Every Cloudflare binding OpenOrange runs, plus the fetch handler and runtime globals. Each is documented with its status, configuration and known differences from Cloudflare.
Questions.
Wait, wasn't Cloudflare supposed to be cheaper?
For small, spiky Workers, it often is: the free tier and per-request pricing are hard to beat. The meters add up once Durable Objects stay busy. Duration is billed per GB-second while an object is active, and every SQLite row written costs $1 per million. The same GB-hours on Graviton EC2 cost about a fifth as much, and writes cost a fraction of that.
Is OpenOrange a fork of Cloudflare?
No. OpenOrange implements the Workers API on Node.js and maps each Cloudflare service onto Rivet. It uses Wrangler to read your configuration, and it reuses some MIT-licensed Cloudflare code, such as the Workflows engine. It does not run workerd.
Why not just run workerd directly?
workerd is Cloudflare's runtime, not its platform. It runs your fetch handler, but KV, D1, R2, Queues, Workflows and the Cache API are bindings that call services you have to build, and Durable Objects keep their data on one machine with nothing to place, replicate or scale them. It also keeps limits designed around Cloudflare's network rather than your servers: only the nodejs_compat subset of Node.js, no native npm modules, and alarms capped at 15 minutes. OpenOrange runs your Worker on Node.js with real SQLite, and Rivet provides the services, so your code keeps working on infrastructure you control. Compare OpenOrange and workerd
Do I need to learn Rivet?
No. Rivet is the control plane underneath. You use the orange CLI and your Wrangler configuration.
What does it cost?
OpenOrange is Apache 2.0 and free. Self-host Rivet and your cloud bill is the only bill. Rivet Cloud and BYOC pricing is at rivet.dev/pricing.
What happens to the data already in KV, Durable Objects and R2?
orange migrate moves Durable Objects while your app serves, with no downtime. D1 databases are interrupted for a few minutes while they copy, because Cloudflare doesn't provide a way to migrate D1 live. R2 is S3-compatible, so rclone or aws s3 sync moves buckets.
Does it run at the edge?
It runs wherever you deploy it, in as many regions as you want. Most backends talk to one database; running next to it is faster than running at the edge. For stateless Worker requests, this works fine today. For Durable Objects, an upcoming Rivet update adds multi-region support, including edge regions.
Who maintains OpenOrange?
Rivet. OpenOrange is not affiliated with or endorsed by Cloudflare, Inc.
Keep the platform. Lose the walls.
Your Cloudflare project in your datacenter.
Built for serious scale.

Windows 0-Day Dropped By Disgruntled Researcher Hands Hackers System Rights
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
03:30:00 UTC
12:30:00 KST

Windows 0-Day Dropped By Disgruntled Researcher Hands Hackers System Rights (arstechnica.com) 34
			
		 	
				An anonymous reader quotes a report from Ars Technica: Nightmare Eclipse, the pseudonym of a researcher with an ongoing, very public beef with Microsoft, has dropped yet another Windows zero-day. On Tuesday, Nightmare Eclipse disclosed SignDrop, a vulnerability that gives local users full System rights on some versions of Windows. The vulnerability resides in the Common Log File System, which Microsoft describes as a "general-purpose logging service used by software clients running in user-mode or kernel-mode." By forcing a privileged process such as Windows Defender to open a crafted CLFS container, an attacker with ordinary user rights can abuse the privileged file creation to elevate privileges to System.
[...] Researcher Will Dormann said in an interview that he has been able to reproduce the exploit and that it works as described, but not on all versions of Windows 11. But there's a catch, he said. "The requirements are important in that it seems to require Windows 26H2, and one that has existed for more than 90 days, it seems," he explained. "That is, you can't just take a new 26H2 system and install updates and have it work." For a system to be vulnerable, Dormann said, the HKEY_LOCAL_MACHINE\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Services\CLFS\Authentication\Mode
value must be 0 or 1." Of the many machines Dormann uses, only a single one met this requirement. Microsoft said in a statement that it was investigating the vulnerability and had no evidence it was being exploited in the wild. The company also said Defender blocks potential exploitation attempts.

Nvidia allegedly engineered scarcity by funding AI startups to buy its GPUs
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
02:52:59 UTC
11:52:59 KST

Friday, November 13th 2026
NVIDIA Allegedly Engineered Scarcity By Funding AI Startups To Buy Its GPUs
Following the departure of now-former NVIDIA CEO, Jensen Huang, a new report by The Wall Street Journal (via Wccftech) has emerged alleging that NVIDIA artificially inflated the ongoing GPU and memory shortage by investing in AI startups that would go on to buy NVIDIA GPUs and build data centers. The reason behind this was to maintain the perception of ongoing high demand and to move an oversupply of semiconductors from TSMC. 
The WSJ report cites an August meeting between Huang and his senior finance team, including NVIDIA's CFO and acting CEO, Colette Kress, during which Kress pushed for a reduction in circular financing agreements, in response to which Huang allegedly lay into Kress, calling her a "deer in the headlights," after she had pushed to scale back NVIDIA's $250 billion deal with OpenAI. Jensen's push for further investment into small-scale cloud providers was seemingly in response to the changing tide that saw AI hyperscalers move away from off-the-shelf components in favor of custom solutions.The situation described in the report saw NVIDIA on the hook for an "avalanche" of GPUs from TSMC, for which it would have no immediate buyers. Part of the issue highlighted by Kress was also that these small-scale cloud providers NVIDIA was investing in could not afford to buy NVIDIA GPUs, meaning they, too, could be a financial dead end.
Sources:
				The Wall Street Journal, 				via Wccftech

Ggml v1.0.0
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
02:28:29 UTC
11:28:29 KST

Repository navigation
Overview
ggml 1.0.0 prunes the deprecated public API and the legacy DIAG_MASK ops, adds configurable YaRN truncation and FP8 support, and brings broad performance work across backends: few-row quantized matmuls and MoE fusion on CUDA/Metal, a Vulkan prefill FlashAttention path, a device-side MoE scheduler on SYCL, decomposed Adreno FlashAttention in OpenCL, new WebGPU kernels, expanded Hexagon op coverage, and scheduler improvements that avoid uploading unused inputs.
API changes
- Pruned deprecated API and removed the legacy DIAG_MASK_INF/DIAG_MASK_ZERO ops, bumping the RPC protocol to reject incompatible peers (llama/29801, llama/31946)
- Added ggml_rope_set_truncate/ggml_rope_get_truncate to control YaRN correction-bound rounding (llama/30206, llama/30802)
- Implemented the backward pass for TRI (llama/30712)
- Added MUL_MAT src1 precision tests and gating for ggml_prec_set_acc (llama/31896, llama/31851)
- Bumped the RPC protocol to v9 with faster loading and graceful failure on unreachable endpoints or failed handshakes (llama/29413, llama/30415)
Core changes
- Scheduler skips uploading inputs that are not read and prefetches only inputs produced by earlier splits (llama/31419, llama/32100)
- MoE cache can compute a share of the cache misses on the CPU and copy the results back asynchronously (llama/30966)
- Meta backend raises the compute view limit for buffers with few tensors (llama/31447)
Backend changes
CUDA
- Added few-row MMQ for batch sizes 2-24 and MMVF for BF16 few-row matmuls (llama/31533, llama/31959)
- Added OCP FP8 support and fused NVFP4 gate/up matmuls with GLU in MMQ (llama/28413, llama/31296)
- Extended FlashAttention with an sm70 head-size-256 config and MMA head sizes 40/72 (llama/27997, llama/30285)
- Improved graph handling: skip compat checks on reuse, re-instantiate on cluster-dim changes, move graph state (llama/32004, llama/31284, llama/32001)
- MoE: skip empty MMQ stream-k tiles, linearize the MoE layout, and sanitize post-bias NaNs (llama/32067, llama/31047, llama/31005)
- Added a debug buffer pool and halved the VMM pool reservation with reserve retries (llama/31919, llama/30542)
- HIP: use a fixed BLAS workspace and add an MFMA lightning indexer for CDNA2 (llama/31838, llama/29050)
- Added a d_state=64 SSM_SCAN fast path and made radix Top-K deterministic for ties (llama/31818, llama/31084)
Metal
- Fused MoE routing and kept fusable node groups inside a single command buffer (llama/31615, llama/31688)
- Made radix Top-K deterministic for ties, added mul_mm for 32<=K<64, and switched TRI to select (llama/31084, llama/30982, llama/30711)
- Fixed GROUP_NORM for ne03>1 and out-of-bounds accesses in the FA output and SOLVE_TRI kernels (llama/31852, llama/31662, llama/32103)
Vulkan
- Added an Intel FlashAttention prefill kernel (llama/29357)
- Added batched tensor copies, a test-fusion interface, and ReBAR detection (llama/31418, llama/30769, llama/31658)
- Enabled dequantized KV with CM2, YaRN truncation, RDNA3/4 large-tile split-K, and large coopmat matmul on newer AMD drivers (llama/31526, llama/30802, llama/30280, llama/31587)
- Added a tiled-transpose concat shader and FWHT kernels for block widths above 512 (llama/30591, llama/29772)
- Fixed MoltenVK out-of-bounds accesses, GROUP_NORM workgroup limits, and FA scratch sizing (llama/31890, llama/31930, llama/30487)
- Capped scalar FlashAttention at 8 rows for large heads on Qualcomm (llama/30829)
SYCL
- Added a device-side MoE scheduler (llama/30156)
- Made the oneDNN FA cache thread-safe and un-gated fused SDPA for Alchemist at head_dim=64 (llama/31138, llama/31574)
- Cached the device id and reject buffers that would exceed VRAM (llama/31126, llama/32013, llama/31285)
- Added exact rounding for CPY to Q4_1/Q5_1/Q8_0, strided CLAMP, and f16 SWIGLU_OAI (llama/31465, llama/31784, llama/30857)
- Fixed AOT for allocations above 4GB (llama/31873)
OpenCL
- Added decomposed Adreno FlashAttention and improved decode performance (llama/32046, llama/31633)
- Added q6_k tiled, q8_0, and f32/q5_k multi-column GEMV kernels and f32 MUL_MAT_ID (llama/31758, llama/30947, llama/31783)
- Added arange, non-contiguous sigmoid, repeat for f16/i32/i16/bf16, and DSV4 HC pre/post kernels (llama/31829, llama/31687, llama/31782, llama/31623)
- Enabled multi-column GEMV by default for Adreno X2 (llama/31694)
WebGPU
- Added batched tensor API support and returned maxBufferSize from get_max_size (llama/31473, llama/31348)
- Added GROUP_NORM, MEAN, COUNT_EQUAL, and PAD_REFLECT_1D kernels (llama/31490, llama/31591, llama/31578, llama/31877)
- Supported the YaRN truncation config (llama/30802)
Hexagon
- Added Q5_0/Q5_1 support and optimized conv_2d while adding conv_3d (llama/32094, llama/31814)
- Fused post-norm residual add into RMS_NORM_MUL and added set_tensor_n (llama/31822, llama/31744)
- Added automatic libhtp loading and fixed 64-bit FastRPC handle truncation (llama/31781, llama/30952)
- Fixed the host build with clang-cl on Windows ARM64 (llama/32011)
CPU
- Switched MUL_MAT to F32 src1 and accumulate FlashAttention V in F32 (llama/31854, llama/29810)
- Added SpacemiT GATED_DELTA_NET RVV and Q4_1 32x256 HP kernels and enabled IME2 MXFP4 repack (llama/31897, llama/31929, llama/31776)
- Enabled the 4x4 simd_gemm tile on ARM NEON and added bf16/q8_0 VXE to llamafile (llama/32128, llama/31866)
CANN
- Fail early when a buffer type is unsupported (llama/31270)
ZDNN
- Accept non-zdnn tensors, invalidate compute tensors after each op, and fix release backend lib detection (llama/31824, llama/31905)
OpenVINO
- Supported the YaRN truncation config (llama/30802)
More info

Ggml v1.0.0
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
02:28:29 UTC
11:28:29 KST

Repository navigation
Overview
ggml 1.0.0 prunes the deprecated public API and the legacy DIAG_MASK ops, adds configurable YaRN truncation and FP8 support, and brings broad performance work across backends: few-row quantized matmuls and MoE fusion on CUDA/Metal, a Vulkan prefill FlashAttention path, a device-side MoE scheduler on SYCL, decomposed Adreno FlashAttention in OpenCL, new WebGPU kernels, expanded Hexagon op coverage, and scheduler improvements that avoid uploading unused inputs.
API changes
- Pruned deprecated API and removed the legacy DIAG_MASK_INF/DIAG_MASK_ZERO ops, bumping the RPC protocol to reject incompatible peers (llama/29801, llama/31946)
- Added ggml_rope_set_truncate/ggml_rope_get_truncate to control YaRN correction-bound rounding (llama/30206, llama/30802)
- Implemented the backward pass for TRI (llama/30712)
- Added MUL_MAT src1 precision tests and gating for ggml_prec_set_acc (llama/31896, llama/31851)
- Bumped the RPC protocol to v9 with faster loading and graceful failure on unreachable endpoints or failed handshakes (llama/29413, llama/30415)
Core changes
- Scheduler skips uploading inputs that are not read and prefetches only inputs produced by earlier splits (llama/31419, llama/32100)
- MoE cache can compute a share of the cache misses on the CPU and copy the results back asynchronously (llama/30966)
- Meta backend raises the compute view limit for buffers with few tensors (llama/31447)
Backend changes
CUDA
- Added few-row MMQ for batch sizes 2-24 and MMVF for BF16 few-row matmuls (llama/31533, llama/31959)
- Added OCP FP8 support and fused NVFP4 gate/up matmuls with GLU in MMQ (llama/28413, llama/31296)
- Extended FlashAttention with an sm70 head-size-256 config and MMA head sizes 40/72 (llama/27997, llama/30285)
- Improved graph handling: skip compat checks on reuse, re-instantiate on cluster-dim changes, move graph state (llama/32004, llama/31284, llama/32001)
- MoE: skip empty MMQ stream-k tiles, linearize the MoE layout, and sanitize post-bias NaNs (llama/32067, llama/31047, llama/31005)
- Added a debug buffer pool and halved the VMM pool reservation with reserve retries (llama/31919, llama/30542)
- HIP: use a fixed BLAS workspace and add an MFMA lightning indexer for CDNA2 (llama/31838, llama/29050)
- Added a d_state=64 SSM_SCAN fast path and made radix Top-K deterministic for ties (llama/31818, llama/31084)
Metal
- Fused MoE routing and kept fusable node groups inside a single command buffer (llama/31615, llama/31688)
- Made radix Top-K deterministic for ties, added mul_mm for 32<=K<64, and switched TRI to select (llama/31084, llama/30982, llama/30711)
- Fixed GROUP_NORM for ne03>1 and out-of-bounds accesses in the FA output and SOLVE_TRI kernels (llama/31852, llama/31662, llama/32103)
Vulkan
- Added an Intel FlashAttention prefill kernel (llama/29357)
- Added batched tensor copies, a test-fusion interface, and ReBAR detection (llama/31418, llama/30769, llama/31658)
- Enabled dequantized KV with CM2, YaRN truncation, RDNA3/4 large-tile split-K, and large coopmat matmul on newer AMD drivers (llama/31526, llama/30802, llama/30280, llama/31587)
- Added a tiled-transpose concat shader and FWHT kernels for block widths above 512 (llama/30591, llama/29772)
- Fixed MoltenVK out-of-bounds accesses, GROUP_NORM workgroup limits, and FA scratch sizing (llama/31890, llama/31930, llama/30487)
- Capped scalar FlashAttention at 8 rows for large heads on Qualcomm (llama/30829)
SYCL
- Added a device-side MoE scheduler (llama/30156)
- Made the oneDNN FA cache thread-safe and un-gated fused SDPA for Alchemist at head_dim=64 (llama/31138, llama/31574)
- Cached the device id and reject buffers that would exceed VRAM (llama/31126, llama/32013, llama/31285)
- Added exact rounding for CPY to Q4_1/Q5_1/Q8_0, strided CLAMP, and f16 SWIGLU_OAI (llama/31465, llama/31784, llama/30857)
- Fixed AOT for allocations above 4GB (llama/31873)
OpenCL
- Added decomposed Adreno FlashAttention and improved decode performance (llama/32046, llama/31633)
- Added q6_k tiled, q8_0, and f32/q5_k multi-column GEMV kernels and f32 MUL_MAT_ID (llama/31758, llama/30947, llama/31783)
- Added arange, non-contiguous sigmoid, repeat for f16/i32/i16/bf16, and DSV4 HC pre/post kernels (llama/31829, llama/31687, llama/31782, llama/31623)
- Enabled multi-column GEMV by default for Adreno X2 (llama/31694)
WebGPU
- Added batched tensor API support and returned maxBufferSize from get_max_size (llama/31473, llama/31348)
- Added GROUP_NORM, MEAN, COUNT_EQUAL, and PAD_REFLECT_1D kernels (llama/31490, llama/31591, llama/31578, llama/31877)
- Supported the YaRN truncation config (llama/30802)
Hexagon
- Added Q5_0/Q5_1 support and optimized conv_2d while adding conv_3d (llama/32094, llama/31814)
- Fused post-norm residual add into RMS_NORM_MUL and added set_tensor_n (llama/31822, llama/31744)
- Added automatic libhtp loading and fixed 64-bit FastRPC handle truncation (llama/31781, llama/30952)
- Fixed the host build with clang-cl on Windows ARM64 (llama/32011)
CPU
- Switched MUL_MAT to F32 src1 and accumulate FlashAttention V in F32 (llama/31854, llama/29810)
- Added SpacemiT GATED_DELTA_NET RVV and Q4_1 32x256 HP kernels and enabled IME2 MXFP4 repack (llama/31897, llama/31929, llama/31776)
- Enabled the 4x4 simd_gemm tile on ARM NEON and added bf16/q8_0 VXE to llamafile (llama/32128, llama/31866)
CANN
- Fail early when a buffer type is unsupported (llama/31270)
ZDNN
- Accept non-zdnn tensors, invalidate compute tensors after each op, and fix release backend lib detection (llama/31824, llama/31905)
OpenVINO
- Supported the YaRN truncation config (llama/30802)

Nvidia director sold nearly $1B in stock weeks before the Jensen Huang scandal
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
02:21:36 UTC
11:21:36 KST

Nvidia director sold nearly $1B in stock weeks before the Jensen Huang scandal broke — and no trading plan was flagged
Nvidia's second-largest shareholder sold about $946 million in company stock in the months leading up to CEO Jensen Huang's resignation on Nov. 12.
Nvidia [NASDAQ: NVDA] Board Director Mark Stevens was one of the top AI sellers in Q3 2026, selling roughly 4.2 million shares, according to filings with the Securities and Exchange Commission. There were three separate batches of stock sales from Stevens, who joined the Nvidia board in 2008 following an earlier stint as a director from 1993 to 2006.
Must Read
- Jeff Bezos backs a platform that lets anyone invest in rental homes for as little as $100 — 5 ways to build wealth like a landlord without actually being one
- Dave Ramsey warns nearly 50% of Americans are making 1 big Social Security mistake. Here's what it is and 3 simple steps to fix it ASAP
- A single line on your car insurance policy could be inflating your premium by up to 30% — here's what to change
The first common stock sale took place from Aug. 31 to Sept. 2 at equity prices ranging from $220 to $226, generating $411 million in new cash. The second sale stretched from Sept. 3 to Sept. 4 at prices ranging between $227 to $234 per share, garnering about $236 million. The third sale occurred on Sept. 18 at roughly $220 per share, netting roughly $300 million.
Still holding plenty of Nvidia stock
Prearranged trading plans are used by executives to buy or sell shares of their firm on specific dates or target prices. It's a mechanism to wall off company employees from insider trading accusations as long as they stick to the automatic plan.
The SEC first instituted the practice in 2000 and modified it in 2022 to include a 90-day waiting period for starting trades. In some cases, the plans were abused by executives in instances like executing share sales just before an earnings report.
In Stevens's case, he didn't have such a trading plan, but that's not necessarily a red flag on its own. Insiders cashing out of their stock will do so for a variety of reasons that aren't always tied to a company's performance, such as quickly raising liquidity. In Nvidia's case, the move could pour water on the enthusiasm of retail investors to buy into an AI titan that has also attracted short sellers.
The first two stock sales in late August and early September followed a robust Q2 earnings report that caused Nvidia shares to jump about 10% in valuein the following week. Stevens still holds about 27.5 million shares in Nvidia, a colossal position.

Security Agencies Worldwide Found to Use Ad-Based Surveillance System
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
02:15:41 UTC
11:15:41 KST

Security Agencies Worldwide Found to Use Ad-Based Surveillance System
An investigation by Bloomberg into Penlink’s ad-based surveillance system Webloc builds off Citizen Lab research, identifying additional customers of the tracking technology. The findings that Webloc has been acquired by security agencies in a number of countries aligns with potential customers we identified in our April report.
Senior researcher Wolfie Christl notes that Webloc “is a powerful mass surveillance tool that’s trickling down from military and intelligence agencies to federal law enforcement and then to state and local police.”
Our report used open source research, freedom of information requests, and server infrastructure mapping to identify Webloc customers in the US, El Salvador, and Hungary. Since then, the Austrian Ministry of the Interior has also been confirmed as a customer. The Bloomberg investigation identified customers in additional countries including the UK, Netherlands, Germany, Mexico, Singapore, Saudi Arabia, UAE, and Israel.
The lawfulness of using these systems in Europe and the UK is highly controversial. While intrusive and disproportionate ad-based mass surveillance is problematic everywhere, its use in authoritarian countries identified by reporting is particularly dangerous.

Elephants found to be increasingly isolated leading to fractured social networks
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
02:02:42 UTC
11:02:42 KST

Elephants Found to be Increasingly Isolated, Leading to Fractured Social Networks
With a troubling decline in the population, researchers warn of a tipping point for the highly social animals
Published Date
Article Content
Key Takeaways
- UC San Diego researchers studied the social dynamics of hundreds of Asian elephants over more than a decade in Sri Lanka.
- They tracked a decrease in the population of the highly social animals that has led to fractured and more isolated communities.
- Surpassing a social “tipping point,” they warn, can be detrimental to the future of the animals.
Humans and many other social animals thrive in well-established community networks. Asian elephants are one example of a species that flourishes in groups that work together to access food and care for their young, among other social bonds.
University of California San Diego scientists in the School of Biological Sciences studying the intricacies of Asian elephant community structures have documented a troubling downturn in the population of the animals resulting in fractured social communities. Their study is published on the cover of the November 12 issue of Science.
Analyzing the social dynamics of more than 440 wild female Asian elephants over 12 years at the Udawalawe National Park in Sri Lanka, the researchers studied key shifts in the demographics of the elephants that seemed to trigger social communities becoming smaller and more isolated.
“We found that gradual accumulation of losses from turnover preceded sudden change in the social networks among females in a wild population of Asian elephants,” the researchers explained in the study, which was led by Postdoctoral Researcher Anastasia Madsen from the laboratory of Associate Professor Shermin de Silva in the Department of Ecology, Behavior and Evolution. de Silva also serves as president and founder of the conservation nonprofit Trunks & Leaves Inc.
The researchers’ analysis indicates a social structure tipping point. As the number of elephants in the population decreased over time, due to a combination of likely deaths, permanent emigrations of elephants and fewer incoming individuals, the population as a whole appears to have crossed a critical threshold that resulted in a shift from large, densely connected social communities, to smaller and less-connected social groupings.
“We see individual elephants leaving and possibly dying at accelerating rates, but new individuals are not coming into the population,” said Madsen. “With fewer individuals in the population, those who are left will naturally have fewer relationships. However, the remaining elephants are also changing their relationships with each other.”
This new dynamic is a cause for concern, says Madsen, since the animals leverage their social groups and connections to forage, acquire information and raise young.
“We can probably all relate to the feeling of social isolation brought about, paradoxically, with the advent of digital communications that seem to remove the physical constraints of needing to be in the same place at the same time,” said de Silva. “But for many social species, which includes ourselves as much as elephants, we have to remember that social connectivity is very much a matter of sharing space and building relationships throughout our lives. Crucially, in order to have resilient communities, we need to be able to replace the relationships that are lost over time.”
Many elephant populations are already impacted through deaths resulting from poaching and conflicts with humans. Other contributing factors may be disruptions in habitat connectivity and less food available for the elephants as ecological conditions change across the 119-square-mile park and surroundings.
The new social fracturing structure observed by the scientists may not be easily repaired, the researchers warn.
“In general, we need to be more aware of these types of tipping points where we see sudden shifts after changes have been slowly building up,” said Madsen. “Once we move past the tipping point, it’s much harder to go back to previous states.”
The new findings will serve future studies seeking to understand shifts in animal society structures, how to detect such tranformations and the factors responsible for vulnerabilities. The study also stimulates ongoing discussions for conserving elephant populations as humans expand into areas where elephants historically thrived.
“We are gaining more evidence that studying social patterns is an important tool for us to see when things are going wrong in natural populations and, hopefully, to be able to do something about it,” said Madsen.
The researchers of the study were: Anastasia E. Madsen, T.V. Pushpakumara, U. Sameera Weerathunga, Devaka K. Weerakoon and Shermin de Silva.
Funding for the research was provided by the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Asian Elephant Conservation Grants (F11AP00258, F14AP00256 and F17AP00308); National Science Foundation (grant DBI-2305860); University of Pennsylvania; UC San Diego; and Trunks & Leaves Inc.
