- low: efficient reasoning optimizing for speed and cost
- none
Run Qwen3.8 Guide
You can now run Qwen3.8-27B in llama.cpp and Unsloth Desktop. Feel free to change quantization type.
- Hugging Face: Qwen3.8-27B-GGUF • Qwen3.8-27B-NVFP4
- ModelScope: Qwen3.8-27B-GGUF • Qwen3.8-27B-NVFP4
🦥 Run Qwen3.8 in Unsloth Desktop
Qwen3.8 can run in Unsloth Desktop, an open-source UI app for local AI. Unsloth automatically offloads to RAM and detects multiGPU setups. With Unsloth Desktop, you can run models locally on MacOS, Windows, Linux and:
- Search, download, run GGUFs, MLX and safetensor models
- Self-healing tool calling + web search
- Code execution (Python, Bash)
- Automatic inference parameter tuning (temp, top-p, etc.)
- Fast CPU + GPU inference via MLX and llama.cpp
- Train LLMs 2x faster with 70% less VRAM

⚡️NVFP4
Like Qwen3.6, we’re also releasing new dynamic NVFP4 Qwen3.8-27B quants that run ~1.5× faster than BF16 checkpoints, with better performance and comparable file sizes. Run Qwen3.8-27B NVFP4 1.5x faster on 24GB VRAM. We also added FP8 KV cache calibration for 2x longer context lengths! NVFP4 requires NVIDIA's Blackwell GPUs like RTX 50X, DGX Spark (see Qwen3.8-27B), B200, B300 GPUs. For older GPUs, our GGUFs work well! Qwen3.8-2.4T-A95B
Qwen3.8-2.4T-A95B is a 2.4T parameter (95B active) model with rivaling GPT-5.6 Sol. Full-precision Qwen3.8-2.4T-A95B requires 4.9TB of storage and 1-bit Unsloth Dynamic GGUFs takes 397GB (91% smaller), and larger IQ1_S takes 508GB.

Recommended Settings
Qwen3.8-2.4T is thinking-only, while Qwen3.8-Max is hybrid.
temperature = 1.0
top_p = 0.95
top_k = 20
min_p = 0.0
presence_penalty = 0.0
- Context length = up to 1,010,000
- temperature=1.0, top_p=0.95, top_k=20, min_p=0.0, presence_penalty=0.0, repetition_penalty=1.0
If the model fits, you will get ~20 tokens/s generation when using B200s and >120 tokens / s throughput. Best rule of thumb: RAM+VRAM ≈ the quant size; otherwise it’ll still work, just much slower due to disk offloading.
Run Qwen3.8-2.4T Guide
You can now run Qwen3.8 in llama.cpp and Unsloth Desktop. For the large Qwen3.8-2.T model, we will be utilizing the 397GB IQ1_XXXS quant (named Q1_0) for best results in terms of accessibility and accuracy and it will require at least 450GB RAM. Feel free to change quantization type.
- 2.4T-A95B: Qwen3.8-2.4T-A95B-GGUF
🦥 Run in Unsloth Desktop
Qwen3.8 can run in Unsloth Desktop, an open-source UI app for local AI. Unsloth automatically offloads to RAM and detects multiGPU setups. With Unsloth Desktop, you can run models locally on MacOS, Windows, Linux. Go to Unsloth Chat or Model hub and search for Qwen3.8 in the search bar and download your desired model and quant. Then run it.
Qwen3.8-2.4T-A95B New 1-bit data-types
We extended IQ1_S in llama.cpp which is 1.5625 bits per weight to 1.1875 bpw by reducing the number of entries in the codebook - we found this works well for large models, and can still retain a lot of accuracy - we also found these new data-types to be fine for post training quantization (PTQ) without the need for QAT or QAD (quantization aware training / distillation). Qwen3.8-2.4T-A95B-GGUF

For Apple Mac / Metal devices, set -DGGML_CUDA=OFF then continue as usual - Metal support is on by default.

First Strike
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
00:00:59 UTC
09:00:59 KST

First Strike
Internalize this graph, we post it on this blog a lot. If trends continue, China will have 10x the electricity of the US in 10 years. Whenever you think about geopolitical strategy, always keep it in mind, because whatever your plans are must consider this.
Here’s a strategy that would make sense for America long term. Sell as many AI chips into China as possible, use the money to purchase solar panel and battery factories, and build out electricity capacity as quickly as possible. Fundamentally, compute is a function of electricity. That’s the sort of long term thinking that gives America a chance. With robotic labor, immigrants like Jensen and Elon, a business friendly culture, and an incredible chunk of land, it’s not so clear who is ahead in 50 years. Regardless, I do know this strategy would lead to massive prosperity for the citizens of both countries.
However, that’s not close to what we see happening. We see a lot of short term zero sum thinking in action. It’s very scary what the US “AI race” mentality leads to. When exactly does the race end? Whatever capabilities you have today will eventually be matched, unless…
…you believe that you can use ASI in a decapitating first strike.
I’m worried that this is what these people see as the only way out.
It probably doesn’t work, can you outthink steel and drone production? I don’t see how, see my critiques here. But I’m not totally sure, I’m not a superintelligence. And if it does work, that may be the end.
My likelihood of imminent WWIII went up a lot with the nationalization of NVIDIA.

Amazon has a plan to index the world’s online shopping data
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
23:49:11 UTC
08:49:11 KST

Amazon has plans to build a vault of data on every product sold anywhere on the internet, even products Amazon doesn’t sell, and make the information available through its own site, according to Reuters. The project, called the “World Product Graph,” is meant to make it less likely that shoppers leave Amazon to do their product research on Reddit or Google, and make it more likely shoppers end up at Amazon even if they’re searching for a product sold elsewhere. The same goes for AI agents — if Amazon has the largest stockpile of shopping data, AI agents may rely on its index of products over others, so even people shopping with bots will end up on its pages.
Amazon has a plan to index the world’s online shopping data
Listing the details of everything for sale everywhere is meant to make Amazon more useful than Reddit for people and AI agents.
Links to outside retailers’ products have already started popping up on Amazon through the “shop direct” feature it launched last year. The company also has its own AI shopping assistant, Alexa for Shopping, previously called Rufus. The World Product Graph project would also be aimed at fixing issues with the AI by providing it more data. According to Reuters, an internal Amazon document on the project says “Many Rufus answers are factually wrong or incomplete” and running users’ Amazon searches with the AI is expensive. Adding more data is reportedly supposed to make the system more accurate and able to use cheaper models.
Whether this actually ships is still up in the air. The internal document reportedly says the project “depends on discovering how brands organize their products on their own websites,” which could involve Amazon crawling other retailers’ sites. Reuters says it’s unclear “if or when this plan may be implemented,” but said the document on it included an October 1st, 2027 release date.
Amazon said in a statement to Reuters that “Draft, outdated documents often paint an incomplete picture and the existence of a brainstorming document such as this one doesn’t necessarily reflect our current plans.”

====================================================================================================

What Happened to 3Com?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
23:48:59 UTC
08:48:59 KST

3Com was a high flying maker of network cards and other network equipment. And then they disappeared with a whimper on November 11, 2009. What happened to 3Com?
3Com at the dotcom boom
Around the time of the dotcom boom, 3Com was a 5.8 billion dollar company. They were founded in 1979 by Bob Metcalfe, the co-inventor of Ethernet. And for the next two decades, they were the go-to brand for network cards. While the 3C501 network card is notoriously bad, their other cards from the 1980s and 1990s are very well regarded, and always were.
They cost more, but had good driver support, the hardware worked reliably, and they were a known quantity. And 3Com made them in the United States, which was becoming unusual even in the 1990s.
I once worked in a shop that didn’t allow network equipment unless it was one of three brands. 3Com was one of them, along with Cisco and Intel. If you plugged a no name NE2000 clone card into the network and had problems, the IT department would replace the card and bill your department. There was a saying in the 1980s that no one got fired for buying IBM. When it came to networking, one could say the same for 3Com, during their prime.
And they were huge. In 1995, 3Com bought the naming rights to Candlestick Park, the legendary baseball/football stadium in San Francisco. It held those rights until 2002.
How 3Com’s long decline happened
But after the dotcom bust, 3Com faded away. What happened to 3Com, ultimately, was a series of bad decisions that led to a loss of profitability. Some of them may have made sense at the time. But not all of them.
The first thing that happened was 3Com walked away from its enterprise networking equipment business. Admittedly Cisco was hard to compete with. But 3Com was the only brand succeeding at doing it. The logic behind the move always puzzled analysts, because it didn’t save any money. Revenue dropped, and 3Com angered its customers and dealers. It’s likely many started buying Intel cards afterward, since they had to change vendors anyway.
Many corporations like to buy multiple things from a single supplier. This was one reason IBM stayed in the PC business for several years into the 21st century even though they were losing money on it. The computers weren’t profitable, but they made it easier to sell services, and the services were profitable. There is always the fear that two vendors will point fingers at each other, saying the network problem was caused by the hub and the network card being mismatched brands. Interoperability was the whole idea behind Ethernet, but not everyone understood that. One could commend 3Com for not wanting to exploit that misunderstanding. But it came at a financial cost.
It also didn’t help that the company branched into other businesses outside its core. The purchase of US Robotics was a key example. US Robotics made modems and Palm Pilots. For a short time, that was big business. But 3Com paid a premium price for products with a limited shelf life left in them.
Nothing good happened to 3Com after that. It took 3 years for Cisco to go from being 90% the size of 3Com to double the size of 3Com. The profitability of selling network adapters dropped. And by the time 3Com realized they needed to get back into network equipment, it was an expensive proposition. From 2000 to 2005, the company didn’t turn a profit.
Why 3Com failed
Ultimately, 3Com failed because it was too dependent on network cards. Network cards accounted for about half their revenue during their bust years. And their cards were expensive. Intel undercut their price to try to gain market share, and they succeeded.
And then they did exactly the wrong thing. It was the late ’90s, and they bought their way into the modem business. The dial-up modem business. At that point in time, everyone knew broadband was the future, they just didn’t know exactly when it was going to arrive. Yet 3Com had no plan to get into that business. It was like betting on AOL even though they were in the position to know the future belonged to AT&T.
Making matters worse, minimum viable product-style modems undercut US Robotics modems, just like the network card business. Not that Intel cards were a minimum viable product. But pretty much any ’90s network card not made by Intel or 3Com was.
3Com sought partnerships, most notably a partnership with Chinese networking giant Huawei. There were even rumors of Huawei buying 3com, though that deal never went through. And 3Com started making product overseas as a cost reducing measure. But ultimately, nothing could make up for all that lost time and all those bad decisions.
Who bought 3com
So what happened to 3Com? 3Com didn’t go out of business and they didn’t sell out to Huawei. They got Compaqed. Hewlett-Packard bought them out, which seems to be where every great ’90s technology company went to die. Hewlett-Packard paid 2.7 billion dollars in late 2009 for 3Com’s assets.
HP still sells network equipment like switches, routers, and access points. But they’re not as big of a name in networking as 3Com had been. And the 3Com pedigree isn’t the selling point. If they put a brand on it other than HP, it’s the newer Aruba brand, not 3Com. The selling point is the price being lower than Cisco. This makes them attractive to executives or purchasing agents looking to cut costs, consolidate vendors, or both. HP will usually beat Cisco’s price. And if the IT department is under pressure to reduce the number of vendors it buys from, HP sells more things than Cisco. So HP gets in the door for one of those reasons, and when their network equipment turns out to be no worse than Cisco equipment, they stay with it. At least until Dell comes around offering discounts.
But the network cards are long gone. When you buy an HP branded network card today, guess what it is? It’s an Intel card with HP’s logo engraved on it. Unless HP is trying to control costs, in which case it just has a sticker.
3Com today
Vintage 3Com network cards are very popular with retro computing enthusiasts. It had top-tier name recognition in the 80s and 90s. All but their very earliest cards generally have good driver support, work well, and are easier to configure than most of the cheaper competitors.
My favorite retro network card is a very specific Intel card, but if you need a card for anything from the 286 to the Pentium II era, you can do far worse than a 3com 3c509 ISA card or 3c905 PCI card. It’s absolutely period correct and absolutely something a knowledgeable professional would have used at the time. I have better luck with the ISA cards under DOS than the PCI cards, so if I want to run DOS and do any networking, I use a 3c509 ISA card in the system. But for Windows 95 and newer, the 3c905 PCI card works really well and is very cheap and common.
David Farquhar is a computer security professional, entrepreneur, and author. He has written professionally about computers since 1991, so he was writing about retro computers when they were still new. He has been working in IT professionally since 1994 and has specialized in vulnerability management since 2013. He holds Security+ and CISSP certifications. Today he blogs five times a week, mostly about retro computers and retro gaming covering the time period from 1975 to 2000.

Doc-foundry – AI writes Security detection rules, then proves they work
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
23:40:12 UTC
08:40:12 KST

Turn a threat (a CVE, a CISA KEV entry, an incident writeup, an ATT&CK technique) into a validated first-version detection — a proven starting point a detection team hardens further, not a claim of finished, forever-bulletproof coverage — plus three artifacts that ship together:
- a Sigma detection rule that has survived a Blue-vs-Red adversarial loop,
- a threat report with the round-by-round convergence history, and
- a proof bundle — a positive corpus the rule fires on, a benign baseline it
stays silent on, an adversarial mutation set, and a computed coverage label
(real-coverage / brittle / broken) with FP/FN numbers and a reproducibility hash.
The thing nobody else produces is #3. A model can write you a Sigma rule; it cannot hand you a proof that the rule works, an adversary that provably evades the weak version, and an honest record of how the rule was hardened until it held.
A bounded, fixed adversary. Red commits to two real, documented attacks in
round one and re-throws those same two against the hardened rule each round — the
same "built once, can't move its goalposts" discipline as the fixed test corpus.
real-coverage therefore means something concrete and honest: the first-version rule
fires on every attack in the corpus, stays silent on benign look-alikes, and caught
both of Red's real attacks. It is a validated baseline, not a promise of coverage
against every future evasion — that is the detection team's job to extend.
Agents propose; the engine proves. Four role-specialized LLM agents (Threat Analyst, Blue Detection Engineer, Telemetry, Red-Team Adversary) do the creative work. Everything that decides truth — FP/FN evaluation, adversarial evasion, the coverage verdict, red-team verification, and the deployment gate — is a deterministic referee. Same input → same verdict → same reproducibility hash.
Two pipelines share this agent cast. The one described above proves — Blue authors, Red attacks, a deterministic referee decides. The Review Chain reviews — draft → Red Team → revision → SOC Lead → final rule, with every note that shaped it. Different question, different deliverable; pick per what you need.
📄 See real output first:
examples/joomla-icagenda-cve-2026-48939/review-history.md
— a live run on a CISA KEV entry, three rule versions, eight reviewer findings.
pip install -r requirements.txt          # or just run --evals on stdlib alone

python src/run.py --evals                 # deterministic engine + proof + workflow tests
python src/run.py --approve alice         # human approves → gated deploy succeeds
python src/run.py --claude "..."          # force claude-opus-4-8 authoring (ANTHROPIC_API_KEY)
python src/run.py --gemini "..."          # force Gemini authoring (GEMINI_API_KEY)
There is no default scenario — the Foundry takes the threat you give it. A bare
python src/run.py has nothing to work on and says so.
Bundles are written to Outputs/<rule-slug>/ (rule.yml, report.md, proof.json).
The pipeline above asks "is this rule provably correct?" and answers with a deterministic verdict. The Review Chain asks a different question — "what would each stakeholder say about this rule, and what does it look like after their feedback is applied?" — and answers with a final rule plus the complete trail that shaped it.
ingest ─▶ extract ─▶ telemetry ─▶ profile
    ─▶ Blue: DRAFT v1
    ─▶ RED TEAM review        (findings: where is this evadable / blind)
    ─▶ Blue: REVISION v2      (each edit cites the finding id it answers)
    ─▶ SOC LEAD review        (findings: volume, triage, severity, tuning)
    ─▶ Blue: FINAL v3
    ─▶ package
python src/run.py --chain "CVE-2026-48939 Joomla iCagenda arbitrary file upload RCE"
python -m uvicorn webui.server:app --port 8000   # → http://127.0.0.1:8000  (chain UI)
                                                 # → /classic for the proof loop above
Red and SOC are not the same reviewer in two hats — they pull in opposite directions. Hardening against evasion broadens a rule; broadening raises alert volume. Running both, in that order, is the point: the final rule is the one that survived both pressures, and the trail shows exactly what each pass cost.
Output lands in Outputs/<rule-slug>/ as final-rule.yml, review-history.md
(the full progression, rendered) and review.json.
→ examples/joomla-icagenda-cve-2026-48939/
— a real run on CVE-2026-48939 (Joomla iCagenda upload-to-RCE, CISA KEV), authored by
claude-opus-4-8. Three rule versions, eight findings, the whole trail:
review-history.md.
Blue's first draft fired on a web daemon spawning a shell that runs recon commands. Both reviewers took it apart — in opposite directions:
Red Team: "No — I'd just skip the recon commands (or use a container's busybox shell) and this rule never fires despite a full RCE."
R1 mandatory recon_intent turns a lineage detection into a keyword detection ·
R2 on Alpine /bin/sh is busybox, so every shell clause misses ·
R3 literal contains dies to shell obfuscation ·
R4 setsid/double-fork re-parents to PID 1 and lineage fails
SOC Lead: "No — this fires on your own benign cron example on day one; the parent scoping is broken and it'll bury the rota in www-data noise."
S1 the OR made web-server parentage optional — any www-data process spawning a
shell fired ·
S2 php-fpm→php is normal web-app behaviour, not RCE ·
S3 the alert carried one field, so the analyst pivots consoles ·
S4 level: high on that noise floor burns out the on-call
Red pushed Blue to broaden; the SOC Lead pushed it to narrow straight back.
That tension is the product: hardening against evasion raises alert volume, and the
final rule is the one that survived both pressures. SOC reviews last, so what ships is
tuned for the queue — v3 made parent lineage mandatory, excluded the known-good
interpreter chains, expanded the triage fields, dropped to level: medium, and carries
a three-step response playbook with a containment threshold.
Reviewed, not validated — and the artifact says so. This chain does not invoke
the proof core. It never executes the rule against telemetry, counts no false
positives, and issues no coverage verdict. A rule that has been through four
professional reviewers looks finished, which is precisely why the packaged output
leads with that sentence. Run the classic pipeline (or --evals) when you need proof
rather than review.
python -m uvicorn webui.server:app --port 8000
# → http://127.0.0.1:8000 ; type ANY threat, watch the loop run
python tools/warm_cache.py "the exact threat you'll demo"   # optional — see below
FastAPI + SSE over the real graph. Two streams are interleaved and neither is
scripted: node events fire when a graph node genuinely completes, and token
events stream each agent's raw model output as it is generated, via the token sink
on providers.py. The graph runs on a worker thread precisely because tokens arrive
mid-node; the queue between them is the only coupling, and it is one-way.
You watch: the LangGraph shape animate (the node in flight glows, the edge into it flows, the loop-back arc fires red on a repair round), the agent console type out what the model is producing right now, the Sigma rule write itself line by line, and the convergence timeline, Kartikeya gate and reproducibility hash land at the end.
It authors LIVE — there are no presets and no fixture/replay path in the UI, so any
threat works. Determinism comes from providers.py's on-disk cache: temperature 0,
keyed by sha256(model|system|user), so re-running the same threat text replays
byte-identical model output → same rule → same bundle hash. Cold-run latency depends
entirely on the model (a 3-round convergence on gemini-flash-lite lands in ~20-40s;
a bigger model is minutes). warm_cache.py pre-pays it for a threat you plan to demo.
Replay is labeled, always. A cached token stream is marked cached end-to-end and
the console badge reads ⟳ REPLAY (cached) instead of ● GENERATING. A viewer
cannot tell replay from generation by looking, so the UI says which it is. Dressing
cached bytes up as live motion would be exactly the "vibes" this project refuses.
The sink is observation only: detach it and the verdict and hash are byte-identical.
Red-team refusals are handled honestly. Safety-tuned models sometimes decline the
adversary role. A refusal is recorded, surfaced in the UI, and — critically —
graph.node_verdict refuses to award real-coverage to a round nobody attacked.
"No evasions found" and "the adversary never ran" must never look alike.
  ingest ─▶ extract ─▶ telemetry            (Analyst normalizes; Telemetry fixes the test set)
                          │
                          ▼
   ┌──────────────── SELF-REPAIR LOOP (LangGraph conditional edges) ─────────────────┐
   │                                                                                  │
   │   blue_author ─▶ evaluate ─▶ battery ─▶ red_propose ─▶ red_verify ─▶ verdict     │
   │        ▲          (FP/FN)   (mutation   (Red-Team     (engine-      (coverage)    │
   │        │             │       battery)    proposes)     verifies)        │         │
   │        │             └── un-evaluable rule ─────────────────────────────┤         │
   │        │                  (engine refuses → broken, skip the round)     │         │
   │        └───────────────── repair: verdict ≠ real-coverage ◀────────────┘         │
   │                                                                        │         │
   └──────────────────────────── done: real-coverage OR round == cap ──────┘         │
                                                                            ▼
                                                                     gate ─▶ bundle
  [ blue/red/telemetry/analyst = LLM agents — PROPOSE ]
  [ evaluate·battery·red_verify·verdict·gate = deterministic referee — PROVE ]
- Agents (src/agents.py) — four distinct personas. The Blue Detection
Engineer authors a robust-by-construction first version (behavior + command-line
intent, not one brittle string) and re-authors on a brittle/broken verdict using the
concrete evidence (the exact evasions + false positives). The Red-Team Adversary
commits to two real, documented attacks in round one and re-throws those same
two against the hardened rule every round — a fixed adversarial test set, not a fresh
ambush each round, so Blue closes a bounded pair of gaps and honest convergence is
reachable.
- Research grounding (src/knowledge_base.py + rule_corpus/) — a two-partition
RAG knowledge base grounds both proposers in real, researcher-authored OSS:
Blue authors from peer-reviewed SigmaHQ rules; Red attacks from MITRE ATT&CK
defense-evasion (e.g. T1036.003 Rename System Utilities) and LOLBAS. The
retriever is lexical (BM25), fully offline and deterministic. It grounds proposals
only — it never touches the proof engine and never enters the reproducibility hash.
- src/engine.py — the deterministic Sigma matcher (the proof core). Zero deps.
Honesty contract: it raises on constructs it can't truly evaluate (|cidr,
aggregations, the |in that models like to invent) rather than silently passing —
an un-evaluable rule is never scored as "covered". The loop treats that as a
broken round and hands the engine's own message back to Blue as repair
evidence, so a rule that doesn't run costs a round, not the run.
- src/evaluator.py — FP/FN evaluation + a deterministic adversarial mutation
battery (binary-rename, casing, whitespace, caret/quote obfuscation, path
relocation), and merge_redteam() which folds engine-verified red-team
evasions into the same verdict. Brittleness is measured, not guessed.
- src/graph.py — the loop. red_verify replays every red-team event through
the engine: a bypass counts only if the engine confirms the rule failed to fire.
Unverified proposals are discarded — the adversary cannot assert a bypass.
- src/governance.py — Kartikeya's Gate = Blast Radius × Capability Delta.
Structural invariants a persuasive model can't argue away: egress always
requires a human, and non-real coverage floors blast radius.
- src/mcp_server.py — governed tool boundary. The graph can neither fetch nor
deploy; deploy_detection refuses any approval token not bound to the exact
bundle hash. The harness holds the privilege, not the agent.
python src/run.py --threat fixtures/certutil_t1105.json — offline, no key:
- Round 1 — Blue anchors on the Image path. The deterministic battery lands the
binary-rename bypass and the Red-Team lands a real-world variant (certutil
copied to AdobeUpdater.exe), engine-verified → verdict BRITTLE → loop back.
- Round 2 — Blue re-authors on rename-resistant OriginalFileName. The battery
evades nothing, and the same red-team rename is now engine-discarded because
the rule fires on it → verdict REAL-COVERAGE → Kartikeya's Gate → bundle.
The report shows the full arc (rule v1 brittle → evasion → rule v2 proven). That is the Foundry being honest about a rule that looks fine and isn't — then fixing it and proving the fix held.
This scenario is a fixture, not the demo: it exists so the workflow runs offline and the test suite has a deterministic end-to-end case. The UI never touches it. A live threat converges on its own merits or doesn't — and "doesn't" is a real result the Foundry is built to report rather than hide.
The Analyst's per-behavior log sources elect a single category for the run (majority
vote); the Telemetry agent builds the fixed corpus on it, and Blue is told both that
category and the exact field names the corpus carries. Blue sees no event values and
no labels, so it cannot overfit — it just cannot key on a field that isn't in the
telemetry, which is an automatic recall-0 broken round for a reason that has nothing
to do with detection quality. (A rule anchored on TargetFilename can never fire on
process-creation events.)
This is the Shiva/Kartikeya thesis applied to detection engineering:
deploying a detection is a capability request (egress to a SIEM), scored through
Gate = Blast Radius × Capability Delta before any human is bothered — and no
autonomous path can push a rule. Least privilege and human-gated egress are computed
and enforced in code, not asserted in a prompt.
Runs fully offline/deterministic. The sibling senior-security-engineering-agent narrates with Gemini; the Foundry authors with Claude — but in both, the model only proposes and the proof is deterministic.
The Foundry's proof core (engine.py → evaluator.py → governance.py) is deterministic and
self-contained. Its seams — ingest, author, convert, test-corpus, deploy, report — are designed
to be fed by open-source detection sources:
- docs/OSS-PARTNERS.md — canonical partner catalog: each repo mapped to the
tool's actual pipeline stage, with priority, integration point, and license posture.
- docs/OSS-INTEGRATION-MINDMAP.md — the full researched map.
First wire-in (highest value): Atomic Red Team → positive corpus + EVTX-ATTACK-SAMPLES → benign
baseline, so the adversarial battery runs against real attack telemetry. Then pySigma + SigmAIQ for
multi-SIEM authoring/conversion (the SigmaRuleArtifact.backends slot already exists for this).

Terminal borders that don't cost a cell
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
23:06:40 UTC
08:06:40 KST

Terminal borders that don't cost a cell
This post (and the code behind it) was written with a lot of help from AI. I'm not going to apologize for that. If that bothers you, feel free to close the tab.
I maintain Ratatui, so I see a lot of the TUIs people build. Many of them end up looking fairly same-ish: plain boxes on a boring background, missing a bunch of the things that good design in other mediums brings us.
Borders are part of the problem. They have to take up space, so they become part of the panels
people want to build. One option is to drop them and just use color, but borders are really nice
for delineating and surrounding things. There are lots of ways to sort of get borders in a
terminal, but none that are 100% good. I've drawn enough ┌──┐ by hand to have opinions about
this.
The usual way is the Unicode Box Drawing block, which has 128 characters for lines, corners and joins. Somehow that's still not enough:
So I've been working on a terminal protocol that draws thin lines on the edges of cells instead. I've got it working as a patch to Ghostty (and an earlier version in Rio before that), and I've built demo apps for it with Ratatui. I'd like feedback from terminal and TUI authors.
Every line in that screenshot is a cell border. None of them use up a row or column. The tab labels sit on the top edge of each pane, the text fields are underlined right under the text, and the buttons are one row tall and rounded.
Here's the same app in a terminal without cell borders, falling back to box-drawing characters:
Each pane loses a row and a column on every side, so the log pane shows two fewer lines and the notes wrap differently. The tab labels move inside the frame. The text fields use the underline attribute instead, and the checkbox and buttons turn into brackets.
What I'd use it for
- Borders, obviously. Panes and panels that don't eat a row and column on every side.
- Menus and popups that sit on top of other content and still look like they're on top.
- Panel style apps, where the layout is a set of regions that each want an edge.
- Widgets like text fields, buttons and checkboxes, which want a line right up against their text.
- Making things look neater in general. A border on the outer edge of a panel's background looks a lot cleaner, and text can run right up to it.
Up close
The settings form, with cell borders and then with the box-drawing fallback:
The tabs are the same story. With cell borders they sit on the pane's top edge. With box characters they need their own row inside the frame:
Charts
Lines between cells also work for charts. btop-style braille graphs fill every cell with dots, so there's nowhere to put a gridline or an average line without giving up a row. With cell borders they sit on the cell edges between the dots. Here's a monitor in that style, built with Ratatui:
And the fallback, which has to drop the gridlines:
In motion
Here's the tiling panes demo moving panes around, opening menus and the about dialog:
Partway through, it turns on a checkerboard of the cells, which shows how everything lines up with the grid. Toggle it here:
What box characters can't do
The root of it is that box-drawing characters are text. Each line is a glyph that takes up a whole cell, and pretty much everything annoying about them falls out of that.
Space is the obvious one. On my 16" laptop with a font I can actually read, the terminal is something like 200x50 (your prefs will move that around a bit). Columns are mostly fine at that size. Rows aren't. A layout with a couple of levels of nested panels can easily spend 6 or 8 of those 50 rows on nothing but lines. That's a lot of screen to give up for decoration.
You also can't put a line between two cells that both have something in them. So there's no way to underline a text field right under its text, or to outline a button, without adding padding around it. Rounded corners sort of work, except the corner glyph has one background color, and when the panel's background is different from what's around it you get a little square of the wrong color in each corner. And the line itself is whatever the font decides it is, weight and position included.
Then there's copy and paste. Select some text inside a box and you get the box too. Everyone who's
pasted from a TUI into Slack knows the little │ cleanup dance. Sometimes you do want the box
(pasting a table into a chat, say), but mostly it's junk. Cell borders aren't text, so they never
get copied, which cuts both ways.
How it works
A cell border is a cell attribute, like a background color. Each cell can carry up to twelve line segments: the two halves of each of its four edges, and four arms from its center. Each segment has its own color, width, placement and style, and the terminal draws them over the cell grid. Borders are stored in the cells, so they scroll, erase, insert and reflow with the text. What happens on erase or scroll already has an answer, the same one as for colors.
The edges are split into halves so borders line up with quadrant block characters (▖▗▘▝ and
friends). That makes it easy to draw a shape that's a half cell in from the grid and still put a
neat border around it. Yes, that still uses a character for the fill, and there might be a better
way to do that part. One problem at a time.
The same idea could stretch to sextants and octants, or to quarter and eighth widths, if designs need them.
Styles and joints
Lines can be solid, double, dashed or dotted. Each corner and center of a cell can be square or rounded, with a configurable radius and anti-aliased arcs. A rounded corner can name an outside color, so the area outside the arc is painted correctly when the panel's background differs from what's behind it.
When two neighboring cells both put a line on their shared edge, they merge into one line. The
stroke with the higher priority wins, then the wider one, then the one to the right or below.
That way a focused pane can raise its priority and keep its whole outline, whatever order its
neighbors were drawn in. My first try was "right/bottom always wins", which lasted until the
focused pane lost half its outline to its neighbor.
The wire format
Everything is one DCS string with a namespace:
ESC P 1 + b cell-border ; OP ; FIELDS... ESC \
The common path is a pen, held in the cursor like the current background color. Set it, print some text, and the printed cells get the borders:
ESC P 1 + b cell-border;pen;48;32;7aa2f7;0;0 ESC \     bottom edge, blue: an underline
hello
ESC [ 0 m                                              SGR 0 clears the pen
There are also rectangle commands that set or clear borders on existing cells without touching
the pen, and a capability query. An app sends the query followed by DA1. If the DA1 reply arrives
without a cell-border reply first, the terminal doesn't support it, so the app falls back to box
characters and reserves the space they need. Terminals that don't understand the DCS ignore it,
the same as any unknown DCS.
The minimum a terminal needs is the pen, the rectangle commands and solid strokes. I've tried to keep that first cut small, because there's always one more "... and this" to add, and that's a slippery slope.
Line styles, rounded joints, priority and outside colors are extension fields listed in the capability reply, and unknown fields are ignored, so they can be added later. I'd rather the early decisions be two way doors where possible.
The spec calls itself version 1 (it's the 1 in DCS 1 + b). That's v1 in the old sense, before
we all got bogged down in semver. It doesn't mean done or stable, it means it's the first one I was willing to write
down. Read it as v0.1, or a draft, or whatever you prefer.
Prior art
Parts of this have been done before:
- DEC ruled lines in DECterm (extension 43 in the DA1 reply) draw lines around rectangles. They scroll with the text, but they're a separate layer: clearing the screen leaves them behind, and they're always one pixel wide in the normal text color.
- SGR 51 to 65: framed, encircled, overline and the ideogram side lines. Some terminals draw borders with them, but they're on/off attributes with no color, width or joins, and there's no attribute for the bottom edge.
- The Win32 console has per-cell grid flags for the top, left and right edges. PDCurses
and X/Open curses (WA_TOP, WA_LEFT and friends) have similar attributes. These are the
closest to this protocol, but they have no bottom edge (Win32), color, width or halves.
- IBM 3270 terminals can outline whole input fields.
What none of these give you is a line with its own color and width on each edge. The rationale document covers the prior art in more detail and explains each choice in the protocol.
My own first prototype was in Rio (raphamorim/rio#1947), with an earlier version of the spec that used APC instead of DCS. Feedback pointed out that APC isn't safe to reuse: Kermit 95 has run APC payloads as Kermit commands since the 90s. DCS is where structured terminal commands already live, and terminals ignore DCS strings they don't recognize, so I switched to that.
The awkward (edge) cases
When I first showed this around, most of the questions were about these. The spec goes into all of them properly, but here's roughly where I landed.
Multiplexers were the first thing people asked about. tmux and friends redraw panes from their own idea of the cell grid, so passing these sequences through would just put borders in the wrong place. A multiplexer that doesn't know about the protocol should drop them and answer DA1 itself, and then apps running inside it fall back to box characters like they would anywhere else. One that does know about it can store borders like any other attribute and redraw them.
Conflicting lines on a shared edge are settled when the terminal renders, using the priority rule above, so it doesn't matter what order the app wrote the cells in.
Erase, insert and delete all work the way they do for background colors. Erased cells pick up the current pen, and lines that get inserted or deleted take their borders along. Scrollback, the alternate screen and reflow mostly come for free, because the border lives in the cell.
On cost: the Ghostty change is about 1,650 lines, and a fair chunk of that is tests. Each cell gets a 16-bit border ID, which fits in padding that was already there, so cells don't get any bigger and a screen with no borders costs nothing extra. The IDs point into a table of distinct border sets (about 100 bytes each) that's shared by the whole terminal. A typical TUI only uses a handful of distinct sets, but the table is never freed, so in the worst case (all 65,535 IDs in use) it's somewhere around 10-15 MB.
Rendering only does extra work for cells that have borders. Each one looks at its four neighbors to resolve shared edges, so changing a row also rebuilds the rows either side of it. Straight lines are a few solid quads per cell. Rounded corners cost more, because the anti-aliased arc is drawn as rows of small quads, roughly one per edge pixel. I haven't benchmarked any of this properly yet, and it's the part I'd most like another set of eyes on.
Try it
The spec and rationale are on my Ghostty fork. The Ghostty branch implements all of version 1. I've only built and run it on macOS so far, so the GTK and OpenGL builds are untested. To build and run it (you'll need Zig 0.17 and Xcode):
git clone -b joshka/cell-borders https://github.com/joshka/ghostty.git
cd ghostty
zig build -Doptimize=ReleaseFast -Dxcframework-target=native
open zig-out/Ghostty.app
Inside that Ghostty, the gallery script on the same branch draws the style gallery above. It detects support first, so it's also a quick check for another terminal's implementation:
python3 scripts/cell-borders.py            # the default view
python3 scripts/cell-borders.py --styles   # line styles, joints and outside fill
Once it's running you can flip between views with keys: p placement, c colors, h blocks, j
junctions, e halves, s styles and l live. w cycles the stroke width, b turns borders off
so you can compare, and q quits. Each view also has a matching flag (--placement, --styles
and so on) if you want to start on it.
The tiling panes demo is on the Ratatui branch. It falls back to box-drawing characters when the terminal doesn't support cell borders, which is how I made the comparison shots above:
git clone -b joshka/cell-borders https://github.com/joshka/ratatui.git
cd ratatui
cargo run -p tiling-panes
Drag panes around with the mouse to retile them. n and v split, x closes, and right click
opens a pane menu. Press b to switch between cell borders and box drawing, or g to shade
alternate cells and see where the edges are.
The btop-style monitor from the chart screenshots is in the same branch. Given how much of it an AI wrote, I did briefly consider calling it bslop:
To run it:
cargo run -p cell-border-top
It's a real system monitor (it reads your actual CPU, memory, network and processes), so it's a
better test of how this looks on a busy screen than the panes demo. 1 to 4 show and hide the
boxes, b and g work the same as in the panes demo, and h or ? lists the rest of the keys.
It needs a newer Rust (1.95) than the rest of Ratatui because of the sysinfo dependency.
What I'd like to hear
I'm not attached to this being the exact solution, but I think it's a problem worth solving, and I'd like it to be more than one terminal's feature (and more than one weekend's worth of yak shaving). If you work on a terminal, would you implement this, and which parts look expensive? If you work on a TUI library or app, which of your widgets would use it, and what would they need that isn't here? Comments are welcome on the Ghostty discussion.

Terminal borders that don't cost a cell
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
23:06:40 UTC
08:06:40 KST

Terminal borders that don't cost a cell
This post (and the code behind it) was written with a lot of help from AI. I'm not going to apologize for that. If that bothers you, feel free to close the tab.
I maintain Ratatui, so I see a lot of the TUIs people build. Many of them end up looking fairly same-ish: plain boxes on a boring background, missing a bunch of the things that good design in other mediums brings us.
Borders are part of the problem. They have to take up space, so they become part of the panels
people want to build. One option is to drop them and just use color, but borders are really nice
for delineating and surrounding things. There are lots of ways to sort of get borders in a
terminal, but none that are 100% good. I've drawn enough ┌──┐ by hand to have opinions about
this.
The usual way is the Unicode Box Drawing block, which has 128 characters for lines, corners and joins. Somehow that's still not enough:
So I've been working on a terminal protocol that draws thin lines on the edges of cells instead. I've got it working as a patch to Ghostty (and an earlier version in Rio before that), and I've built demo apps for it with Ratatui. I'd like feedback from terminal and TUI authors.
Every line in that screenshot is a cell border. None of them use up a row or column. The tab labels sit on the top edge of each pane, the text fields are underlined right under the text, and the buttons are one row tall and rounded.
Here's the same app in a terminal without cell borders, falling back to box-drawing characters:
Each pane loses a row and a column on every side, so the log pane shows two fewer lines and the notes wrap differently. The tab labels move inside the frame. The text fields use the underline attribute instead, and the checkbox and buttons turn into brackets.
What I'd use it for
- Borders, obviously. Panes and panels that don't eat a row and column on every side.
- Menus and popups that sit on top of other content and still look like they're on top.
- Panel style apps, where the layout is a set of regions that each want an edge.
- Widgets like text fields, buttons and checkboxes, which want a line right up against their text.
- Making things look neater in general. A border on the outer edge of a panel's background looks a lot cleaner, and text can run right up to it.
Up close
The settings form, with cell borders and then with the box-drawing fallback:
The tabs are the same story. With cell borders they sit on the pane's top edge. With box characters they need their own row inside the frame:
Charts
Lines between cells also work for charts. btop-style braille graphs fill every cell with dots, so there's nowhere to put a gridline or an average line without giving up a row. With cell borders they sit on the cell edges between the dots. Here's a monitor in that style, built with Ratatui:
And the fallback, which has to drop the gridlines:
In motion
Here's the tiling panes demo moving panes around, opening menus and the about dialog:
Partway through, it turns on a checkerboard of the cells, which shows how everything lines up with the grid. Toggle it here:
What box characters can't do
The root of it is that box-drawing characters are text. Each line is a glyph that takes up a whole cell, and pretty much everything annoying about them falls out of that.
Space is the obvious one. On my 16" laptop with a font I can actually read, the terminal is something like 200x50 (your prefs will move that around a bit). Columns are mostly fine at that size. Rows aren't. A layout with a couple of levels of nested panels can easily spend 6 or 8 of those 50 rows on nothing but lines. That's a lot of screen to give up for decoration.
You also can't put a line between two cells that both have something in them. So there's no way to underline a text field right under its text, or to outline a button, without adding padding around it. Rounded corners sort of work, except the corner glyph has one background color, and when the panel's background is different from what's around it you get a little square of the wrong color in each corner. And the line itself is whatever the font decides it is, weight and position included.
Then there's copy and paste. Select some text inside a box and you get the box too. Everyone who's
pasted from a TUI into Slack knows the little │ cleanup dance. Sometimes you do want the box
(pasting a table into a chat, say), but mostly it's junk. Cell borders aren't text, so they never
get copied, which cuts both ways.
How it works
A cell border is a cell attribute, like a background color. Each cell can carry up to twelve line segments: the two halves of each of its four edges, and four arms from its center. Each segment has its own color, width, placement and style, and the terminal draws them over the cell grid. Borders are stored in the cells, so they scroll, erase, insert and reflow with the text. What happens on erase or scroll already has an answer, the same one as for colors.
The edges are split into halves so borders line up with quadrant block characters (▖▗▘▝ and
friends). That makes it easy to draw a shape that's a half cell in from the grid and still put a
neat border around it. Yes, that still uses a character for the fill, and there might be a better
way to do that part. One problem at a time.
The same idea could stretch to sextants and octants, or to quarter and eighth widths, if designs need them.
Styles and joints
Lines can be solid, double, dashed or dotted. Each corner and center of a cell can be square or rounded, with a configurable radius and anti-aliased arcs. A rounded corner can name an outside color, so the area outside the arc is painted correctly when the panel's background differs from what's behind it.
When two neighboring cells both put a line on their shared edge, they merge into one line. The
stroke with the higher priority wins, then the wider one, then the one to the right or below.
That way a focused pane can raise its priority and keep its whole outline, whatever order its
neighbors were drawn in. My first try was "right/bottom always wins", which lasted until the
focused pane lost half its outline to its neighbor.
The wire format
Everything is one DCS string with a namespace:
ESC P 1 + b cell-border ; OP ; FIELDS... ESC \
The common path is a pen, held in the cursor like the current background color. Set it, print some text, and the printed cells get the borders:
ESC P 1 + b cell-border;pen;48;32;7aa2f7;0;0 ESC \     bottom edge, blue: an underline
hello
ESC [ 0 m                                              SGR 0 clears the pen
There are also rectangle commands that set or clear borders on existing cells without touching
the pen, and a capability query. An app sends the query followed by DA1. If the DA1 reply arrives
without a cell-border reply first, the terminal doesn't support it, so the app falls back to box
characters and reserves the space they need. Terminals that don't understand the DCS ignore it,
the same as any unknown DCS.
The minimum a terminal needs is the pen, the rectangle commands and solid strokes. I've tried to keep that first cut small, because there's always one more "... and this" to add, and that's a slippery slope.
Line styles, rounded joints, priority and outside colors are extension fields listed in the capability reply, and unknown fields are ignored, so they can be added later. I'd rather the early decisions be two way doors where possible.
The spec calls itself version 1 (it's the 1 in DCS 1 + b). That's v1 in the old sense, before
we all got bogged down in semver. It doesn't mean done or stable, it means it's the first one I was willing to write
down. Read it as v0.1, or a draft, or whatever you prefer.
Prior art
Parts of this have been done before:
- DEC ruled lines in DECterm (extension 43 in the DA1 reply) draw lines around rectangles. They scroll with the text, but they're a separate layer: clearing the screen leaves them behind, and they're always one pixel wide in the normal text color.
- SGR 51 to 65: framed, encircled, overline and the ideogram side lines. Some terminals draw borders with them, but they're on/off attributes with no color, width or joins, and there's no attribute for the bottom edge.
- The Win32 console has per-cell grid flags for the top, left and right edges. PDCurses
and X/Open curses (WA_TOP, WA_LEFT and friends) have similar attributes. These are the
closest to this protocol, but they have no bottom edge (Win32), color, width or halves.
- IBM 3270 terminals can outline whole input fields.
What none of these give you is a line with its own color and width on each edge. The rationale document covers the prior art in more detail and explains each choice in the protocol.
My own first prototype was in Rio (raphamorim/rio#1947), with an earlier version of the spec that used APC instead of DCS. Feedback pointed out that APC isn't safe to reuse: Kermit 95 has run APC payloads as Kermit commands since the 90s. DCS is where structured terminal commands already live, and terminals ignore DCS strings they don't recognize, so I switched to that.
The awkward (edge) cases
When I first showed this around, most of the questions were about these. The spec goes into all of them properly, but here's roughly where I landed.
Multiplexers were the first thing people asked about. tmux and friends redraw panes from their own idea of the cell grid, so passing these sequences through would just put borders in the wrong place. A multiplexer that doesn't know about the protocol should drop them and answer DA1 itself, and then apps running inside it fall back to box characters like they would anywhere else. One that does know about it can store borders like any other attribute and redraw them.
Conflicting lines on a shared edge are settled when the terminal renders, using the priority rule above, so it doesn't matter what order the app wrote the cells in.
Erase, insert and delete all work the way they do for background colors. Erased cells pick up the current pen, and lines that get inserted or deleted take their borders along. Scrollback, the alternate screen and reflow mostly come for free, because the border lives in the cell.
On cost: the Ghostty change is about 1,650 lines, and a fair chunk of that is tests. Each cell gets a 16-bit border ID, which fits in padding that was already there, so cells don't get any bigger and a screen with no borders costs nothing extra. The IDs point into a table of distinct border sets (about 100 bytes each) that's shared by the whole terminal. A typical TUI only uses a handful of distinct sets, but the table is never freed, so in the worst case (all 65,535 IDs in use) it's somewhere around 10-15 MB.
Rendering only does extra work for cells that have borders. Each one looks at its four neighbors to resolve shared edges, so changing a row also rebuilds the rows either side of it. Straight lines are a few solid quads per cell. Rounded corners cost more, because the anti-aliased arc is drawn as rows of small quads, roughly one per edge pixel. I haven't benchmarked any of this properly yet, and it's the part I'd most like another set of eyes on.
Try it
The spec and rationale are on my Ghostty fork. The Ghostty branch implements all of version 1. I've only built and run it on macOS so far, so the GTK and OpenGL builds are untested. To build and run it (you'll need Zig 0.17 and Xcode):
git clone -b joshka/cell-borders https://github.com/joshka/ghostty.git
cd ghostty
zig build -Doptimize=ReleaseFast -Dxcframework-target=native
open zig-out/Ghostty.app
Inside that Ghostty, the gallery script on the same branch draws the style gallery above. It detects support first, so it's also a quick check for another terminal's implementation:
python3 scripts/cell-borders.py            # the default view
python3 scripts/cell-borders.py --styles   # line styles, joints and outside fill
Once it's running you can flip between views with keys: p placement, c colors, h blocks, j
junctions, e halves, s styles and l live. w cycles the stroke width, b turns borders off
so you can compare, and q quits. Each view also has a matching flag (--placement, --styles
and so on) if you want to start on it.
The tiling panes demo is on the Ratatui branch. It falls back to box-drawing characters when the terminal doesn't support cell borders, which is how I made the comparison shots above:
git clone -b joshka/cell-borders https://github.com/joshka/ratatui.git
cd ratatui
cargo run -p tiling-panes
Drag panes around with the mouse to retile them. n and v split, x closes, and right click
opens a pane menu. Press b to switch between cell borders and box drawing, or g to shade
alternate cells and see where the edges are.
The btop-style monitor from the chart screenshots is in the same branch. Given how much of it an AI wrote, I did briefly consider calling it bslop:
To run it:
cargo run -p cell-border-top
It's a real system monitor (it reads your actual CPU, memory, network and processes), so it's a
better test of how this looks on a busy screen than the panes demo. 1 to 4 show and hide the
boxes, b and g work the same as in the panes demo, and h or ? lists the rest of the keys.
It needs a newer Rust (1.95) than the rest of Ratatui because of the sysinfo dependency.
What I'd like to hear
I'm not attached to this being the exact solution, but I think it's a problem worth solving, and I'd like it to be more than one terminal's feature (and more than one weekend's worth of yak shaving). If you work on a terminal, would you implement this, and which parts look expensive? If you work on a TUI library or app, which of your widgets would use it, and what would they need that isn't here? Comments are welcome on the Ghostty discussion.

How Not to Build a Music Player
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
23:04:30 UTC
08:04:30 KST

The Twelve-Day War made me realize online music players aren’t a viable option for those who live under an oppressive regime that disconnects the internet across the country as a way to silence public opinion.
So, as soon as the connection came back, I started archiving tracks and looking for offline alternatives and discovered Strawberry. It’s a feature-rich music player and kept me happy for quite a while, but I have this habit of looking at song lyrics and singing along! Strawberry fetches lyrics from the internet when it recognizes a song, but sometimes the lyrics weren’t line-synced and worse, it displayed the timestamps next to the lyrics which I found pretty annoying to look at. I wasn’t aware of other music players such as foobar2000 which my friend just pointed me to while writing this post.
I decided to write my own music player and call it AMP with a few goals in mind: minimal feature-set, no C++ dependencies, proper system integration to make it feel like a native app, and of course, write it in Zig (including the shaders!). I also wanted to roll my own UI because of the fun memories of working on V’s UI during my first days of being introduced into system programming. But before I could make any meaningful progress, a new wave of protests started. Following the tragedy of January 8 and 9, another internet blackout began, lasting more than four months.
During the blackout, there were these anonymous people on Telegram selling internet access through personal White SIMs, whitelisted servers, Starlink dishes, etc. A black market had formed for the freaking internet! You’d get charged $5 or more for just 1GB of traffic. The prices dropped slightly after a while, but it was still hella expensive. The regime surely made a lot of money out of it. I ended up spending hundreds of dollars on these services and other methods just to stay in touch with my friends and keep my hobbies alive. Killing time was becoming a problem. Especially for someone like me who built their entire social life online.
I carefully used this slow connection to download repositories, documention, etc. to continue working on my music player. It really brought me immense joy during this hard time.
Once the connection was mostly restored to the pre-blackout days (UDP traffic is still blocked, though, which naturally also affects QUIC), my attention was shifted towards LLMs. It had become very hard for me to ignore the hype and progress around frontier LLMs. As I really never thought about using them seriously for coding. They are still everywhere in the news, and I was just reminded that I owned a Claude Max 20x subscription gift from my boss back in 2025. Eating dust and maybe soon expiring. So I decided to give it a try and experiment on my music player. It was a very addictive experience. I literally couldn’t hit the plan limits no matter how hard I tried, so after a few non-stop days of prompting, a good chunk of AMP was vibecoded..
At the time, I thought this was an okay approach for a hobby project and I could just un-slopify it later. Little did I know I’m mentally incapable of reviewing tens of thousands lines of code. Shortly after, Anthropic figured that I live in Iran and instantly banned my account. Then Codeberg announced its no-AI policy, which gave me two choices. Either get rid of all that slop or move the repository elsewhere. Going with the former option, I started reading every single line of code, rewriting them. I was surprised by my own ignorance and how much LLMs like to complicate stuff. (I wish I could show an example, but my memory is extremely fragile and I’ve got no pre-rewrite history of the repo to check).
Four months later, nearly half the codebase is gone, and somehow it’s more robust with the same set of features (and more). I regret relying on LLMs in the first place, but hey, lesson learned. That said I think it’s still possible to create a healthy relationship with LLMs as long as you look at them as a fancy IDE feature. A lot of times they are just running long sed or regex commands that are a no-brainer act for humans when done by hand but still consumes a good time. For now and hopefully forever I think I’ll be going the old school way. Never rush your hobbies!
Btw this is how AMP looks like right now!
Worth mentioning that the blur shader is written in Zig based on this excellent blog post!.
Roadmap to v1.0:
- Music server
- Android and Windows support
- Packaging for major Linux distros
- Any suggestions?

Lego's BrickLink Marketplace Back Online After Week-Long Outage Caused By 'Cyber Incident'
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
23:00:00 UTC
08:00:00 KST

Lego's BrickLink Marketplace Back Online After Week-Long Outage Caused By 'Cyber Incident' (brickfanatics.com) 4
			
		 	
				LEGO's BrickLink marketplace is back online after a week-long outage caused by what the company described as a "cyber incident," during which an unauthorized third party accessed some user data. Details about exactly what data was accessed remain unclear. The site went offline on November 5 and remained unavailable until November 12, preventing users from buying or selling LEGO sets and parts during the busy holiday shopping season. According to Brick Fanatics, LEGO initially described the outage as "necessary work" before confirming that the platform had suffered a security incident.
In an email to users, LEGO acknowledged that "some of the information associated with your account was accessed and disclosed by an unauthorized party," including potentially usernames and IP addresses. However, the company insisted that "your password, postal address, and financial information were not disclosed." Those assurances have since come under scrutiny following reports of convincing phishing emails containing detailed personal and purchase information.
The outage also dealt a significant blow to independent sellers who rely on BrickLink for income. Simon Webster, who operates stores on both BrickLink and rival marketplace Brick Owl, reported that his overall sales revenue fell 52% over a four-day period, even as sales through Brick Owl rose 79%. "Their comms are amateur at the best of times, but surely they should be able to send an email to let sellers and buyers alike know what is happening and what they're doing to rectify it," Webster said. "But no, we're all left in the dark."
The incident has renewed concerns about BrickLink's aging infrastructure. Founded in 2000 and acquired by LEGO in 2019, the marketplace reportedly runs on a complex collection of interconnected systems that makes modernization difficult. The Brick Fanatics report questions whether the 25-year-old platform remains fit for purpose and whether prolonged outages and security concerns could push sellers toward competing marketplaces like Brick Owl.

Lying to Zed to get its remote server running on FreeBSD
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
22:54:47 UTC
07:54:47 KST

I’ve used VS Code since I was about 16, so for almost ten years and honestly it was mostly fine. But as of late it has become unbearable: slow, and increasingly weighed down by AI slop. grahamperrin@ pointed out to me that VS Code now has chat.disableAIFeatures, and they’re right. I totally missed it! It really does turn the built-in AI features off atleast from what I can see in limited testing, and it works. But even with it off, VS Code still feels clunky as hell. Zed, on the other hand, is fast, and it had a native switch that turns all of its AI stuff off slightly earlier than VS Code did, which I absolutely adore. (It’s a toggle in Settings, or "disable_ai": true in your settings file.)
That’s not because I avoid LLMs. I just don’t want them trying to edit every line I write, or sitting in the corner judging me ominously. I still write most of my FOSS code myself: it keeps me sane, and I feel FOSS developers have a responsibility to keep code clean for LLMs to steal from lol. What I do hand off is the tiring part: regression testing, edge cases, and exploration beyond what my tiny 20-watt brain can hold. For that I use harnesses that work well with models, like OpenCode. Beyond auto-complete.. I’ve always found entire harnesses like claude code etc. very clunky in editors.
Getting it working
Zed has no FreeBSD remote server offering (yet), but FreeBSD can run Linux binaries in spades, so we use Zed’s Linux one. All you need is a FreeBSD server you can SSH into.
1. Turn on Linux compatibility
sudo sysrc linux_enable=YES
sudo service linux start
sudo pkg install linux_base-rl9
kldstat -m linux64    # should print a module
2. Make Zed pick the Linux server
Zed asks the host what platform it runs. FreeBSD says FreeBSD amd64, there’s no server for that, and Zed gives up. This wrapper says Linux x86_64 to that one question and passes everything else to the real uname. Save it as ~/.local/bin/uname and make it executable:
#!/bin/sh
case "$#:$*" in
  1:-sm|1:-ms) printf 'Linux x86_64\n' ;;
  *) exec /usr/bin/uname "$@" ;;
esac
Put ~/.local/bin on PATH for non-interactive SSH sessions. For zsh, add this to ~/.zshenv (use your shell’s equivalent):
export PATH="$HOME/.local/bin:$PATH"
It’s a small lie, but a contained one, and the part I’d love to retire.
3. Connect.
Connect over SSH from Zed and open a folder. The first connection uploads the Linux server to ~/.zed_server/. To watch, run tail -f ~/.local/share/zed/logs/server-workspace-*.log.
4. Run a kernel with the (FreeBSD) fix
Without it you’ll connect fine and get an empty project tree, with this in the server log:
error processing entry failed to read directory entry
Caused by:
    Invalid argument (os error 22)
The fix is ec6629628cb, in main, stable/15 and releng/15.2, so 15.2-RELEASE will include it. If the error persists, check you booted the new kernel with uname -v.
The bug in layman terms
To show your project, Zed asks the server for the names of the files in each folder, a few at a time. Zed sets aside a small amount of space for each batch.
Picture a teacher reading a class roll to a student who writes the names on a sticky note (I couldn’t come up with a better analogy, bare with me). The teacher crosses off each name as she reads it, and she never reads a crossed-off name again. The student’s note has room for 6 names. The teacher reads in groups of 4, a sensible habit that works fine almost every time. But 6 isn’t a whole number of groups, so she reads 8 names and crosses all 8 off. Only 6 fit on the note.
Now she’s in a bind. Her slip was grabbing more than fit before checking. She can’t hand over the 6 that fit and keep the other 2 for later, because they’re already crossed off and she would never read them again. So she reports an error instead, which at least is loud. When I tried the quiet way, 112 of my 1,002 files vanished (from the listing) with no warning. A loud error beats a quiet loss I’m pretty sure. Zed saw the error and decided it couldn’t read the folder, and showed it up empty.
The fix is small: read no more names than fit on the note. Read 6, cross off 6, come back for the next 6, until the roll is done. In the real bug, the teacher is FreeBSD’s Linux translator (Linuxulator), the note is a 768-byte buffer, and a group of 4 names is a 512-byte block.
For the kernel nerds
This is a regression. A February 2026 fix for NFS directories returning bogus entries (110f2567cb51, PR 292282) changed how sys/compat/linux/linux_file.c sizes its native read, in both linux_getdents() and linux_getdents64():
/* before February 2026 */
buflen = min(args->count, MAXBSIZE);
/* after: every request rounded up to a 512-byte block */
buflen = min(roundup2(args->count, DEV_BSIZE), MAXBSIZE);
If the caller’s buffer isn’t a multiple of 512 bytes, the native batch can outgrow it. The emulation layer converts records until one doesn’t fit and returns EINVAL, but kern_getdirentries() has already advanced the directory offset past the whole batch. That’s why returning a partial batch isn’t enough on its own.
The fix (ec6629628cb, reviewed in D60760):
if (args->count >= DEV_BSIZE)
	buflen = min(args->count, MAXBSIZE);
else
	buflen = roundup2(args->count, DEV_BSIZE);
It’s safe because Linux directory records are never larger than FreeBSD’s: for dirent64 the header is 19 bytes against 24, and both round each record up to a multiple of 8. A native batch that fits in the caller’s buffer translates into one that fits too.
Legacy getdents had the same bug for 32-bit and 64-bit callers. My first patch only fixed getdents64; fixing both took the test matrix from 86 failures to zero.
Acknowledgements
I’ve had plenty of patches merged before this one, but this was among the quickest to land in releng/15.2: it didn’t sit in stable for long (thankfully!). The release engineering team (re@) runs a really cool process that I am yet to fully understand in depth. Thank you also to Kyle Evans (kevans@) and Mark Johnston (markj@) for reviewing the patch and for their notes along the way.
